 TOMINA IN RETREAT

CHAPTER I

A DOCTOR'S PRESCRIPTION

"YOU want to bury me," Tomina said in a slow sweet-toned voice, "but if
you do, are you certain of my resurrection?"

"I want to bring you back to a normal woman's life. The pace you have
been going would kill a strong man. You have no right to complain of a
worn-out body, of shattered nerves, of sleepless nights, for you have
reaped that which you have sown."

Doctor and patient faced each other in the handsome consulting room in
Harley Street.

Tomina sat back in her chair, looking very fragile and white. She was
a slim young creature, clad in irreproachable London garments. Her
bright brown eyes seemed the most vivid part of her; they were now
twinkling with the irrepressible humour that always bubbled up in her
at the most inopportune moments. She had a small oval high-bred face,
with clear-cut features and a most determined mouth and chin, but she
was too thin to be beautiful. She looked as if a breath of wind would
blow her away; and yet when she opened her mouth to speak, she gave
every one the instant impression of a hidden force and vitality under a
somewhat placid and indifferent surface.

"I don't think I am such a wreck," she said slowly. "I only want to
sleep, and sleep won't come. Now, Dr. Carisbrooke, if I follow your
prescription, where can I bury myself? You don't advise an ordinary
rest cure. I would not go to one if you did; I am dead tired of the
Riviera. I have been to India, Cairo, and Spain; I would not find
Continental cities a rest."

"Why do you butterflies of fashion think you must leave your own island
to have a quiet rest?"

"Because we want to get away from our friends and acquaintances,
and—perhaps from our doctors."

She flashed a mischievous glance at him.

The old doctor who had helped her into the world, and who had at
different intervals prescribed for her and watched over her for many
years, looked at her with grave compassionate eyes.

Tomina went on talking.

"Motors find out every quiet corner in England. I have tried week-ends
in country cottages—you know I have. The mice, the owls, the early
morning birds, especially the fiendish cock, are worse than dear
London's hoots and toots and rumbles."

"I know a few corners unfrequented by motors. If you will really take
my advice, I will find—"

"The necessary grave? Thank you. Well, how long must I stay in it?"

"Six months at least. Possibly a year."

"Have mercy!"

But Tomina surrendered, and a quarter of an hour later was driving home
in her neat little brougham, having promised her doctor to leave town
the end of the ensuing week. He was to find her retreat and arrange
everything for her comfort.

Her stepmother's house in Park Lane was very crowded when she returned
to it. Lady Gavin's "At Homes" were much appreciated. She was a woman
to whom philanthropy and politics appealed very strongly, but she was
also one of Society's favourites, and Tomina in her present mood rather
shrank from joining the gay throng. But she pressed her lips together
and did it, and was the life and spirit of her little circle for the
next hour.

Then she went upstairs to her luxurious room, and as it was early
March, drew up a chair in front of her blazing fire and began to think.
In a flash she reviewed her life.

Up to seven years old, she and her beloved twin brother, Tom, had
revelled in a country home. Then came their father's second marriage;
the country house was shut up and finally sold. London—a boarding
school for Tom, a governess for herself. Occasional visits to Scotland
and the sea, then the death of her father. After that, more schoolroom
life—a year in Germany, another one in Paris. And then Tomina came
home to take part in the tireless nerve-racking life of a "débutante"
in Society. She was highly strung, clever, and thorough in all her
undertakings.

She pursued the goddess of pleasure with a zest that made her
stepmother uneasy. Lady Gavin was kind to her stepdaughter, and no girl
had more opportunities of getting the most out of town life than she
had for several years. Many wondered that she did not marry, but Tomina
kept her own counsel.

She was bright, she was good company, and a general favourite. But her
dearest friends always complained that she would not confide in them.

Now, as she looked into the flames of her fire, Tomina's bright eyes
shadowed. She remembered too well the passage in her life which had
altered her whole outlook, and made her pursue her gaiety with such
reckless "abandon" and such a determined indifference to the needs of
her physical being.

She was twenty-seven now, and she had pretty well exhausted all her
resources for killing time. Motoring, yachting, golfing, travelling—she
had had a surfeit of them all. She dragged her weary feet through
endless receptions and social crushes, listened with an aching head to
operas and concerts, looked out with weary eyes at all the best plays,
and acknowledged to herself as she leant back in her cushioned chair
that she was bored to death.

"I don't care to live, but I don't want to die," she murmured, and her
eyes began to twinkle in spite of herself. "Who or what in the wide
world could satisfy me? I feel I am a superfluity. I think if somebody
or something wanted me, I could continue to exist. As it is, how
delighted some of my acquaintances will be when I disappear from their
society for a time! Shall I ever come back, I wonder?"

A knock at the door interrupted her musing. It was her stepmother.

"Well, Tomina, you have been to the doctor's. My dear child, you do
look worn! What did he say?"

Lady Gavin settled herself down upon a comfortable couch and looked at
her stepdaughter a little critically.

"He has told me to cut and run," Tomina responded in a brisk tone. "You
won't miss me, and if you do want a girl in the house, have Madge—she
will be in the seventh heaven at the very thought."

"Where does he want you to go? To the Riviera?"

"No, to some ungetatable place in England. I am to vegetate and imbibe
unpolluted air, and invoke Nature to be my nurse. You know the jargon.
He is a little crazed over country life."

"My dear, how dreadful! He does not expect me to go with you?"

Tomina smiled, and her smile irradiated her face with sunshine.

"No, dear, he is not quite so cruel. I am to be buried for about a
year."

"Perhaps," said Lady Gavin, slowly, "it is the best thing you can
do. Your nerves seem all gone to pieces, and you will return to us
thoroughly refreshed and rejuvenated. But I think he might have sent
you to the Continent. Will you take your maid?"

"Not Juliette. She would never stand the dullness. I shall take no one.
I am going to try the simple life."

"You have often tried that at our week-end cottages."

"But not alone."

Lady Gavin looked at her in a perplexed fashion.

"You won't be long away, Tomina. I am quite positive that you will
never stand the loneliness of it."

"I don't know. Spring is coming, and spring has still a fascination for
me."

Then Lady Gavin plunged into another subject which was more to her
liking, and Tomina listened with a good-natured indifference to her
stepmother's proposition of a fancy fair for a certain charitable
society which was in dire need of funds.

Her departure from town did not make much stir amongst her relations
and friends. They all knew she was not well, and thought it a wise move.

Dr. Carisbrooke was as good as his word. He came round and told her
that he had found the very place for her—a little old-world village
tucked away in a corner, two miles off any main high-road, and at the
top of such a steep stony lane that no motorist would be able to mount
it.

"It is a furnished cottage, and the tenant has suddenly come into a
property of her own and wants to let it."

"And what about servants?" asked Tomina.

He looked a little blank.

"Won't you take some of your own?"

"My dear doctor, our maids think the country only fit for the most
ignorant and depraved of our fellow-creatures. I suppose there is
somebody in the village who will do for me. I hope I am not surrounded
by other cottages and children?"

"No, I have been down to inspect it myself. It stands alone at the end
of the village, and just touches the rectory gardens."

Tomina made a little grimace.

"I hope I am not to be tucked into the pocket of the Rector?" she said.

"I believe he is a delightful sort of parson—one of the Herberts, a
county family. He won't hurt you."

Tomina looked unconvinced.

"You will be nine miles from a station, and no one owns a motor in the
village. You see, I am preparing you for the worst."

"It all sounds so awful that you cannot let me down any farther. I am
at the very bottom."


But Tomina made her arrangements for her move very quickly, and
started from Paddington Station one morning, not altogether certain
whether dislike or curiosity preponderated in her mind for this fresh
experience.

It was a wild, wet day. Rain lashed against the carriage windows. She
was alone in a first-class carriage. She had papers, flowers, a basket
of early strawberries, and the affectionate farewell of some of her
friends who came to see her off still rang in her ears.

"If I were only a poor woman," she murmured to herself, "I should enjoy
my comforts. I shall really practise the simple life for a time—not
play at it."

She looked out of the misty windows reflectively, then closed her eyes,
and, though sleep was far from her, determinedly refused to meditate
upon her past or future.

It was about four o'clock when she reached her destination. The small
country station looked deserted and depressing. Rain and wind assailed
her as she alighted on the strip of gravel bordered by wall-flowers. A
pleasant-looking station-master came up to her.

"Are you the lady for Little Honeycombe? A fly has been ordered for you
from Westlebury, and is waiting."

Tomina stepped through the tiny booking-office, and climbed into a very
ancient and musty carriage.

The driver was hidden in a big mackintosh. He seemed to take
very little interest in his fare or in her luggage. It was the
station-master who packed everything in for her, and gave her a
cheering nod as she drove off.

"'Tisn't a nice welcome for you, ma'am, but rain is good for us or
'twouldn't be sent."

Tomina's progress was slow. Having been accustomed to motors and
taxi-cabs, she thought at first that the horse never went out of
a walk. But soon the very slowness of the journey began to have
compensations.

"I don't want to hurry," she repeated to herself. "There is nothing to
hurry for—nobody waiting at the other end. If I take a week driving to
this village, it does not matter."

Arriving at the top of a hill, the fly came to a standstill. Tomina
opened the window. The rain was stopping. There was a faint yellow
light stealing across the sky, and standing talking to her driver was a
tall figure in shooting tweeds. He doffed his cap when he caught sight
of her, then strode away whistling to his dogs. Tomina put her head
out. There was a peculiar freshness and sweetness in the air. At the
bottom of another steep hill she made her driver stop.

"I will get out and walk," she said. "I want some air. What is the
matter with your horse? Is he very old?"

"No, 'tis just easy-goin'. He's a bit lazy, but we don't hurry in these
parts."

"I see you don't."

Tomina picked up her skirt. The mud and roughness of the road appalled
her, but she resolved not to be daunted. She was going to live in the
country, and if her town shoes did not suit their atmosphere, the
sooner they were discarded in favour of strong country boots the better.

Her bright eyes roved here and there—from a bush of tender green buds
all glittering with raindrops to some patches of exquisite moss on a
bank. The yellow light grew brighter, birds began to sing, the smell of
the fresh moist earth was almost as good as a tonic. Tomina's aching
head was soothed.

When the top of the hill was reached, she looked down over a stretch
of green valley. Somewhere in a hollow a church clock rang out. She
counted the strokes. Five o'clock. In a flash, she saw her stepmother's
drawing-room with its heavy perfume of spring flowers, its guests in
fashionable garments, their soft voices relating the latest scandal in
town, and outside the hum and roar of the city life going by.

Her driver accosted her.

"Be you goin' to ride agen?"

"I suppose so. How long is this going on?"

"Another five mile or so."

"How awful! Who was that man who spoke to you?"

"'Twill be the new squire at Corring. St. Vincent they call him."

"Where is Corring?"

He pointed with his whip to a clump of trees down at the bottom of the
hill.

"See them 'chimleys?' That be the house—just done up fine. Charlie
Simmonds got the job from Westlebury, but there was a Lunnon chap down
who fussed round. We drives through the village."

Tomina did not feel interested. She got into the fly, and when a little
later they rumbled through a cobbled street with thatched cottages on
either side, she did not even look out.

Her walk uphill had refreshed her, but strangely tired her, and she
actually fell into an uneasy doze which lasted until with a big jolt
the fly stopped.

"Are we there?" she called out, awaking with a start.

"'Tis a steep pull up."

"You want me to walk?"

She sprang out, and found herself looking up a steep lane. Cottages
seemed to rise higher and higher until they clustered round a rugged
old grey tower with a steeple.

"Little Honeycombe Church," announced the driver.

Then he started his horse. Tomina slipped behind the fly, and brought
up the rear.

"Little Honeycombe," she murmured to herself. "I wonder if I shall get
any honey out of you?"

She was conscious as she struggled up the steep street of various heads
peeping out at the cottage doors and windows.

Halfway up she met the white-haired Rector. He lifted his hat, then
held out his hand.

"We don't need an introduction. Welcome to Honeycombe. I have heard
about you, of course, and I have taken the liberty of sending in a few
flowers. We are next-door neighbours, you know."

"I shall not be a very neighbourly person, I am afraid," said Tomina.
Her face was grave, but her eyes smiled.

"You are coming here to regain health, I hope, and the sun is shining
out after a day of storm and wet. It's a good omen."

Tomina felt irritated, why she did not know.

"I'm going to my funeral," she said, humour getting the better of her
annoyance, and she passed on up the street with a little smiling nod.

"I want to be alone," she said to herself. "I will not be befriended so
quickly."

The top of the lane was reached at last. Then they passed a
school-house, the church, the rectory in its grounds with a high stone
wall round it, and finally they came to the cottage.

Seen in that March evening's sun, Tomina was forced to own that she
could find no fault with its outward appearance.

It stood back in a green garden. A grass lawn stretched across from a
yew hedge to an old red brick wall. In the centre was an old-fashioned
mulberry tree. There was a wide porch painted green, a border of
daffodils under the low broad windows. The cottage itself was white
with a thatched roof, and the upstair windows were framed with red
japonica in full bloom. Tomina opened the wooden gate and went slowly
up the path. The front door stood open.

A very small lobby and rather steep stairs at once confronted her. For
a moment she hesitated. And then an oldish woman, upright but very
small, came briskly forward.

"Good afternoon, ma'am. I saw your carriage, so I slipped over, and the
fire is burning brightly. Our Rector, he thought you'd like me to be
here to welcome you. I did for the lady which has left, and not having
a family and being a widow and having lived in good service—"

"You thought you would suit me," said Tomina, looking at her kindly.
"I'm very glad to see you, for I'm tired, and I want somebody to do
everything for me. May I see the rooms?"

She was shown into the biggest sitting-room. A very pleasant,
simply-furnished room it was.

Appallingly simple, Tomina told herself. The walls were whitewashed;
the carpet was a green felt. There was a straight narrow couch, an oak
gate table, two oak armchairs with green cushions, and an oak press in
the corner. No pictures on the walls; no curtains. On the opposite side
of the lobby was a tiny room with table, a glass cupboard of china,
and four rush chairs. But on each table was a big jar daffodils and
narcissus.

"It is austere and pure," murmured Tomina to herself, "but can I live
up to it?"

Without a word she mounted to her bedroom.

Whitewashed walls again, a broad low bed, and the same severe style of
furniture.

"Miss Wadhurst would have no hornyments," said the woman, quickly
reading Tomina's silent disapproval. "She were one for air and health
and cleanliness, and were that tidy it was a pleasure to clean up for
her."

"Yes, it all looks beautifully clean. Will you see to my luggage and
pay for the carriage? Here, take my purse. And then could you bring me
some hot water?"

Susan Jobson walked down the stairs with uplifted head and heart.

This was a true lady, she assured herself, worthy of her service, for
she trusted her on the spot before she really knew her.

The hot water was brought, and then a little later, Tomina descended
the steep stairs to find a fire crackling in the biggish sitting-room
and Susan bustling about preparing a little meal.

"Shall we say a tea dinner, ma'am, for to-night, being now six o'clock?
I've taken the liberty to order a few things from the shop. The Rector
thought I might."

"I'm not hungry," asserted Tomina—"anything will do."

Then she sauntered out into the tiny red-tiled kitchen, peeped into the
still tinier back kitchen and larder, and then found her way out at the
back of the house.

Here was a delightful old garden, originally an apple orchard, with
winding paths between herb beds and herbaceous borders. The most
delicious scent was in the air. She looked about and found it came from
a sweet-briar hedge that ran the whole length of the garden. At the
end of it was a small lawn surrounded by old apple trees, and under
a bit of old brick wall was a seat. Tomina seated herself upon it,
disregarding the dampness of the wood.

She lifted her head. The scent of the wood smoke from the cottage
mingled with that of the wet sweet-briar hedge, and she opened her
lips to inhale it. A blackbird from behind her suddenly burst into his
liquid melody of song. There was a hush and stillness around. She sat
with folded hands; the little creases upon her forehead and between her
eyes smoothed themselves out, and she murmured to herself—

"I believe I am going to like it here."

She sat on unheeding of time. The yellow light in the sky slowly faded
away, the blackbird finished his song, and only the fussy twittering
of the birds getting ready for bed remained. And then up the path came
Susan.

"Oh, ma'am, you'll really catch your death of cold if you sit there
without a wrap on. And your repast is waiting for you."

Tomina laughed outright.

"I feel more ready for my 'repast' than I did when I arrived. Now, tell
me, what did your former lady do all day long?"

"Miss Wadhurst, ma'am, was a lady of great talents. She painted
beautiful pictures, and could darn a tablecloth so as you couldn't see
the darn at all. She was always doing, Miss Wadhurst was. She was out
and in all day long, and when it rained she would say to me: 'Now,
Susan, for a long day at dressmakin'.' And her dresses were that well
cut and finished, and had the style too, that was the envy of the
village."

"I suppose she was too busy to read," said Tomina, stifling a yawn.
"There are no books in the house, I notice."

"I really don't think," said Susan slowly and meditatively, "that she
required to read. She knew such a lot, you see, ma'am. It was all
stored in her head."

"Oh, what a paragon!"

Tomina almost danced into the house. The fresh keen air was already
bracing her. She sat down to a fried chop and potatoes, a baked
apple, and a cup of tea which would have frightened her doctor by its
strength. The country bread and butter pleased her.

After her meal was over, she had some further talk with Susan, and
engaged her definitely as her one and only factotum. Then she retired
to her bedroom, and spent quite half of the ensuing night in sleep—a
thing which she had not done for months past.

CHAPTER II

SETTLING DOWN

WHEN the morning came, Tomina crept to her window and looked out. A
most wonderful stretch of country met her view. She gazed at it in
delight, and then was surprised at herself for enjoying it so much.

"Perhaps, after all, I may have a country soul. I'm certainly thankful
to be away from our ceaseless grind in town: 'The treadmill of
dissipation,' I heard a preacher say once—and he hit the right nail on
the head."

She dressed herself leisurely, then went downstairs to find a simple
breakfast waiting for her.

It was a bright sunny morning; the birds were full of song outside; the
air again struck Tomina as being peculiarly sweet and fresh. She opened
the window and looked across at the little church.

"I suppose you are all very good here, Susan?"

Susan gave a little smile.

"We are much as most folk are," she said.

"And have you no shops near you—no town? Surely the village girls want
to buy little fineries sometimes?"

"We're seven miles from Damerton; they walk in and out. I've done it
myself scores of times."

"I suppose a carrier comes along."

"Ah, miss, I only wish it would! When I was a girl, we had one. John
Thomas his name was, and he died, and we've never had one since. There
seems nobody with a spark of gumption to do it, and the roads are bad,
and we're off the high-road, and they say it wouldn't pay one: Why,
I remember we used to take our eggs and creams and butter to market,
and make quite a lot out of 'em. I'm sure it would pay. It would be a
downright blessing to many of us if we had a carrier."

"Well, the question is, how am I to get into Damerton? I want to buy
a few cushions and an easy-chair or two. I really cannot live like
this. I must have some alleviation from my Spartan rule of life. And
books—oh, Susan, I shall want a lot of books to keep me going!"

"Maybe the Rector is going in; he drives himself in to a rare lot o'
meetings, and 'tis mostly on a Friday, for that be the market day."

"The first step, then, is to get hold of the Rector."

Tomina demolished her breakfast thoughtfully. By the time it was over,
she had changed her mind: her moods were variable. She assured herself
that she was too tired to make the effort. Then she sauntered out into
the sunshine.

"What a blessing to be a stranger in a strange land! I needn't speak a
word to a single soul."

She crossed the road and peeped into the church. The door was open, and
she saw that it was a very simple unpretentious little building.

She was not much interested in churches, so she did not pursue her
investigation. Then she went on up a little narrow road, and suddenly
caught sound of her own name.

Two people were talking the other side of the hedge.

"'Gavin,' did you say, John? I wonder if she is one of the Gavins of
Cheshire? I shall call this afternoon. An elderly woman, I suppose? She
could not come to a better place for quiet. I wonder if she would like
to come to my working party? I must ask her."

Tomina quickened her pace.

"I shan't be in. Now for a horse. Where shall I find one? I won't be
drawn into parish working-parties. That must be the Rectoress."

She caught sight of a snug red-gabled house between some budding lilac
trees, and concluded it was the Rectory. She walked along an empty
road for some distance without meeting a soul, and then she suddenly
came upon a green wooden gate leading up to a very pretty old thatched
farmhouse.

Leaning over the gate, smoking his pipe, was a stalwart-looking man
dressed in rough tweeds and leggings.

"The farmer," Tomina assured herself.

Encountering rather a keen glance from a pair of very blue eyes under
some fiercely shaggy eyebrows, Tomina lifted her head and with sweet
hauteur spoke—

"Good morning. Can you tell me if I can hire a trap in this
neighbourhood?"

The pipe and cap were removed at once.

"Were you wanting to drive yourself?"

"I should prefer to do so."

"Perhaps Crake at the 'Red Bull' would oblige you. It's a little way
farther on, half a mile or so, just on the turnpike road."

"Thank you."

Tomina hesitated for a moment, and glanced over the gate.

"What a sweet old house!" she said, and a note of enthusiasm crept into
her tone.

The farmer smiled.

"It's my own," he said almost boyishly, "and it's good to hear it
admired."

"And you are married, I suppose?"

His face turned scarlet.

"Two months ago, and I'm waiting for the postman. My wife is expecting
to hear from her mother."

His voice was that of a gentleman. Tomina began to feel interested.

"Is this your wife coming down the path? Please forgive a stranger's
inquisitiveness, but I have come to live in this village, and I am
bound to know you sooner or later."

He turned, and seeing the light flash from his eyes Tomina knew the
answer to her question and walked on.

"A bride and bridegroom," she murmured to herself. "How I have disliked
the species amongst my own kind! But here it seems fitting, and it's
spring and the time for youth and love. Now, here is the postman. I
hope he has not anything for me."

He had not, but he looked Tomina up and down with a kindly critical
eye, and she asked him in that sweet calm tone of hers—

"What do you think of me?"

"You 'em woll sott up," was his brief reply.

Tomina shook her head comically.

"I do hope I shall learn to speak like you soon," she said. "Now,
listen to me, postman. Don't you dare to bring me any letters for a
week at least. I am supposed to be buried, and a buried person receives
no communications from her friends."

"But you be a long way from churchyard," said the postman, with a
twinkle in his eye. "We did hear tell you weer in a gallopin' disease
of the nerves, akin to consoomption, Mary Anne said. But you look fair
well and saucy."

"Thank you. We mustn't stop gossipping, for there's somebody waiting
for his letters."

The postman had put down his bag upon the ground, and was now leaning
against the hedge lighting up his pipe.

As Tomina pointed to the farm, he hastily shouldered his burden again.

"Eh, day! That be John Merryweather; he be a terrible one for lickin'
the toime. But he be high stock: his feyther was Parson Merryweather to
Crottesley, an' he be wedded fresh. Makes 'im testy and short in his
temper."

He strode away.

Tomina pursued her road, and eventually found the "Red Bull" and hired
a rather shabby trap. It was to call for her at two o'clock that
afternoon. She returned home very pleased with her morning's work.

Susan shook her head when she noted her white cheeks and her scanty
appetite for her midday meal.

"You be going too fast, ma'am. You haven't got over your journey
yesterday. You 'ad best go upstairs and have a lie down; 'tis a long
drive to Damerton."

Tomina laughed wilfully.

"I have been stagnating all this morning," she said. "My good Susan,
you have no idea of my strenuous life in town. We fill every minute of
every day. I can't drop into my grave with too big a flop. I shall bury
myself gradually."

Susan said no more.


The trap came round. It was indeed a shabby one, and the horse was
huge and bony. Tomina was a good whip, and had more than once driven a
four-in-hand through Hyde Park: but as she started, and the springless
trap bumped and jolted and swung her from side to side and the old
horse ambled and cantered in the most unexpected and ungainly fashion,
she began to wish she had not been so impatient over her shopping.

It was past six o'clock before she returned. And she staggered up the
garden path, to fall into Susan's arms in a dead faint.

The trap was loaded with packages, and a man had been picked up at the
"Red Bull" to deposit them inside the house and take the trap back.

Susan was too concerned with Tomina to think of taking in the parcels.

She got her upon the little couch in the sitting-room, and by trying
different restoratives brought her round to consciousness again. Then
she helped her up the narrow stairs and put her to bed like a baby.

"It's my stupid heart," Tomina explained. "That brute of a horse pulls
your arms out, and I did a lot of shopping. I shall be all right
to-morrow."

But she was not, and another faint occurred when she tried to dress
herself the next morning.

Susan began to get alarmed, for Tomina's face was a grey-blue.

"You must have the doctor, ma'am. We have such a nice one—a real
gentleman he is—and he only lives three miles off. I'll send Jack Gigg;
he'll pass the house on his way to the farm where he works."

"I don't want doctors," murmured Tomina. "I will go to bed again."

"If only you hadn't tried to do so much! And Mrs. Herbert were round to
see you yesterday. She were fair disappointed not to find you in."

Susan was determined to have her way. And before noon, Tomina heard
from her bed a horse canter up to her gate, and a very cheery voice say—

"Well, Susan, and who is your patient now? I thought your good lady had
left us?"

Tomina did not hear Susan's reply. But in a moment or two, her door
opened, and in walked a tall broad-shouldered man with dark eyes that
invariably twinkled and a short square-cut brown beard.

"A country bumpkin," was Tomina's first impression. But when he spoke,
his soft mellow voice had the ring of a gentleman, and his hands were a
true doctor's—slender and strong and delicately made.

Tomina was now sitting up in a pink silk tea-jacket, resting back on
her pillows, with a faint flush on her face.

"I did not send for you," she said; "Susan is responsible."

"Well, she has forestalled my visit. Only I did not put two and two
together, and had no notion I was coming to see Carisbrooke's patient.
He has written to me about you. He and I are both Guy's men, and did
our terms together. But he gave me no address, and I did not expect to
see you in such simple quarters."

"Dr. Carisbrooke might have given me a fair start. He never told me he
had written."

A little wrinkle came between Tomina's eyes, then she laughed.

"You know all my iniquities. I simply overdid it yesterday. This is an
experiment, my coming down here. If you knew what my life has been, you
would feel sorry for me; for I assure you I have some brain left, and
to stagnate like a vegetable will need some determination."

"Oh, we don't stagnate here!" said the doctor, cheerfully, putting his
hand out and feeling her pulse. "Life goes on as strongly and fully
and unexpectedly with us as with you in town. It is not stagnation to
change from one world to another. It's rather interesting. You are
the second patient sent down here to rest; you have both been burning
the two ends of your candles, and yet you are as far asunder in
circumstances as the poles."

"Who is my fellow-sufferer?"

"She is a Miss Merryweather—a very clever and cultured woman, who has
treated her body disgracefully and is now paying for it. She has been
working in London, and has dipped into countless philanthropic business
ventures, has been poor law guardian and a matron of an institution for
convalescent gentle-people, and a board school mistress, and heaven
knows what else! And then the crash came, and she's at present lodging
in a farmhouse. Her brother lives close by, but she's too exhausted to
have speech with him. She's doing a rest cure, and like you, she is
resenting the fate which has overtaken her."

"Oh, I don't know," said Tomina, perversely. "I think I welcome this as
a change. I am a traveller leaving the busy high-road and turning down
a green bye-lane. I don't know what is going to turn up, but am ready
for anything. Why don't you stagnate down here?"

"Too many human beings," said the doctor looking at her with his
twinkling eyes. "I'm very interested in humanity. People are always
surprising and edifying me."

"Have I done either?" asked Tomina.

The doctor looked at her in silence. Then he got up and paced the room.

"Oh, you young creatures!" he said. "Why won't you be temperate and
moderate in all things? Health is the best gift ever given us, and you
murder it and throw it away for mere soap-bubbles."

"Please, go on. You're very like Dr. Carisbrooke. We all have our
worlds. Your clever Miss Merryweather has hers, and considers that
working the brains that have been given to her comes before everything
else. I—well, you consider me a fool, I know. I'll own that I have
blown many bubbles and seen them burst and disperse. But I've had a
full life, and been much too engrossed and busy to let my health stop
me in anything that I wished to do. Now you are a slave to health; you
think of it, you work for it, you worship it—it is your profession.
Perhaps one day you may find that other treasures outweigh it in the
scales."

Tomina was nothing if she was not audacious.

The country doctor only smiled at her.

"You are feeling better," he said. "I'll send you a tonic I should like
you to take. Is it any use telling you to take a week's quiet—very
quiet—living before you attempt another drive to Damerton?"

"I might try it," said Tomina, very meekly.

She felt snubbed, and was too surprised to resent it.

He stayed a short time longer, then got up to go.

"When you are more sociably inclined," he said, "I should like you to
know Miss Merryweather."

"Must I know everybody round?" Tomina protested. "My old doctor has
banished me from people."

The doctor gave a little nod.

"There is plenty of time," he remarked, "What you have to do now is to
sleep and eat and cultivate a serene disposition."

"I have always been considered most even-tempered," Tomina remarked.

He shook hands with her.

"And when I am out of hearing, you will say, 'What rude bears these
country practitioners can be'!"

She gave a little laugh.

When he had gone, she did not know whether she liked him or disliked
him.

But she was tired and exhausted, and slipped down amongst her pillows
and went fast asleep.


It took Tomina four days to pull up after that drive to Damerton. But
when she came downstairs, her usual energy began to assert itself. She
made Susan undo the unwieldy packages which were blocking up the narrow
hall. Two very comfortable easy-chairs, some soft, rich rugs, half a
dozen cushions of different shapes and sizes, a couple of footstools,
and over a dozen books, were all distributed and arranged in the
sitting-rooms.

Then Tomina went out into the spring garden and brought in great bowls
of budding sweet-briar, golden daffodils, and red japonica.

When she rested that afternoon in the most comfortable chair, with two
novels by her side, and inhaled the perfume of the sweet-briar and
listened to the far-away call of the cuckoo, she owned to herself that
life here would be bearable.

When Susan brought her tea, she was in a mood for gossip.

"Susan, who is this gifted Miss Merryweather? Do you know her?"

"Indeed I do, ma'am. The Merryweathers lived in the next parish to
this, four mile by the road, and he was the vicar there. There were a
long family, but four died young of a dreadful outbreak of diphtheria,
and the poor mother wore herself out and was buried with twins within a
twelvemonth. I was brought up in their parish, so I knew them all. Mr.
George went into the Navy, and Mr. Harry went to India as a judge, I
believe. And then there were Miss Alice and Mr. Philip left, and when
the vicar died, she went up to London. But Mr. Phil he has always lived
here; he just dotes on farming. And before the vicar died, he took the
Glebe Farm, and then there was a bit of money, and he bought the Brook
Farm just outside the village."

"Oh yes; I know. I thought the name seemed familiar, I saw him the
other day. Hasn't he just married?"

Susan nodded a little mysteriously.

"'Twas a trial to Miss Alice. She always came back and forwards to the
farm when she got a holiday from her work, and she were powerful fond
of her brother. I went up to take their cook's place when she were home
one Christmas; and my dear life, she talked as clever as a parson. She
were always reading and spouting to Mr. Phil. Her favourite words were:

"'Let us cultivate our intellect, Phil.'

"But he—well, he used to smoke and say nothing. She were learning him
all she could to take to her books, and she is a rare scholar. She had
a room all got up for herself at the farm, and 'twas nothing but books
on every side.

"Well, men are men, ma'am, and Mr. Phil it was his way to live at peace
and say nothing. But he rather wearied, I used to think of his sister's
clever talk. And then, when she went back to London, he used to ride
over Damerton and courted pretty Miss May, a parson's daughter who
lived with her widowed mother who taught a small private school. And
when Miss Alice come home next, 'twas all fixed up, and the weddin' was
talked of. I always said 'twere a heavy blow to her, but she went right
away again, and when the weddin' came off two months ago, she were too
broken in health to come to it.

"Just six weeks ago, Mr. Phil he went up to London and fetched her
back, and said his home were hers, but I haven't heard the full rights
of it. I know she and Miss May only had two weeks together, and then
Dr. Merton he stepped in, and took her over to the Glebe Farm where
Mrs. Tubbs has been nursing her back to health.

"I always do say a bride and bridegroom must be left clear of outsiders
for a full year, and 'tis only two months now since they were wed. Miss
Alice be a dear by herself, but she and Miss May—I ought to say Mrs.
Merryweather—are just the very opposite of dispositions, and with one
man atween them—well, there was a bit of friction, and our dear doctor
just settled it. We all love Dr. Merton; he puts puzzles together and
cures other ills besides that of the body."

"Is he married?"

"He was, ma'am; and the sweetest lady you ever set eyes on to look at,
but a nasty stinging tongue and a jealous temper. God forgive me for
speaking so, and she in her grave. But she did bring bitterness into
his life, only it seemed to make him the sweeter! Then she left him
after givin' birth to a boy which died with her. And he have lived on
quiet and happy with his housekeeper. Only I've seen him look wistful
like at small boys. He be very fond of children."

Susan paused for breath. Tomina gave a little yawn. She was tired of
her neighbours histories, and Susan wisely slipped away.

CHAPTER III

TOMINA'S SCHEME

TOMINA soon became acquainted with her Rector and his wife. She liked
him. He was a simple straightforward courteous gentleman who lived
entirely in his village, and for the good of his parishioners. Out of
the pulpit he was beloved, by sick-beds he was an ideal parson, but
he had no gift for preaching, and his sermons were dry and dull, and
delivered in a monotonous sing-song tone.

Tomina went to church, but did not enjoy it. Mrs. Herbert tried to take
her under her wing and manage her, but her efforts failed. Tomina was
sweetly and determinedly rebellious. She looked at Mrs. Herbert with
humorous eyes when that lady called for the third time, and told her
that if she would not join her working party, she could not object to
working at home for the Mission in India, to which all their work was
sent.

"I never work, Mrs. Herbert. I never put a stitch in my own clothes,
and would not presume to attempt those of others."

"But surely with all your leisure you could give a little of it towards
the needs of our Mission?"

"I want all my leisure for myself," said Tomina, calmly.

"But, my dear Miss Gavin, we do not live for ourselves alone, do we?"

"I do."

Tomina was sweet and smiling, but a naughty twinkle was in her eyes;
and Mrs. Herbert saw and resented it.

"Then no wonder you are a bundle of tired nerves. I pity you from the
bottom of my heart."

Mrs. Herbert left her.

And when she had gone, Tomina heaved a heavy sigh.

"I let her have the last word, but how tiring these good people are!"

Nevertheless, Mrs. Herbert's last word stung her and recurred to her
often.

"A bundle of tired nerves! Yes, I am that, but I'm too tired to mind
it, and if I'm worried by people here, I shall creep back to town and
die there. And yet there are days when life here seems worth living. If
it was always fine, I should feel happy. The country air is better than
all the doctor's tonics."


The wet spring days were certainly a trial to Tomina. But soon the
weather improved, and restless energy began to assert itself.

She altered her furniture; she tried her hand at gardening. She walked
round the village, and visited every single cottage in it on one
afternoon. And when she came back and was too exhausted to eat her
evening meal, Susan remonstrated severely with her—

"It's too foolish of you, ma'am. You never do nothing by halves. Why,
our Rector himself don't make all his visits in one day. It takes him
a month to do the round, leastways unless there's sickness. Each of us
don't expect him more than once to the month!"

"I think I've always been accustomed to go on till I drop," said
Tomina, languidly. "We do in London, you know. We have no time to
rest our bodies, as you country people do. And I wanted to see what
everybody was like."

"Did they all take it well?" asked Susan, with a shade of anxiety in
her tone.

Tomina laughed.

"They were civil; perhaps not quite as sweetly civil as I was. Some of
them pitied me, and seemed to think me a very poor thing! When I get a
little stronger, I shall surprise them and you, Susan. I have a plan in
my head which I am longing to carry out, but I shan't tell you yet."

Susan looked at her and said no more. She could not understand this
London young lady, who had so many moods, and was so unlike her quiet
and conventional Miss Wadhurst. Yet, in spite of her contrary moods,
and her inability to help at all in the work of her small cottage,
Susan confessed to herself that there was a strange charm about her.

"It seems to me," she confided to her great friend Mary Ann Grubb,
"that there be a sort of gentry in the world that no storm or bout of
misfortune can beat down. They hold their heads high through it all.
This young lady of mine is plainly alone in the world, and now her
health be given way, her fine friends have deserted her, and she be
forced to seek for health and quiet in one of our country villages.

"Yet, though she have let out to me the many servants she have had, she
daily goes her way, and expects me to wait on her hand and foot and
count it a favour and honour to do it. My Miss Wadhurst would sooner
do anything than ring the bell for me, and the 'pologies she made if
she made an extra mess of her room or did anything to put me out. Now,
this lady she litters her rooms, and won't so much as keep the fire
in, or make her own tea, or dust her bedroom, or even fasten her own
dresses, she quietly waits for me to do it all. And she has a way with
her—well—there! 'Tis no good talking; I'd lie down and lick her boots
if she asked me to do it! She just charms the will out of one!"


The warm weather came very soon. One afternoon, Tomina was on the top
of a ladder which was placed against the wall of her cottage. Under the
thatch of her roof some swallows had built their nests, and curiosity
had prompted her to pay them a visit.

Suddenly she heard voices below her, and looking down, saw Dr. Merton
and a lady at her gate.

She descended quickly, and though feeling annoyed, welcomed them with
her sweetest smile.

"This is Miss Merryweather," said Dr. Merton. "I want you to become
acquainted. She and you are both town birds. I expect you will have
many interests in common."

Tomina's bright eyes encountered Miss Merryweather's grave soft ones.

Alice Merryweather was tall and dark. She was more interesting-looking
than handsome. She was dressed in grey with a simple shady straw hat
upon her head. When she spoke and smiled, she was a very attractive
woman.

She smiled upon Tomina now.

"I meant to call upon you," she said, "but I have felt unfit for much
walking, and to-day, meeting Dr. Merton, he beguiled me here almost
before I knew it. May we come in? Are we disturbing you?"

Dr. Merton hastily explained that he had visits to make in the village,
and disappeared. Tomina took her visitor indoors.

"When somebody tells me that I must be friendly with a certain person,
I generally feel the reverse," began Tomina, with her gay laugh. "Do
you think we have many tastes in common?"

"Shall we probe each other to see?" said Alice, smiling.

"No, we'll just chatter about our neighbours," said Tomina; "that seems
the only subject of conversation in the country."

A slight frown came over Alice's face.

Tomina rattled on—

"I have heard all about you, but though we both come from town our
poles are asunder. My world is not yours, nor is yours mine."

"How do you know? We are both women, both interested in our
fellow-creatures."

"Oh no, I am not! I am bored to death with them; I have come down here
to get away from them. My world is what the preachers call 'the giddy
round of pleasure.' Your round is one of duty and work, I am sure."

"I suppose I look like a working woman, but may not work be a pleasure?"

"To a bee—not to a butterfly. That is what my doctor calls me—a
'butterfly of fashion.'"

"Well, we are both living in the same environments at present. Are you
fond of the country?"

Tomina looked at her with grave thoughtful eyes.

"I was not. But I really do believe I am getting fond of it. I still
feel a stranger and an outsider to its ways and customs. I have been
investigating a swallow's nest for the first time in my life. You found
me at it."

"I have been brought up in the country," said Alice, "but I do not
think I love it. Life is so full, so strenuous in town; one's mind
starves in the country."

"Does it?" asked Tomina, with interest. "Tell me how."

"Oh, you get so small, so petty-minded, so narrow. Books may be
available, but they are not the same as rubbing up against and
conversing with thoughtful cultured men and women. Country people are
self-centred; town people take broader and bigger views of life."

"I suppose my friends have not been intellectual," said Tomina,
thoughtfully, "at least not many of them. I did not find we were so
broad-minded. We were entirely occupied with our own set. Don't you
think that in town and country every individual can live any life they
like?"

"Perhaps so. I feel a great pity for those who don't cultivate their
brains. They lose so much."

"Do they?"

"Necessarily they must. How can they enjoy pictures if they know
nothing of history or art or mythology or the classics? How can
they enjoy beautiful buildings, unless they have some knowledge of
architecture? Or politics and the progress of our Empire unless they
know something of political economy and constitutional history?"

"Oh!" gasped Tomina. "Please stop! You must be an encyclopædia of
knowledge. I maintain that every one enjoys life differently. There
are circles for us all. I am in the gay frivolous one; you are in the
serious, contemplative one. You educate your mind. I educate my senses
and my body. And we've both overdone it, have we not? And now we're
vegetating together."

She would not be grave. She discussed the village and its people; she
talked of her garden and her novels. And then when Alice rose to go,
Tomina said gaily—

"And now you're going off disgusted with my levity, and you'll tell our
friend the doctor that really you cannot stand such shallow frivolous
gossip as comes from my lips. There can be no point of interest in
common between us."

"No," said Alice, smiling, "I think our brains may be equal in quality,
but you are refusing to use yours at present, and I—well, lately my
brains feel used up and asleep, so I cannot preach. I expect you will
think me prosy and pedantic—."

"At all events, you haven't tried to make me work, as Mrs. Herbert has.
Are you piously inclined, Miss Merryweather?"

Alice hesitated.

"I am a clergyman's daughter, but—but I do not—I have not the faith my
parents had, I am afraid."

"I don't and I won't like good people," said Tomina, rebelliously.

"Then don't have too much to do with Dr. Merton, or with a Mrs. Francis
who lives near."

"Who is she? I have never heard of her."

Alice slightly shrugged her shoulders.

"I prefer to leave others to tell you about her."

She said good-bye and went.

Tomina watched her down the village street; then she made a slight
grimace.

"My dear, I don't think I want to see much of you. You are rather dull."


The very next day, Tomina hired the trap and drove into Damerton.

She came back radiant and in the best of spirits.

"Susan, I am beginning to live! Now, will you guess what I am going to
do? Something that will bring joy to the village; something that will
make my living here worth while after all. Something that will show
what an idler is driven to do when she is being harassed on all sides
for contributing nothing towards the world's good."

Susan shook her head helplessly.

"I know, whatever it is, it will be what nobody else would be likely to
do," she said sagely.

"To-day is Friday. In a fortnight from to-day, a carrier's cart is
going to run from Little Honeycombe to Damerton every Friday, and
on every Tuesday, it is going to run from Little Honeycombe to St.
Martha's Cross. Have you ever been there, Susan? There is a fine market
there and a station on the main line!"

Susan gasped.

"You do beat all, ma'am. However have you managed it, and who's going
to do it?"

"Me myself, Susan. I am going to be the driver. It is my cart and
horse, and I'll sit on the shafts and jog along like a gipsy."

"You are joking?"

"I'm in sober earnest. I happened to see an old carrier's cart lying
idle in a wheelwright's shed. I had a long talk with the wheelwright,
and he promises he will get it rigged up in a fortnight. It's going to
have a brand-new tarpaulin top and will be painted and renovated till
you won't know it from a new one. And my horse is got. A good stout
eight-year-old bay, who won't shy if a cannon is let off in his ear,
and who will make nothing of his two journeys a week."

"And may I ask, ma'am, if you feel indisposed one Friday or Tuesday,
who's to run round to all the villages, and tell 'em as there will be
no carrier that day?"

"I shall never feel indisposed on Tuesday or Friday, if I lie in bed
every other day of the week," said Tomina, valiantly. "I shall never
fail, and if—if—I did, I should get you to be my substitute. You told
me you could drive a cart anywhere!"

Susan retreated from the room with such a face of horror that Tomina
laughed aloud.


The news spread like wildfire over the village; the excitement was
intense.

The very next morning, Mrs. Herbert appeared upon the scene.

"I really must remonstrate with you, my dear girl. It is an impossible
'rôle' for you to play. No woman could do it; you might have tipsy
farmers returning late with you, you don't know who your passengers
would be. All sorts and conditions."

"Yes, I know," said Tomina; "I have thought it all out, but there is
safety in numbers, and I keep to the main roads, and it's going to be
perfectly delightful. I've only to sit still and drive and listen to
the country gossip going on inside; the fresh air and motion will make
me as strong as Hercules! You shall come the first trip with me, if you
like. It will be something like a caravan. I'm tired of motors. I want
to move at a still slow pace and enjoy details."

"And what about the rough weather?"

"I shall sit inside the cart then, instead of outside, and wrap myself
in oilskin, and still enjoy myself."

"It's a mad freak!"

Mrs. Herbert departed with her final shot.

"You are an invalid and under doctor's orders. The strongest woman in
the world ought not to make herself into a carrier!"

Tomina for a moment felt damped, then she pressed her lips firmly
together.

"I am my own mistress, and not 'all the king's horses and all the
king's men' can prevent my doing what I like."


The day after, came Dr. Merton.

"Is this a professional visit?" asked Tomina, with twinkling eyes.

She was looking and feeling remarkably well. Opposition always drew out
all her latent energy.

"Of course, you have come to scold," she went on; "and Mrs. Herbert has
sent you."

"My dear young lady," said Dr. Merton, with much emphasis, "you have my
entire and most hearty approval for this step."

Tomina's eyebrows slightly raised themselves.

"Are you in earnest?"

"Emphatically so! An open-air life is the only cure for you; and two
days a week—it is to be two days, is it not?—will not hurt you in the
very least. If you are fatigued, it will be wholesome fatigue, and you
will sleep the sounder after it; the long day in the air will give you
an appetite, I'll be bound! And the benefit you will bestow upon the
villages round will be your reward."

"Oh, you are a sensible man! I could hug you!"

Then Tomina quieted down.

"It came to me as I was jogging to Damerton in a hired trap, how
fascinating and soothing a country drive is. And then I overtook two
old dames trudging along with baskets of eggs, and I gave them a lift,
and they were such old dears, and called down such blessings on my head
that I began to think if I could not call for them another market day,
and save them their weary four miles in and out. And I had thought of
starting a carrier a short time ago. But now I have determined to be
the carrier myself. And when I undertake a job of any sort, I see it
through, Dr. Merton. You have helped me a good deal by your approval."

"Of course," Dr. Merton said, "there may be difficulties, but if you're
cautious and wise you'll surmount them. And you must have a dog, and
take him with you as your bodyguard. I have two mastiffs, and one is
quite young and as gentle as a lamb, unless you want him to go for any
one. But he guards in splendid fashion. Would you like him? He wants
exercise, and you would grow very fond of him."

"Thank you very much. I don't know whether I should not be frightened
of a big dog. I've been very fond of my stepmother's prize Pekinese.
May I come and see him? Or will you bring him to see me?"

"He is outside the gate now waiting for me," said Dr. Merton, with
alacrity. "I'll bring him in. I don't want two dogs, but I've brought
them up since they were puppies and can vouch for their good behaviour."

The mastiff appeared. He was a huge brown dog with a white patch on his
head and breast.

For one moment Tomina and he eyed each other uncertainly. Then he
stepped up gingerly and sniffed at her feet. Tomina was in a tremor,
but when he sat down in front of her and thumped his tail up and down
upon the ground in approval of her, she took her courage in both hands
and patted his head. Then she took hold of his right paw and stooped,
looking straight into his brown eyes.

"You're a beauty! I won't be afraid of you, but shall I ever have the
courage to give you a licking, I wonder? Perhaps you will never need
it. Are you going to be faithful to me?"

The mastiff blinked and looked a little bored.

Dr. Merton spoke.

"Say 'Yes,' Thor."

The mastiff raised his head and lowered with a nod.

Tomina was enchanted.

"You're a darling! I'll have you. We shall be chums. Ever so many
thanks, Dr. Merton. Is he really a gift? And is Thor his name?"

"If you will accept him, I shall be glad. We called him Thor because
his bark is always heralded by a tremendous growl, and he was born on a
Thursday—Thor's day, you know."

Tomina did not know, but her heart warmed to the mastiff.

"Are you going to leave him here for good?"

"Hardly to-day, I think. You will want a kennel for him, won't you?"

"And you think he'll settle down with me? He won't want to run back to
you?"

"Feed him yourself and be kind to him; and he'll settle down."

Thor had already lain down at Tomina's feet, his nose between his paws.
But beneath his lowered eyelids, his eyes were fixed on his master's
face. And then Dr. Merton went on talking about the carrier's cart.

"I shouldn't have given you my benediction if it had been a motor, for
they're not entirely a health-giving product. But a quiet drive along
the country roads, with your fellow-beings close by, to instruct and
amuse, will be thoroughly beneficial."

"Oh, bother my health!" said Tomina. "I am doing it because I like it,
because I want diversion, and because it is badly needed."

"And what are you going to charge our poor people?"

"Oh, nothing! How could I take any?"

Dr. Merton looked grave.

"It will be a case of mistaken charity, and your cart will be the abode
of any tramp who comes along. You daren't refuse them if you give free
lifts to any one."

It was Tomina's turn to look sober.

"What a tiresome man you are, making such difficulties! I shall have to
charge and put it into the collecting plate on Sunday."

"That you can please yourself about. It is seven miles to Damerton. If
you want to charge them cheaply, make it fourpence single, sixpence
return journey—"

"Can they afford it?"

"Quite well."

Tomina looked dubious.

"I'll consult Susan. She lives in the village and is a discreet woman.
Now, Dr. Merton, who is Mrs. Francis? Where does she live?"

"She's the widow of a naval officer, and she keeps house for her
brother our Squire, Sir Alfred Digby."

"It's strange I have not come across her."

"She and Sir Alfred are both away in town. They will be returning
before long, and then you will meet them."

Tomina looked at him thoughtfully.

"Miss Merryweather coupled her name with yours in a very undesirable
fashion."

Dr. Merton looked rather taken aback.

Tomina began to laugh.

"Oh, don't be frightened! I am rather stupid in the way I put things
sometimes. She said that if I didn't like good people, I mustn't have
anything to do with you or Mrs. Francis—"

"Poor Alice!"

Dr. Merton looked relieved and a little amused.

"Have I proved a bogey?"

"No, and beyond your province, you have not preached to me
objectionably. But I suppose you are feeling your way with me?"

"Perhaps I am," said the doctor, laughing. "Please don't take us at
Miss Merryweather's valuation. Mrs. Francis is sure to call upon you
when she returns, and then you can judge for yourself whether she is a
person to be avoided. I must be off. I'll send Thor over to you in a
couple of days. Keep him fastened up, take him out with a lead, feed
him yourself, and he will be your devoted slave in a week's time."

Dr. Merton shook hands with her and departed.

Tomina was so grateful to him for his unqualified approval of her step
that she bestowed one of her sweetest smiles upon him.

Thor marched away after him with much dignity. When he was at the gate,
he looked back. Was he appraising his future home? Tomina waved her
hand airily to him.

"'Au revoir,' you beauty! When next you come, you come to stay."

Susan heard of Thor with a dismayed face.

"I know them dogs well. They are not vicious, but they are
blood-curdling when they bark, and this cottage is really too small to
hold him, and he'll eat more than both of us together!"

Tomina reassured her.


Before another week was over, Thor had become an inmate of the cottage.
He was a little restless at first, and had a way of shaking himself in
his kennel at night that sounded truly alarming, and was not conducive
to sleep. But Tomina took great pains to conciliate and beguile him,
and in a fortnight, he followed her everywhere.

Thor was sociably inclined; he liked to lie in the sitting-room with
his mistress. He was given a big mat, and was made to understand that
when that was spread inside the door, he could come in and occupy it.
In three days' time, however, he appeared at the door with the mat in
his mouth, and after that, he would bring it in whenever he liked.

Tomina was enchanted with his intelligence, and was always glad of his
companionship.


The carrier's cart was not ready to start in a fortnight. Country
workmen could not hurry; and Tomina had her first lesson in patience.
She spent her time in having some pamphlets printed to say that the
cart would start running in the month of May, and these she distributed
in the villages round.

She found she had a good many arrangements to make as to the horsing
and stabling of her horse and van. At length, she persuaded a small
farmer close by to promise to put them up. He was to be allowed to use
the horse on the intermediate days for light farm work, and he only
charged her for forage.

CHAPTER IV

THE CARRIER'S VAN

AT last, the Friday came when at eight o'clock in the morning, a
brand-new shining covered cart drew up outside Tomina's door, and she
took her seat as driver. It was a lovely spring morning. The crowd of
villagers who assembled to see the start acknowledged that Tomina was
suitably equipped for her position. Her long covert coat and shady
straw hat were simple and unassuming. Town people might have recognised
the Bond Street cut to the coat, and would have known that the Leghorn
hat cost at least three guineas. The tarpaulin of the cart was snowy
white, and the black letters across it showed very plainly its
destination. The brass harness glittered in the sun. Bran, the horse,
reared his head and tossed his great mane, impatient to be off.

At first, Tomina had been afraid that her passengers would be too many.
Every housewife in the village wanted to be in the first trip, but she
craftily held out the advantages of the following Tuesday, when the
rail could be reached, and for sixpence they could visit the county
town.

"I must keep some seats for people on the road," she said.

Susan had quite approved of the charge which the doctor had suggested,
and it was she who made Tomina take plenty of small change with her,
and a big purse from which she could transact her business. She longed
to make the first trip herself, but though Tomina begged her to do so,
she decided to stay at home.

"You be best by yourself and on your own, without me to look on. I
shall hear all about it from Mary Anne!"

Mary Anne was Susan's married sister who lived in the village.

Thor took up his station behind the cart in the orthodox fashion. He
had been well trained from a puppy to follow behind a trap.

There was a great deal of chatter and laughter when the women
climbed in and took their places. Many Anne was first and foremost.
An old woman, Mrs. Crabbe, was going to see her married daughter in
Damerton. Mrs. Tabbs and Mrs. Goss were going to the market. They were
neighbours, and both mothers of "long" families. A little maid-servant
from John Merryweather's farm was spending her "day out." Two young
girls, one from the village shop and the other the blacksmith's
daughter, and a lanky boy of fifteen with a basket of eggs and four
dead fowls in a basket, completed her number.

Tomina cracked her new whip with a flourish. The Rector came out to see
her pass, and some children on their way to school raised a cheer.

Through the village they solemnly went, down rather a steep hill, when
Tomina had to put on her heavy drag, and then along a pleasant sunny
high-road arched by budding trees. Soon the first stop was made. At
the corner of another road, the two women with their egg baskets were
waiting to be taken up. The chatter inside the van waxed fast and
furious.

Tomina sat outside in silence. At times, she studied a pencil list
which Susan had thrust into her hands at starting. There were things
for her own cottage which she had to get, but also there were
commissions from the villagers who had been prevented from coming. The
list of these ran as follows:—

 2 hand half of unblitched corse.
 Mrs. White.

 One boiler from Clarks and he ows fourpence to Tom's Bill
 larst Wed weeke for cissor ground.
 Mary Tubbs.

 Plese Miss sorsages and a peece of beef buttock from Andrews Market.
 Martha Gadd.

 Seven yard of pretty lace for Flories best police.
 Jane Hutton.

 Pudding bason, butter jar, pennorth yeste and four pennorth
 Reckitts blue.
 Fanny Spoke.

There were several other household utensils Tomina could not decipher.
She puckered her brows in perplexity.

"A carrier's tasks are heavier than I thought. I wish I had brought
Susan. But I'll hand over some of these purchases to one of the women
inside."

She pocketed the list, threw up her head, and inhaled with delight the
cool fresh spring air.

When there was a level bit of road, she put Bran at a trot. When
there was a steep hill, she got out and walked, but dared not ask any
villager to copy her example, for they were determined to have the full
value for their money. Presently, she overtook a man with a heavy pack
on his back. He looked like a pedlar.

And as Tomina passed him, he called out a cheery "Good morning."

She stopped.

"Want a lift? We're going to Damerton."

"Ay, and that's my goal. Shall be thankful, but isn't this a new thing?
Never saw a carrier on this road before."

He was clambering in as he spoke and took his seat beside Tomina.

"I like the air," he said.

He was a little dark wiry man about middle age, but his eyes reminded
Tomina of a robin's; they were so round and bead-like and sparkling.

If he was surprised at the unusual kind of carrier by his side, he did
not show it.

"I have had a small stall in the market for my wares," he continued,
"and I shan't know myself, to be spared these five miles! Though this
may be my last day in these parts. I am moving on to other towns."

"I suppose you're a pedlar?"

"A pedlar of sorts, though they call me a colporteur."

"I don't think I've ever heard of a colporteur," said Tomina, slowly.

He gave her a quick look.

"You're not of these parts, miss. Is it owner of this van you are?"

Tomina smiled at him.

"Yes—my first day. What are your wares?"

"Books, nothing but books; food for the soul and spirit."

"I'm fond of books. Any new novels?"

"There's a book I have which is so interesting, so magnetic, that the
more people read it, the more they want to. And though it was written a
few thousands of years ago, it's as fresh as spring, and keeps its hold
on people's hearts."

"Some of the old classics, I suppose?"

The little man dived into his pack and put a book quietly upon Tomina's
knees.

"Have you ever read that book through, from cover to cover?" he asked.

Tomina looked at it, then handed it to him back, with a little grimace.

"The Bible. Well, we hear it in church, don't we? I had one of my own
once."

"Have you ever read it through, miss?"

"Not that I know of. I used to learn chapters and psalms at school. I
believe I was rather good at Scripture history when I was small."

"Will you take this copy?"

"No, thank you. I'm not at all a good sort of person, and don't want to
be one. I never could see anything in religion myself, and hate people
who are what I call weekday parsons."

"It's funny—" began the colporteur, and then he stopped.

"Well?"

Tomina glanced curiously at him.

He was getting restive. Suddenly, he blazed out—

"People talk of there being no devil! It's sheer proof of his presence
and handiwork, when young gentle things like you scoff at the One Who
made them, and sacrificed Himself for them and loves them now with a
deep undying love!"

Tomina looked startled. She chirruped to her horse and with her whip
flipped some of the early green of the hedges.

"Good people want the young to give up all their pleasures. The God I
have heard about is not a God of love, unless you give yourself up to
good works and say good-bye to all gaiety and fun."

There was silence. Then the colporteur pointed to a meadow full already
of golden shining buttercups.

"That's gay enough," he said, "and good to look at. They've grown up
in the sunshine and are reflections of the sun himself. Not much gloom
about them, eh?"

Tomina followed his gaze.

"I've no patience with the gloomy mood," he said quickly. "God made a
good world and a gay one, and the happy folk are the ones who reflect
His face."

"You look happy."

"I am—I have so much to make me. And you?"

Tomina laughed lightly.

"I have been happy on and off, but I'm tired of trying to be when I'm
not. I've lost the taste for so many things, and feel as old as the
hills. I have been sent into the country to bury myself, but with the
purpose of making me pop out of my grave again quite fresh and new."

"A new birth," said the colporteur, gravely.

"That's very interesting!"

"Something of that sort," said Tomina, lightly.

She could not explain to herself why she talked to this man. But she
felt he was not an aggressive individual, and his round smiling face
amused her.

"'Very' interesting," repeated the colporteur, softly. Then he pointed
upwards to some young green limes arching overhead. "There's new birth
for you! They had their gay time last summer. Then they died; now the
resurrection of spring has got hold of them. Do you know why they're
bursting into bud and blossom?"

"Why?"

"The sap is rising inside them. It begins from below, and rises and
flows into every bare dry branch and twig, till the barest of them is
bound to break and blossom. And you're needing to get the sap, the
source of life, into your empty soul and let the Water of Life flow
through you. You won't be scoffing at the good if you get it, you'll
be bursting with joy and happiness, and blossoming with fruit to come.
'The water that I shall give him, shall be in him a well of water,
springing up into everlasting life.'"

The sun shone through the young limes as Tomina looked up through their
flickering leaves. Then a diversion occurred. Midway across a field to
the right of them stood a low-thatched cottage in the midst of a garden
gay with spring bulbs. There was a little wooden gate in the hedge, and
at it stood two little ladies, one stout, one thin. They waved a packet
of books in the air as Tomina approached them. She pulled up. One of
them rather shyly approached her.

"Is the carrier here? We heard the van would pass us about this time
for Damerton, and we wondered if he would kindly change these books for
us at the library. Saunders the name is."

"I am the carrier," said Tomina, with dignity. "I'll do it with
pleasure, unless you'd like to have a lift into Damerton yourselves."

The ladies looked at each other. There was a longing in their eyes, but
dignity and conventionalism got the better of it.

"Thank you, no. We have never used the carrier's cart as a personal
conveyance. It has not been running for some years. When last it was,
we lived in the Rectory and had our own trap and horse. We have put a
list of books we should like to have. Mrs. Saunders knows us. And what
time will you be coming past here this evening?"

Tomina considered.

"About six o'clock," she said.

Then she drove on. Curiosity compelled her to glance at the books
delivered into her keeping. She was surprised to see that there was
a modern novel amongst them by a popular writer. A book on the late
excavations in Egypt was another, and Maeterlinck's "The Bee" the third.

"They don't rust then," she thought to herself.

There was no more serious conversation between herself and the
colporteur. She kept him at bay. When they eventually reached Damerton,
he jumped down at the entrance of the market and paid his fare.

"Good day to you, miss. I'm leaving this part of the country this week,
but I won't forget you."

He lifted his hat and departed.

Tomina was too busy to give him a thought again. She drove to the
"Golden Bell" and put up her van. Her passengers scattered. She told
them she was going to start home punctually at five o'clock. Then she
got hold of one of the capable women, who good-naturedly offered to get
some of the most difficult things on her list.

All the morning she was busy shopping, then she had a comfortable lunch
at the "Golden Bell," and afterwards sauntered about the market and
town, enjoying herself in a leisurely fashion. She went to the library
and got some new books for herself, as well as for the Miss Seatons.
And as she was coming out of the shop, she met Miss Merryweather, who
shook hands warmly with her.

"May I congratulate you on your venture? It is the talk of the
countryside. Are you taking root here, otherwise, isn't it a pity to
start something that will have to be dropped?"

"Why should it be dropped? I live in the present, and I never look
beyond it. Did you drive in?"

"Yes, with my brother. He comes into Damerton every week, you know."

"I suppose he does. I see you're looking at my books. You won't approve
of them, but I've got to let my mind laze down here, so can read
nothing solid. And I hope you're doing the same."

Alice Merryweather smiled.

"I feel as if I can read nothing at all at present. But I'm going to
order some books for my brother."

"Is he a reader?"

"He used to be."

A shadow fell upon her face; she turned quickly away, and Tomina went
out wondering what her choice of books would be.

At five o'clock she was in the yard. She found her van crammed with
parcels of all sorts and sizes. Two of the women were sitting inside
chattering gaily.

Four extra passengers were waiting to be taken up and dropped on the
way. One old man was loquacious with drink. Tomina eyed him severely
and insisted upon him sitting at the further end of the van.

"'Tis Jack amongst the maidens I'll be!" he laughed, as he took his
seat. "Crowd in, me dears, an' the last maiden, if so be the van is
full up, shall sit on Jack's knee. Don't 'ee all try to be the las' one
now!"

Tomina ignored his pleasantries, but the women tittered and laughed and
thoroughly enjoyed them.

Eleven passengers inside was a tight fit. The lad, Bill Short, sat on
the outside seat with Tomina. She began to talk to him and was quite
interested in what he told her about himself.

His father had died four months ago. He was only fifteen, but he was
running a small poultry farm with his mother.

"I mean to be a farmer, and shan't stop at the bottom of the ladder.
I'm saving and putting by every year, and I look to rent one of our
Squire's farms in another two years."

"Is he a good landlord?"

"Oh yes—not bad! Sir Alfred's a fair man if he be a bit sharp at times.
He's a rare good hand at business. Knows a good deal more than his
agent."

When they reached the Miss Seatons' cottage, he volunteered to jump out
and take their books up to their door, as there was no sign of them.

"We are a little early," Tomina remarked. "It is difficult to tell the
first day how long we take from point to point."

"You ought to have a little chap with you, miss, who can carry parcels
in and out. I reckon your custom will increase, and there'll be a good
bit of parcel-carrying."

"Yes," said Tomina, thoughtfully, "I think I must."

Bill Short got out halfway to Little Honeycombe. He was going to spend
the evening with an uncle of his.

And Tomina found that several of her parcels which had to be delivered
on the way necessitated her getting out of the cart herself and
delivering them at the doors.

"I shall have a whistle next time," she told the people. "I can't leave
my horse to knock at your doors. You must be on the look-out for me."

The last parcel she personally delivered was left at a farm at the top
of a steep hill. It was a hamper with live fowls in, and she had to
call the man out to take it in. He took his time over it, and as Tomina
was giving him some change, she heard a shout and commotion. Looking
round, she saw that the old man had clambered from his back seat in the
van to the front one, and was belabouring the horse with the whip.

Bran started downhill at a clumsy gallop. Tomina held her breath as she
saw the van swaying to and fro, and realised that no brake was on. Then
she raced downhill after it, furiously angry at being treated in such a
manner and greatly alarmed about the safety of all concerned. Suddenly
from a bend in the road, a rider on a big grey horse confronted her.

She stopped with raised hand. Then, as she looked up into his face, the
rich colour left her cheeks and lip.

"Tomina! I-er-mean-Miss Gavin!"

"Colonel Norman!"

Then she recovered herself, and said with reckless haste—

"I believe I would welcome the devil himself at this juncture! Ride
back and stop that runaway van! Did you not meet it? Stop it, and keep
it waiting till I overtake it."

The rider looked at her sternly.

He was a tall broad-shouldered man with clean-cut features, a square
and determined mouth and chin, and very thick and bushy eyebrows which
almost hid his eyes and gave a fierce expression to his face.

"Do you want a ride in a carrier's cart?"

"I do. The cart belongs to me. Don't waste another moment—that drunken
wretch will upset the whole concern."

He wheeled round on his horse with a muttered exclamation and cantered
down the hill.

Tomina heaved a sigh of relief and walked after him.

"I shall not hurry. They will have to wait for me. My cup of
humiliation is full to have to ask a favour from him! But it was
unavoidable. How can he be in this part of the world?"

When she eventually overtook the cart, it was standing still at the
bottom of the long hill. Women's heads were crowded together looking
out, and two figures were standing in the road. One was Colonel Norman.
His left hand was holding his horse by his bridle, the other was
clutching the offender by the coat collar, who was nervously crying—

"Only my joke—a little joke, sir. Us be so dull cooped up like pigs in
a pen! No harm done. Letsh me go, sir!"

"Now, then, what do you want done with this fellow?" the Colonel
demanded, turning to Tomina with a wrathful eye.

Tomina jumped lightly up to her seat and took the reins in her hands.

She ignored the women's gasps and cries, knowing that they were
thoroughly enjoying the situation. Then she spoke shortly and severely.

"Leave him where he is. Thank you for your help."

And she drove off without turning her head.

"Ah, my dear soul!" ejaculated old Mrs. Crabbe. "We have been in a
terrible fright. But poor old man! It do seem a cruel shame to strand
him four miles from home. He lives close to our village."

Tomina said nothing. The women went on talking amongst themselves. One
of them asked her if she was feeling all right.

"'Twas a great shame to treat a young lady so! And Will Carpenter do
deserve to be put down. But he were mischievously inclined. And I do
hope he b'ant offerin' violence to that stranger gent. What a good job
you had him to help you, miss! Whatever should us have done if us had a
galloped home without you!"

"I think six or seven women ought to have been able to restrain a
feeble old man the worse for drink," said Tomina, regally. "But it will
be a lesson to me in the future. I shall deliver no more parcels unless
the owners come to the cart for them."

The rest of the journey home was uneventful. But when Tomina walked
into her small sitting-room, Susan exclaimed at her looks—

"You never can stand such days, ma'am. You look proper fagged out."

"I'll go to bed," said Tomina. "I have nothing else to do but rest when
I'm home, thank goodness!"

Later on, when she was in bed and had had a comfortable little meal
brought up to her, she told Susan about her misadventure.

Susan was horrified.

"And was Thor no good, ma'am?"

"What could Thor do? He was with me when I left the cart. I only tell
you because I know how they talk in the village. It was a mercy they
weren't all upset."

Susan looked much concerned, but she wisely offered no consolation.
Tomina was too proud to accept such from any one.

She went to sleep that night slightly discomfited, and very determined
