that she would be stricter with her passengers and parcels in future.

CHAPTER V

A STRAY CHILD

TOMINA rested all the following day. In the afternoon, she was sitting
in her easy-chair in the back garden, when Susan came flying towards
her.

"A gentlemen has called, ma'am, and would like to see you. Here is his
card."

Tomina hardly glanced at it.

"I am not at home, Susan."

Susan looked astonished.

"I'm sorry, ma'am, but I asked him in and told him you were in the
garden."

"You had no business to do either. I am not going to see him."

Susan stood irresolute.

"Please, ma'am, what can I say?"

"I have told you what to say."

Tomina's tone was calmness itself. She took up her book and went on
reading, but the words danced before her eyes. Susan went back to the
house with slow unwilling steps.

And when she was out of sight, Tomina let her feelings get the better
of her.

"How dare he!"

Thor looked up and began to growl. He had been lying at her feet, but
it seemed as if he scented an unwelcome intruder.

"Quiet, Thor! He will see that I mean what I say. What has possessed
him, of all people, to come into this part of the country?"

Tomina was much perturbed, but nobody but Thor had the clue to her
thoughts.

Susan went about with an offended face. She was angry that she had
"been made a fool of," so she told Mary Anne when talking it over with
her.

"And such a handsome set-up gentleman, quite a stranger, and when I
went back to him with her message, I felt like a dog with my tail
between my legs.

"'Won't she see me?' he says.

"And I says, 'She's not well enough for company this afternoon, sir.'

"'Did you give her my card?' he says.

"And I says, 'Yes.'

"With that, he walks off like a haughty duke, and I do call it a shame
of her!"

Tomina was conscious of Susan's disapproval, but was only amused at it.


The next morning, she had another visitor, and this time it was Mrs.
Judd, who kept the general shop in the village.

"What does she want to see me for?" Tomina asked Susan.

She was having rather a late breakfast, it being ten o'clock, but she
had slept badly, and so did not hurry to get up.

"I think she'll prefer to tell you that herself, ma'am," said Susan, a
little grandly.

"Let her come in; perhaps she wants to be paid. We do owe her for some
sugar, don't we?"

Mrs. Judd was announced. She was a stout woman with very small
features, her eyes were almost hidden by her cheeks, and she now
appeared red and uncomfortable.

"Good morning, Mrs. Judd," said Tomina, graciously.

"Morning, ma'am. You'll excuse me; I'm a person who always acts prompt.
My dear departed father always brought me up so.

"'M'ria, if you knows a step to be right, take it, and take it quick.
If you dilly-dallies you be lost!'

"He used to say that times without number, and if 'tis a bit
disagreeable for you, ma'am, you'll face it and see the right is on my
side."

She paused.

Tomina looked up expectantly.

"Yes, what is the matter?"

"'Tisn't to be expected you gentry knows how the working folk toils
from morn to night, and gathers their pennies with the sweat of their
brow! I've built up my business with a load of care and trouble, and
just when I'm ridin' on the crest of the wave, so to speak, you comes
along and with thoughtlessness and carelessness, and with smiles to
everybody, you knocks it all down in the dust!"

"My dear woman, what do you mean? For goodness' sake don't beat about
so, but say out plainly what your grievance is. How can I knock down
your business?"

Mrs. Judd's portly bosom heaved; she put her apron up to her eyes and
began to cry.

"I see it a-coming. I've sugared Mrs. Tabbs and Mrs. Goss for these
eight years, and I hear tell they've bought seven pound each in
Damerton because they could get it cheaper. And I've teaed Mrs. Crabbe
for longer than they, and she hasn't come near me all yesterday, which
is her day for droppin' in for sixpennorth of tea and chat. And my
niece have told me all the groceries hidden under the seat so as she
shouldn't report it to me, and I do think it's most un-Christian and
unkind to drive my custom away from me in your van to Damerton every
week!"

Tomina looked blankly at her. At first, she nearly laughed, but Mrs.
Judd's tears and tragic face warned her that this was no laughing
matter.

"My dear soul, I'm most awfully sorry. I wouldn't hurt your custom for
the world! Now dry your tears, and sit down and let's talk it over.
Have a cup of coffee? It will pull you together!"

Mrs. Judd accepted the coffee. Tomina knew how to win hearts, and
before long, she had Mrs. Judd in a smiling mood again. She suggested
to her that she or her niece should get supplies from Damerton
themselves, instead of trusting to a traveller to buy for them.

"You will get everything far cheaper, and will see all kinds of
novelties that you may feel inclined to invest in. Then, if you can do
some things a little cheaper, your customers won't leave you. And I
promise to get all my supplies from you. We won't let you suffer. Why,
what would the village do without you! I think your shop is a marvel!
And it does one good to go in and see your smiling happy face. Oh, you
can be quite sure my van will help your trade, not hinder it."

So Tomina talked, and Mrs. Judd finally departed with the conviction
that the carrier's van was going to make her fortune.

Tomina was thoroughly discussed in the shop that same afternoon.

"She has manners," said Mrs. Goss. "'Twas quite parlour manners when us
got in.

"'Will you sit here, Mrs. Goss,' she says to me, 'so as to rest your
back here?'

"And she had a pretty word for every one. But where she lacks is in
strength. Us do want a man to heave of us up and our parcels. And when
all's said and done, 'tis more racy-like to have a man a-drivin,' and
not a bit of a girl!"

"But if us can't get a man, us must be thankful for her!" said Mrs.
Tabbs. "And when she gets hold of a horse, she knows right well how to
manage 'im!"

Then her family history was surmised, and her health and appearance
criticised, and the final verdict was that she "were makin' things a
bit livelier in the village, and liked to spend her money."


When Friday came round, Tomina started out again. This time she had
fewer passengers. Only five from Little Honeycombe. But she took up
four on the way, and Bill Short was amongst them. Tomina suddenly
evolved the idea of engaging him to come with her every time, for she
saw that she ought to have some one to lift the different baskets and
bundles in and out.

Bill responded gladly to her suggestion. She told him she would give
him two-and-sixpence a day and his food, and he said he would be
delighted to come with her for that.

When St. Martha's Cross was reached, Tomina was as interested as her
passengers in seeing the quaint little old-fashioned town. She called
at the railway station late in the afternoon and there found a small
boy sitting on a bundle waiting for her. A woman by his side was
voluble in her explanation.

"'Tis little 'Arry Chuggs. An orphan he be, no father or mother, and I
used to be nex' door neighbour to his granny. He lives with his granny.
She were taken ill and sent him to me, but I be called over to London
to see my darter. Her boy be two days old, and there be complications;
and she be very bad, so back to his granny he must go. And you pass her
house. 'Tis halfway to Little Honeycombe back from the road—Mrs. Chuggs
her be. I'm hopin' she be up and about agen by this time."

Tomina promised to deliver the small boy safely. Something in his tiny
face and mass of dark brown curls appealed to her. And when he insisted
on sitting up by her side and chatted to her in a most quaint and
friendly fashion, Tomina ended by losing her heart to him.

"Have you ever seed God?" he asked her, as they slowly drove up a
long hill. He was gazing up into the blue evening sky with a seraphic
innocence as he spoke.

"No," said Tomina.

"I have," he said, with a pleased nod. "He's always on top of us, you
know, and Satan is under. I seed God las' night when I was in bed and
'twas black with the dark. He was jus' hangin' over me, and He smiled."

"But how could you see Him in the dark?" questioned Tomina.

"Oh, some people see quite well in the dark," replied Harry, quickly.
"God and me sees quite as well as in the day. We look at each other
quite easy."

There was a pause. Tomina was unused to children. She did not know
whether she ought to reprove him or not. He gave her a side look
through his long black eyelashes, and continued—

"Satan is very angry when I see God. He tries to get in the way, and
then I stamp on him hard, and he crackles up like coal does!"

"What will granny say when she sees you? Does she know that you're
coming?" asked Tomina, thinking it best to change the conversation.

Harry snuggled closer to her, and laid his head against her arm.

"I do love you!" he said. "I wis' granny lived with you."

Then his small hand stretched out for the reins, and Tomina weakly gave
them into his hand.

In an instant he was sitting upright, his big eyes blazing with pride
and joy.

"I could drive you to Lunnon," he asserted. "He's my horse, and this is
my cart, and I'm going to sell you all to the market."

"Who would buy us?" asked Tomina, enjoying this child as she hadn't
enjoyed anything for a long time.

"I'll sell you for a shilling each to a farmer to fatten for Chris'mas;
he'll keep you in a sty, but not you. I'll whisper in your ear. Bend
down."

So Tomina bent.

"I'll buy you back and take you home with me, and to-morrow we'll go
and look over the edge of the world."

His talk so fascinated Tomina that she was quite sorry when his
grandmother's was reached.

She got down herself when they arrived at the cottage, and took him up
to the door. A woman opened it immediately, and exclaimed when she saw
Harry—

"Why, his granny died this morning! Poor little chap! 'Tis no use for
him to come here. Me and my darter have been seeing to the poor soul.
She were took sudden at the end. Was quite herself yesterday, and then
when the doctor left yesternight, went unconscious like and died at
five o'clock this morn. Would you like to come in and look at her,
miss? She's beautiful to see!"

But Tomina shrank back in horror.

"I wouldn't come in for worlds!" she said frankly. "But now, what's to
be done with this poor child?"

"He'd best go back where he came from," said the woman, shortly.

She was hurt and offended at Tomina's refusal to see the dead, and
said within herself that she was not going to be bothered with other
people's children.

For an instant, Tomina stood still considering. Then she seized Harry
by the hand, and took him back to the van.

"You shall come home with me, Harry, to-night. I'll find you a bed. You
amuse me."

When the inmates of the van heard of what had happened, they were loud
in talk and lamentation. One of them claimed an acquaintance with
Harry's grandmother.

"She was a good woman, and a favourite with Mrs. Francis, and I've
heard tell that the little lad were often up at Hall. Mrs. Francis took
a likin' to him. Pity she be still away!"

"Well," said Tomina, "if you hear of any one who can offer him a home,
let me know. Till then, he stays with me."


When Tomina finally reached home, and placed Harry in Susan's arms,
that good woman was almost stunned.

"A child, ma'am! Well, you do really beat all! I always did feel you
were never cut out for a carrier!"

"He'll have to stay till we get him a home," said Tomina. "Give him
some supper and make him up a bed somewhere, Susan. He's getting sleepy
now, but he's the most entertaining little creature I've ever met."

Later on, when Tomina was enjoying her own dinner, Susan came to her.

"I foresee, ma'am, we shall have a handful. He's sitting on the floor
crying for you. He's ready for bed, but won't get in."

Tomina went into Susan's room where Harry was to sleep. He looked a
disconsolate little figure in his scanty shirt as he sat cross-legged
on the floor. Seeing Tomina, he held out his arms. And Tomina gathered
him to herself with almost a mother's tenderness.

"What is it, lambkin?"

He pointed his small finger at Susan accusingly, whilst his under lip
still quivered, and big teardrops hung on his eyelashes.

"Her says 'tis no use to say 'God bless granny,' 'cause granny is dead.
I can't go to sleep till I've said my prayers, and my prayers is 'God
bless granny.' Where is granny? Is she where God can't bless her?"

Tomina was ignorant of religious doctrines and orthodoxy.

"Of course you can say 'God bless granny.' Come and say it now."

So Harry dropped on his knees with a smile through his tears.

 "O God, bless granny and make Harry a good boy, and—and love him when
he's naughty, for Jesus Christ's sake. Amen."

Then he got up and stared solemnly at Tomina with his big eyes.

"Aunt Jane said God didn't love naughty boys, but He will if I ask Him,
won't he?"

"I expect there would be very few in the world left to love if He did
not," said Tomina gravely.

Then she lifted him into the little bed made up for him and kissed him.

And when she was in her sitting-room alone, the colporteur's words
suddenly came back to her.

"People talk of there being no devil! It's sheer proof of his presence
and handiwork when young gentle things like you scoff at the One Who
made them, and sacrificed Himself for them, and loves them now with a
deep undying love!"

"I suppose God loves us all," she mused. "I've never thought much about
it. I should like to meet that Bible pedlar again, he seemed so happy
and certain. And I liked that about the sap rising. I suppose if one
could be religious as he was, it mightn't be such a gloomy affair after
all."

She thought about it when she went to bed, and when she at last slept,
she had a strange dream. She dreamed that Harry and she were walking
hand-in-hand through a strange country, and they came to a deep blue
lake. On the other side were lights and people, but darkness seemed
gathering round them, and Tomina heard herself saying anxiously—

"We must get home, Harry! We must get home!"

And then Harry pulled her down to the water.

"Let's come through it quick, and then we'll get home."

But when her feet touched the cold water, she shrank back and shivered.

"I can't, Harry, I can't. We shall be drowned. We must wait for a boat."

But Harry pulled her forwards.

"It isn't cold water, it's full of God's love."

And when she hesitated, he went into the lake, and she watched his
laughing face and curly head bobbing up above the water and calling to
her to follow him.

"It's quite easy—come!" he called.

She looked round for a boat, but there was none, the darkness of a
coming storm was behind her and around her. She tried to move after the
child, but her feet stuck to the ground, and then she called out in
terror, and awoke.

Beads of perspiration were on her brow. It was early morning. Birds
were singing outside her window.

"Oh!" she gasped. "What a comfort it is to wake from a frightful dream!"

Then she turned again on her pillow, and slept till Susan called her.


At breakfast, when Harry sat opposite to her, her dream recurred to her.

"Do you ever have dreams, Harry?" she asked.

Harry was in a very matter of fact mood this morning.

"Some more sugar in my tea, please," he asked, "and may I go in the
garden with the big dog?"

"If you aren't interesting, I shan't keep you," said Tomina, rather
severely.

Harry opened his eyes.

"What's intellessing?"

"I want you to amuse me."

Harry shook his head.

"I want 'you' to 'muse 'me,'" he said.

Tomina gave him up for the time with an impatient sigh.


But after breakfast, when he had had a scamper in the garden with Thor,
he came back to her with a most coaxing face.

"Tell me a story like Mrs. Francis does."

"What kind of stories does she tell you?"

Harry gazed up into the sky with rapturous eyes.

"'Bout the Home for little children in the blue sky."

"I'm afraid I don't know much about it," said Tomina. "You tell me what
you know."

"It's where granny has gone, isn't it? Jesus stands at the door to
shake hands, you know—"

Harry's eyes grew dreamy.

"When I take His hand, I shall cry because of the hole in it. It's a
pity He let them be so cruel to Him, wasn't it? But Mrs. Francis said
it had to be. Can you sing that hymn about a green hill?"

"I think I used to once, but I've forgotten the words."

"The story I like best is David and Goliar. Do you know that? Tell it
to me."

He had twined his small fingers round hers and was leaning against
her knee. Tomina felt almost mesmerised. She began at once, in rather
halting words, to tell the old Bible story, but Harry knew it better
than she did, and prompted her every minute.

She was sitting by the open window, when suddenly a voice interrupted
them.

"Good morning. May I come in?"

It was Dr. Merton.

CHAPTER VI

AN UNWELCOME PASSENGER

TOMINA looked rather relieved than otherwise at the interruption. Harry
dashed to the door with a shout of welcome.

"I'm living with her now. Isn't she a pretty lady?"

"You're in luck's way, young man. Do you think you could make yourself
scarce for a little?"

"Harry, run out into the kitchen and stay with Susan till I call you,"
Tomina said.

The child obeyed her.

Then Dr. Merton took a seat.

"Well, this isn't a professional visit to-day. I heard last night of
your kind deed, and have come to ask what you mean to do?"

Tomina sat back in her chair and looked at him with laughing eyes.

"I really haven't the faintest idea what I mean to do. I live in
the present, as you know. Please don't credit me with being a
philanthropist, for it was just an impulse. He is an adorably pretty
boy! And he began to talk so confidentially and quaintly to me that I
thought he would amuse me for a day or two. What queer things children
are! He seems so deeply, powerfully religious, if you know what I mean.
I hear he is Mrs. Francis's 'protégé.' I suppose that accounts for it."

"I think it does. Eleanor Francis has a wonderful gift of influence,
and a little child is so susceptible."

"He hardly seems like a villager's child. Has he any special history?"

"Yes. His grandmother, Mrs. Chugg, was once Mrs. Francis's nurse. She
married the butler—a most superior man, and for some time kept one of
the lodges on the Digbys' property. Her daughter was a very pretty,
refined girl, and was very often at the Hall playing with the children
there. She married very well, a rising young architect who did a good
bit of business for the old squire. They went to London, and in three
years' time, both died of scarlet fever, a virulent type—he took it
first and she contracted it nursing him. Their little boy came back to
live with his grandmother. That's his history.

"I don't think we shall let him go to the workhouse. If you are tired
of him already, you had better hand him over to me. I am quite ready to
give him a home for the present. Harry and I are old friends."

"I haven't said that I am tired of him. You are too fond of jumping to
conclusions."

"Children want an infinite amount of patience and care," said Dr.
Merton, looking at Tomina gravely; "are you very fond of them?"

"Not as a class, but if they are very pretty and amusing, I lose my
heart to them. And Harry has a way with him. Oh, I dare say I had
better hand him over to you. I fancy I might not be able to satisfy his
requirements. I certainly am behind him in Biblical knowledge. I don't
even possess a Bible of my own. I have one in town, I believe, but I
never used it."

Tomina always had a perverse longing to shock Dr. Merton, but he never
rose to her bait.

"You're improving in looks," he said. "Our country life suits you. How
does the van work?"

Tomina's eyes sparkled. She spoke naturally now.

"I am getting to love it. The slow leisurely motion suits me. I think I
have adapted myself very quickly to my—my banishment! I never thought
that country people of the lower orders, could be so interesting.
They're pathetic, and comic and tragic by turns. I listen to their talk
and am learning a lot."

"I hear the carrier's van is a great success," said Dr. Merton,
heartily.

"Well, we have only had two days of it as yet. I've had some funny
experiences. The first day I took up a book pedlar, who preached me a
sermon. He was rather a dear. I fancy I have tumbled into a nest of
good people down here. When are you going to have your turn with me,
Dr. Merton? I am sure you are longing to begin!"

"I want you to be healthy both in body and soul," responded Dr. Merton,
gravely. "You will tell me your soul is none of my business, but I say
that the soul affects the body, and as you have been trying to kill
both, these few years, I am naturally anxious that you should learn the
laws of health for both."

Tomina's eyes twinkled with mischief. She folded her hands in her lap.

"Please go on," she said.

"Oh, I am not going to preach. But I do think that every civilised
Christian should own and read the Book that contains those laws."

"I suppose you mean the Bible, but I don't think that says much about
our bodies, does it?"

"Doesn't it? You look and see. We have wandered from our point. How
long do you want to keep Harry?"

Tomina did not reply. She was gazing out of the window somewhat
wistfully.

The doctor longed to know her thoughts, but he waited in silence.

At last, she said—

"Do you really want him so much? I believe you are like me, though, of
course, you wouldn't allow it for worlds! You have your lonely moments,
when you get bored to death with yourself and your thoughts, and you
seek distraction!"

Dr. Merton smiled.

"I hope the quiet simple life is not going to fail you."

"If it keeps quiet and simple it won't," said Tomina, emphatically.
"But if fragments of the other world keep flying across my path, I
shall, as the Americans would say, 'quit'!"

Then she smiled, and Tomina's smile made every one feel at once what a
joyous thing life was.

"I came across rather a big fragment the other day," she continued,
"but it went as quickly as it came, and as I'm determined to live in
the present, I oughtn't to give it a thought."

Dr. Merton opened his mouth as if about to speak, then shut his lips
determinedly.

"And Harry?"

"Oh, Harry. Leave him with me a little longer. Are you going to adopt
him?"

"I have not thought of doing so; only I thought I would keep him till
Mrs. Francis returns, and then we can talk over his future together."

"Poor little waif! Has he no relations on his father's side?"

"I am afraid not. But that will require investigation. Mrs. Francis
knows about his belongings better than I do. I must be off. I am
thankful to leave him in such good hands as yours, and if he proves too
much for you, let me know at once."

Just as the doctor was going out at the door, he turned.

"By the way, somebody was asking after you the other day. A Colonel
Norman, whose father has recently died. His mother lives about five
miles off. I believe he is retiring from the service and going to look
after the property."

"I used to know a Major Norman," said Tomina, indifferently, "but he is
not a friend of mine."

The doctor went.

Tomina sat very still. An impertinent robin perched upon the
window-sill and lifting up his head, looked at her saucily. Then there
was a rush of small feet along the passage and he flew away.

"Susan is cooking me a fruit tart. She says I can pick up the snails in
the ivy, and Thor could be made to draw your wheelbarrow, couldn't he?
Oh! There's such lots to do! Do come on and help me."

Harry had got hold of her, and had dragged her out into the garden
before she had time to protest.

For the rest of that day, Tomina gave herself over to childhood and to
childhood's games.

When she was tired, she handed Harry over to Susan, who soon became
his willing slave. He certainly kept them both lively, and however
mischievous and restless by day, he was a little angel when bed-time
came. Tomina was obliged to be always present during his prayers. She
said to Susan one evening, when she came away from him—

"Do all children consider that the Almighty's sole occupation consists
in watching over them and listening to every word that comes from their
lips? Harry thinks that his concerns come first and foremost in the
world to his Creator!"

"Ah well," said Susan, "'tis said, ma'am, 'of such is the Kingdom of
Heaven.' They were very dear to our Lord, and 'twould be better for all
of us if we were more like them!"

"I suppose it would," was Tomina's grave reply.


Carrier's day came round again. But it dawned in lashing showers of
rain, which set in for a steady downpour when the time to start came.

Tomina had thought of taking Harry with her, but that was impossible
now. She arrayed herself in her oilskins, and as she surveyed herself
in her glass, she laughed aloud.

"I'm looking a genuine carrier now," she said to Susan.

"Well, ma'am, you're really very like a man in them things. But 'tis
rough weather for you, all said and done!"

She had only four passengers to start with. Bill Short was, of course,
in attendance, and she made up for the lack of passengers in the
hampers and parcels which were packed in.

Rain and wind beat in their faces as they started. The whole country
was obliterated by a grey driving mist. Tomina sat well back under the
awning and drove. The four women snuggled together in the back of the
van, and talked in low voices over the shortcomings of their families
and the depression of trade. Everybody seemed out of spirits, including
Bran, who plodded doggedly along, occasionally shaking his great mane
when it became water-logged, and Thor, who tucked himself under the
shelter of the van and held his tail well between his legs.

Presently, as they began to ascend a long steep hill, they overtook
a pedestrian in mackintosh and slouch hat, well pulled down over his
eyes. He hailed them as soon as he saw them, and climbed up into the
front seat by the side of Tomina.

"A bad day for you," he said pleasantly.

Tomina turned in her seat and stared at him. There was no mistaking
that voice. It was Colonel Norman again.

For an instant, Tomina wondered if she were recognised, but only for
an instant. Then a swift furious colour rose in her cheeks, as she
encountered his keen steady gaze.

"Why wouldn't you see me the other day? You might have known we should
be bound to meet before long."

"I call this an impertinent intrusion," said Tomina, in icy tones.

"Surely not? Surely a carrier's van is open to any and all who can pay
his fare? I am going to Damerton on business."

"Then you could have driven yourself."

"But I wanted to see you and have a talk with you, and this was my
opportunity."

His tone was very determined.

Tomina was so dumfounded by the blow fate had dealt her that she was
absolutely silent.

He continued after a pause.

"I heard of your disappearance from town, but I little thought I
should find you in my own county. Is there any special reason for
this masquerade? When I remember you in the old days, I can hardly
reconcile—"

Then Tomina turned her head.

"The old days, as you call them, are dead and buried, and so is every
one and everything associated with them."

"Do you really mean that?"

"I do."

"Thank Heaven! That leaves me a fair fresh field! Tomina, I am going
to begin to court you again. I won't even attempt to explain away or
apologise for the past. There is no past. You and I are in a new fresh
world, the only man and woman in it so far as I am concerned. I felt an
hour ago that the very heavens were weeping for our spoiled lives, and
irrevocable mistakes, our pitiful failures of life with its glorious
possibilities.

"Now what is the rain? It is washing and purging and beautifying all
that has become besmirched with the dust of time. You and I are new
creatures. We have evolved a fresh era, our future lies before us, to
be made or marred according to our likings. We are at the beginning of
our friendship."

Tomina received this burst of eloquence with a very slight and
indifferent shrug of her pretty shoulders. Her back was almost
turned upon him. Only Bran was conscious of an unusual and spasmodic
tightening of his reins.

After a moment's silence, she spoke.

"You have twisted my words to suit yourself. If neither of us has a
past, we are absolute strangers one to the other, and I intend that we
remain as such. I never wish to know you, and no amount of effort on
your part will make me do it. As a gentleman, and I believe you are
one, you cannot force your presence upon me!"

"I can and will do anything to break down the barrier between us!"

There was a tense silence, then the Colonel spoke again.

"No, I am not going to play your game any more. Neither of us can
forget the past; you think that you cannot forgive it. How long ago is
it? It is eight years to-day since your head was lying on my shoulder,
and we sealed our compact with our lips. I have never had but one woman
in my life, and I never shall. I was hot-headed and jealous, I own.
I was arbitrary where I ought to have been persuasive. You overheard
something that you misconstrued, and I was too proud to exonerate
myself. We both determined to put each other out of our lives.

"I've worked hard and allowed myself no time for thinking. Now I have
retired, and isn't it a remarkable thing that every day since I have
been home, your personality has haunted me? I never knew until I met
you the other day that we were neighbours. Tomina, my heart still beats
true for you; it has never wandered from you. Why should we pretend to
be strangers? You know that we are not."

Tomina's face was stern and set. It was the woman who showed hardness
and determination; the man's face was softened, and into his eyes had
crept a pitying tenderness.

The top of the hill was reached. Without a word, she whipped up Bran
more severely than was her wont. He broke into a sharp trot. The van
swayed and creaked and groaned, and one could hardly hear oneself
speak. The rain still lashed into their faces. Tomina began to wish for
some fresh passengers. How could she endure six or seven miles more of
Colonel Norman's company?

When they were once more forced to go slowly, she spoke—

"I came down to this part by doctor's orders. I have been very unwell,
and I thought I had found peace and rest, and freedom from all that I
have learnt to dislike. I do not intend that you shall spoil my life
again. You did it once, and a 'burnt child dreads the fire.' I never
will speak to you willingly again. Nothing that you can say or do will
make me change my mind."

Then scorn and anger crept into her voice.

"You acknowledge that all these years you have never given me a
thought! Why should you do so now? Your world always came first with
you. It is only in your idle moments that you consider women to be
useful to you. You forget that women now are not mere playthings for
men."

"You were never a plaything in my eyes."

"No, perhaps I used the wrong word. It was on the subject of play
that we differed so. You considered you had the right to withhold
from me any pleasure that you deemed fit to condemn. Your will was
always to come paramount. I was to be your obedient servant. And your
straight-laced Pharisaical views of life were to be mine, you reminded
me, when I became your wife. Thank Heaven, I escaped from such bondage.
Did you think that my grudge against you was because you told your chum
that the only chance for an undisciplined girl was for her to marry a
man who could always master her?"

"That speech of mine was not in reference to you. I never had a chance
to explain. Davis was working as a curate in the slums; we were talking
about factory girls and their ways."

Tomina laughed scornfully.

"What does it matter now? I say that that was only an indication of
your habit of thought. I was a young joyous-hearted girl in those
days. I felt kindly disposed towards all my fellow-creatures. I could
have been led, but never, never driven! You turned my gladness into
bitterness. You made me hate all goodness and good people and religion
in every shape and form. If I had cared enough, you would have driven
me to the devil. You took my love, but you only wanted it to minister
to your needs. I was to be your bird in a cage to sing to you, but
my wings were to be clipped, and you would have gone on clipping and
clipping till no feather was left!"

Tomina paused for breath. Her voice was low pitched; not one of the
women in the van could hear what she said, but passion vibrated through
every word.

Colonel Norman looked straight in front of him, and again there was a
tense silence between them. Then he drew a long deep breath.

"I never knew a woman's love could turn into such hate!" he said. And
clapping down a shilling on the wooden seat, he said curtly, "My fare,"
and swung himself down upon the muddy road. He raised his hat, and fell
behind the van.

Tomina never looked out or noticed whether he trudged behind them for
long. She only felt when she reached Damerton that she would give all
she possessed to be able to creep into her bed at home and cry herself
to sleep, so tired and weary, so utterly miserable was she!

But business had to be done, and her day had only just commenced. She
had always had plenty of self-control, and it did not fail her now.

In the afternoon, the weather cleared. The return journey was made in
sunshine, and every bush and tree seemed to lift up their heads to
greet it.

"Jus' like our days," said old Mrs. Crabbe, who was somewhat of a
philosopher; "we gets a reg'lar downpour o' trouble and set-backs, and
then it all goes, and the sun comes out beter'n ever."

"Will the sun ever come out for me?" thought Tomina. "I am sure it will
not."

Susan was not surprised when the next day came and found Tomina in bed
with a racking head and high temperature. Even Harry's prattle had to
be hushed.

"I always said she wouldn't be fit to stand rough weather," said Susan
to herself. "She's got a chill and is going to be really ill. I shall
send for the doctor, and he'll have to take away the child."

CHAPTER VII

TWO VISITORS

BUT in a day or two, Tomina was quite herself again, and Harry stayed
on. Everything that was softest and best in her came to the surface
when she had him by her side. Miss Merryweather called one afternoon,
and found her seated on the floor with a pack of cards. Harry and she
were erecting towers, to be demolished with a pop-gun as soon as they
were erected.

Tomina rose from the floor with reluctance.

"I have always enjoyed a game of cards, but I think this is the most
engrossing one I have ever played," she said. "Now, Harry, I am going
to rest for a little. Will you run out into the garden and tell Thor,
with my love, that he must take his turn at amusing you now?"

"And he'll shake his head and say, 'No.' Susan says he likes an
afternoon nap. But I'll tell him what you say, and if he doesn't do it,
I'll—I'll make him!"

He trotted away with flying curls; and Miss Merryweather looked her
astonishment.

"I have heard about your 'protégé.' You are rather a marvel to me!"

"Am I? I think you would have acted in the same way had you been in my
shoes."

Alice Merryweather shook her head.

"No, I should know I could not make him happy. You told me you had come
down here to get away from your fellow-creatures, but on all sides I
hear of the friends you are making. Allow me to congratulate you upon
your enterprise. You are turning into a successful philanthropist."

"You mean my van? My dear Miss Merryweather, that is pure amusement and
delight to me, and so is Harry. I have never done anything yet that I
dislike doing."

"But did I not tell you when you talked about my work that I found work
a pleasure. All people do who throw their hearts into it. You have
turned into the busy bee down here, I into the drone."

Tomina looked at her keenly.

"You are really ill," she said.

"I'm never without a headache," confessed Alice; "and my very limbs
seem powerless. You don't know what a helpless wretched creature I have
become. The only solace to me is my books, and those are a forbidden
indulgence."

"Forbidden by our tyrant the doctor, I suppose? Oh, I would snap my
fingers at him! Doesn't he interfere in everybody's concerns? Even
Harry here may be at any moment snatched away by him. He wants him now,
but I want him too, so our wills are in conflict."

Alice laughed.

"I think your will will come uppermost," she said. "Dr. Merton is very
kind at heart. Tell me about your carrier's cart. I feel inclined to
come with you one day."

"Do; it is most entertaining! Do you know two old ladies called Seaton?
I had such a quaint old-fashioned note from one of them this morning.

 "'The Misses Seaton will be much obliged if Miss Gavin will kindly
reserve two seats together for them in her van next Friday when she
goes to Damerton. They are obliged to go into Damerton on business,
otherwise they would not trouble her, and will she kindly see that the
two seats are under cover, and out of the draught. If very wet, they
will not attempt the journey; but if fine, D.V., * they would like her
to stop at their gate as she passes.'

"They are such old dears! But they told me, with heads in the air, that
they had never used a carrier's cart for their personal convenience."

 * D.V.—Deo Volente ["God willing."]

"Oh, I know them well," said Alice, smiling; "they are awfully afraid
of letting themselves slip down from the station in life to which they
were called, and most of their conversations are sandwiched with, 'Our
late father, the Rector of Twistham,' or, 'When we lived in Twistham
Rectory.' You cannot get them off it.

"I asked them once if they would like the post of librarians in a
country town not far from London. They are fond of books, and are
well-read women. I could have got it for them, and as they're so very
badly off, I thought they would have jumped at it, but they were quite
shocked at the idea. They are very early Victorian, and think women
ought never to work out of their homes."

"I don't like the type of working women I have met in town," said
Tomina, "they're so sure of themselves, and so argumentative and
dictatorial!"

"What class of woman have you met?"

"A lady doctor or two, and some Poor Law Guardians, and an American
barrister; she was the best of the lot, she knew how to dress herself!"

Alice laughed.

And Tomina joined her, adding—

"I don't care; women have no business to make themselves into seedy
caricatures of men. They generally despise them; why should they ape
their dress and manners?"

"You are talking of the aggressive style of woman. The hardest workers
are not that sort."

"Don't let us talk of women," said Tomina. "I'm not very fond of my
own sex. Men are, as a rule, bigger-minded and more interesting. I see
rather a scornful glint in your eye? The person I like best in the
world is a man who is now in Australia."

"I suppose you are engaged to him?"

"Indeed I am not, nor shall I ever be. He is my twin brother, and his
name is Tom. I have to thank him for my ridiculous name. He is the
elder by an hour, and he had to be a Thomas. His godfather insisted on
it. And then they named me Tomina. We grew up together, and I don't
think I could have had a nicer brother. If I could, I would follow him
round the world; but he is in the Navy, and they won't have women on
men-of-war."

"I should think not, indeed," said Alice, laughing. "I am afraid you
won't have much men's society here. You will soon be wanting to go back
to town."

"No, I feel as if I never wish to see it again. You forget that I have
entered another life—or world is it? And I am getting keenly interested
in the villagers. They are quite a new experience to me. You should
hear the conversations in my van! They're as good, and better than a
play. Five women were made to thrill last Friday by the description of
a neighbour who went to church in her weekday clothes! And they're so
good and generous to each other, much more so than we are! Oh, I'm in
love with my burial place. I shall never want to leave it."

"I came to ask you if you would do a commission for me when next you go
to Damerton. Will you bring me back a parcel of books, and let me call
for them here the next day?"

"With pleasure. Oh! Who is this? Not a visitor?"

A carriage was stopping at the gate. Alice Merryweather looked out.

"Why, it is Mrs. Norman! It is not often she comes this way."

"Oh dear! I don't want visitors. I'm not at home! That wretch Susan is
already at the door. Do go out and say I can't see her!"

Alice laughed.

"My dear Miss Gavin, you had better go through it with a good grace.
Mrs. Norman would have forced her way in, for she saw us at the window.
Nothing escapes her eyes. I am sorry for that nice son of hers who
has come home to look after her. She leads him a dreadful life. She
is one of those people who can never keep still; her energy is almost
superhuman."

The next moment, Susan ushered in a very animated little lady dressed
in the height of fashion.

She greeted Tomina almost affectionately.

"We cannot be strangers, though I have not been in town for years. We
were abroad for five years before my husband died, but your stepmother
and I are old friends. I am so pleased that you have come to live in
this part of the world. Why did you not let me know before? You are
down here for health, are you not? Leading the simple life, and so on.

"Well, we have all had our turn at that. Before we went abroad, I
got my husband to rent a small cottage for week-ends, and I tried
my best to adapt myself to it. But mice and earwigs conquered me,
and I fled. As I say, our life at Yockley Court is a simple one, and
for the present I am tied there. Between ourselves, our agent was
a gentlemanly loafer, and has let the whole place go to ruin, and
through mismanagement—I won't hint at any possible want of honesty—a
few thousands of ours have disappeared, and we have to economise. This
is what brought Gerald home. He has come to look after his mother, and
bring order out of chaos. You know my son, Miss Gavin, do you not?"

Tomina's face during this long speech was a study. Alice sat still, and
quite enjoyed it.

Then Tomina spoke, with her head thrown back, her eyes partly closed,
and her voice very sweet, but very remote in tone.

"It is kind of you to come to see me, but I am not to have visitors, my
doctor says. I am to enjoy absolute quiet. You see I am doing a rest
cure. I have shed my fashionable friends with my fashionable clothes,
and hours, like a skin! You will understand that as an invalid, I
cannot return this kind visit. I want to regain my health, and so I am
determined to follow my doctor's orders."

"My dear girl, I am indeed sorry for you! I did not know you were in
such delicate health, but I see Miss Merryweather has discovered you."

"Yes," said Alice, "our doctor introduced us. We are supposed to be
two invalids, and he thought we might like to discuss our respective
symptoms."

"I am so thankful for my superb health," said Mrs. Norman, with pride.
"I never have had a day's illness for years. I am up every morning at
half-past five, and in my garden at seven. I have just started a water
garden at the end of my rock garden, and it is most fascinating. Do you
know any rare water plants, Miss Merryweather? I know you are a very
encyclopædia for knowledge of all kinds."

Alice began telling her of a few plants by name, and it was Tomina's
turn to sit silent and listen. She could not reconcile this youthful
energetic mother with her stern determined son. She wondered how they
got on together, whether the son or mother ruled the house.

And in a very few minutes, Mrs. Norman rose to go.

"Well, I am glad to have found you at home, my dear, and I will send
the carriage over to bring you to lunch with us one day. You must not
mope here. I am glad I have seen you. I heard a most ridiculous story
afloat, about a young lady running a carrier's cart, and somebody said
it was you. Now I have seen you, I can contradict the story. You do not
look as if that 'rôle' would suit you. Do you know of any girl about
here who is doing such a thing? Some farmer's daughter, I suppose.
Girls will do anything nowadays! And the more unfeminine a pursuit may
be, the more it appeals to them."

Tomina, lying back in her chair in a daintily embroidered muslin frock
and looking a picture of fragile porcelain femininity, gazed at Mrs.
Norman languidly through her long eyelashes.

"Is driving not a feminine pursuit? My carrier's cart is the only thing
I really enjoy."

Mrs. Norman gazed at her with horrified eyes.

"Then the report is true? My dear girl, does your stepmother know about
it? You must have far more energy than your appearance credits you
with. Well, good-bye; you have astonished me! I wish I had time to ask
for details, but I am due at the rectory for a committee meeting. Dear
me! What a tangled medley is the composition of a modern girl!"

She bustled out of the room. Alice Merryweather accompanied her to the
gate, then returned to Tomina.

"You are a tangled medley," she said. "Mrs. Norman spoke truly there."

"And I seem to myself so simple!" said Tomina. "Life must be so here.
I am not at all tangled in my mind. I only do the things I want to do,
and leave the rest."

"You have no high ideals," said Alice, musingly. "Those are what
require effort and produce restlessness and discontent."

"What are your ideals?"

"To cultivate to the fullest extent every fraction of my brain. I would
not have an ounce of me lying fallow. To do work in the world that
will last. Not to stagnate, and be a source of danger to others by
stagnation!"

Alice spoke in a low voice, but her tone vibrated with earnest passion.

Tomina looked at her and smiled, then she sighed.

"I don't know whether I am so simple after all," she said. "I am
playing a game, trying to match my environment, but simplicity means
goodness, and I am not good. And I never wished to be good till—Shall I
make you my father confessor? Why am I talking like this, I wonder!"

She stopped abruptly.

But Alice looked interested and sympathetic, so Tomina went on—

"Till I met an absurd little book pedlar, and he gave me a lesson on
limes coming into leaf, and every time I pass those limes, they repeat
his words to me. I sometimes think that unseen forces are surrounding
me, making me think of religion, and compelling me to be religious. I
bought a Bible the other day. Harry forced me to. He wanted some of his
Bible stories. I think if I have an ideal at all, it is to have the
faith of a little child. I told the quaint pedlar that I came down here
to bury myself, and that eventually I meant to emerge a new creature.
He at once began to talk about a new Birth, and the sap rising in the
dead trees of winter. He told me I wanted the sap to rise in me.

"I laughed at his little parable, but this morning I was hunting about
for a fresh Bible story and I came across this verse. Do you know it? I
never remember having heard it before—

 "'If any man be in Christ, he is a new creature: old things are passed
away; behold, all things are become new.'

"You see, a part of that is my experience. Old things are passed away.
I did away with them when I came down here. I don't want them back
again. I'm sick of my town friends and gaieties. And I'm wondering, if
I want to resurrect myself properly, whether I had not better take my
soul into account, and get made a new kind of person altogether!"

Tomina paused. A wistful look was in her eyes.

For a moment Alice was speechless, then she said gently—

"Well, have a try. I'm busy cultivating my intellect; you cultivate
your spiritual part. We are all of us a medley of parts, you know."

"How can I set about it?" asked Tomina.

Alice's early training saved her from making a flippant reply. After
all, she argued to herself, Tomina was not a clever individual, so why
should she not get the comfort of religion if she chose? She would not
be the one to prevent her.

"I suppose," she said slowly, "the Bible will show you. I believe the
study of it feeds the spiritual part of you. I used to be told so, I
remember."

Then she got up to go. And as she shook hands, a smile flashed over her
face.

"You are a tangled medley. Mrs. Norman was quite right."

"I hope something or somebody will untangle me before long," retorted
Tomina. "But one thing I know, and that is that Mrs. Norman is not
going to trot me out to luncheon parties, and I don't mean to return
her call."

"You cannot avoid doing that."

"Oh yes; an invalid can avoid anything."

"But not an invalid who drives a carrier's cart."

Tomina laughed gaily.

"I am the queen of my van. I can sit for hours in silence if I choose.
Conversation at lunch or dinner bores me to distraction. It must
necessarily be so forced and artificial."

"Oh, I don't agree. Think of Dr. Johnson's breakfast table, and—"

Tomina waved her hand.

"Good-bye. Of all things, I hate a literary meal. I believe half the
speeches are made up beforehand. Each one vies with the other in making
the most brilliant sallies. I have been to one or two of those edifying
repasts, so I know what I am talking about."

When Alice had gone, she went out into the garden.

"And now I shall refresh myself with Harry."

CHAPTER VIII

UP A TREE

AND now came a spell of hot weather. Tomina spent most of her time in a
hammock in her garden. Harry was still her chief joy. One morning she
promised to take him fishing.

"We will take our dinner with us, and spend a day on the river. We'll
drive out to it, for I've heard there's good trout-fishing four miles
out."

Harry was enchanted. Susan said rather grimly that she thought a bent
pin and a worm at the end of a piece of string in the nearest brook
would be enough for him. But Tomina said—

"You forget that I'm a fisherwoman, Susan. I've landed small salmon by
myself in Scotland and I'm going to teach him proper fly-fishing. You
won't see us till dusk. Pack a good big basket. I've got leave to fish
where I'm going. Dr. Merton owns that bit of the river."

The hired trap took the two of them, accompanied by Thor, along the
lanes in the early morning. It was an ideal day for fishing. Still and
grey, with not much sun. But it was oppressively warm.

Tomina had meant to put up her trap at a little farm near the river,
but when they came to it, they found the people away for the day. So
she drove on, and tied the horse to some stout oak fencing. Then she
and Harry began their sport; but they had no luck. Harry was a restless
little soul, and he soon got tired of holding his line at a stiff angle
and waiting for a bite which would not come.

"I expect the poor fis' don't want to be killed to-day, Tommie," he
said. "Tommie" had told him he might call her what he liked, and he had
responded at once. "Let's take off our shoes and stockings and get into
the water," he said.

"Tommie" declined, but she let him play about in a shallow part of the
stream, and rather gladly reeled up her line and lay down under the
shadow of an old oak tree. She was an adept at doing nothing in the
open-air.

The coolness of the fresh-running stream refreshed and soothed her.
Presently, she fell asleep, and a little later awoke with a start.

Where was Harry? There was no sound or sight of him. Her old horse was
still munching in the hedge. But Thor had disappeared. She called, but
there was no reply.

"Oh dear!" she said to herself. "Why do I bother myself with a child?
They're always an anxiety."

She walked for some way along the banks of the river. Could he possibly
have fallen in, or been carried down by the current? She wondered, and
a little shudder came over her at the thought. Then she thought of
Thor, and was reassured. Surely he would have returned to her. There
was a slight clearing in the woods which stretched along one side of
the river, and in the distance she suddenly espied Harry's straw hat.

That brought relief to her mind.

"The little imp has run off into the wood."

She walked on, calling him by name as she went. And at last in the
distance she heard his voice and the quick, short bark of Thor.

"Tommie, oh, Tommie, come quick!"

She hastened her step. There was a group of old trees in front of her.
As she came up to them, a small voice cried to her from the topmost
boughs of one—

"Oh, Tommie, I can't come down! I've climbed up too high. I'm
frightened. Fetch me down!"

Looking up through the thick leaves of an oak, she saw a white-faced
child clinging desperately round a bough. Thor was standing below,
looking up in a perplexed fashion.

Tomina's heart sank within her. Then she pulled herself together.

"Harry, you mustn't be a coward! You climbed up—you can climb down. I
can't come up to fetch you. Be a brave boy and come down."

Harry began to sob, and kick feebly with one leg.

"There's nothing to step on, there troofully isn't! I've tried all I
can. I'm going to fall. Do ask God to help me. I've asked Him till I'm
tired. I'm so 'fraid He wants me to be killed this afternoon, and I
don't want to be!"

A vision came back to Tomina of her childhood, when a kitten had
climbed a tree and had been rescued by a fat butler on the top of a
tall ladder. She looked wildly round for help. Harry must be rescued,
but she could not be his rescuer.

"Do come up!" cried the child. "I'm sure you could climb easy, and then
you'd help me down."

"I haven't climbed a tree since I was a child," said Tomina,
helplessly. "Oh, Harry dear, I can't! Do try to come down. There's a
branch below you. Can't you reach it?"

"My legs won't stretch," sobbed Harry, "and I can't hold much longer!"

Then, after another wild and fruitless glance around, Tomina nerved
herself for the effort. She tucked up her skirt and began the ascent.
It was not a difficult tree to climb, otherwise Harry would not have
attempted it. She was surprised at herself for the ease with which
she did it, but happening to glance down when she was fairly high up,
terror seized her.

With great self-control she looked up at Harry, and went on climbing.
She reached a strong bough beneath him, and sitting astride on it
caught hold of him, and the next minute, he was by her side. He clung
hold of her and smiled through a mist of tears.

"I do love you, Tommie! I'm quite safe now. I thought I was going to
fall."

"You are a little wretch, and as to being safe, I don't know about
that. Why is it so much easier to climb up than down, I wonder?"

Tomina looked down through the leafy branches with fearful eyes.

"I broke one branch on my climb up, and I don't believe I shall be any
cleverer than you. There is no foothold for either of us, Harry, unless
you can find the way. Can you climb down now?"

"I can get on your back while you do it," suggested Harry, cheerfully.

And Tomina laughed in spite of herself.

"You've inveigled me up into a tree, and here we shall have to stay.
It's ridiculous! Look here, I'll hold your hand, and you must start
away down."

Harry valiantly did his best, but the branches beneath swayed in an
alarming way with a sudden gust of wind. And in terror, he climbed back
to the safe, strong bough on which Tomina was sitting.

"I can't! I can't!" he said. "You go first, Tommie dear."

Tomina made the attempt, but found it impossible. And the sudden
rushing wind that now was sweeping through the wood filled her with
fresh alarm.

"A storm is coming up," she said. "I knew that sultry feeling in the
air was a sign of it."

Then she looked down at Thor, who was whining uneasily at the foot of
the tree.

"Go home, sir! Fetch help! Fetch it! Fetch any one!"

Thor bounded to his feet, looked round wildly and dashed away.

"He's gone!" cried Harry, in delight. "He'll fetch Susan!"

"Susan!" gasped Tomina. "A lot of good Susan would do! I hope he'll
have sense enough to bring a man. Oh, Harry, you have brought disaster
on us!"

As she spoke, there was a low rumble of thunder in the distance, and
heavy drops of rain fell with a thud upon them.

She took Harry into her arms, got into a more comfortable position, and
then awaited, with all the calmness she could muster, the coming storm.

"In a tree in a thunderstorm," she murmured. "It is sheer madness, but
things are so bad that they can't be worse."

"Do you think God will take care of us? I don't like thunder,"
whimpered Harry. "Do ask God to keep us safe."

It was not the first time that Harry had forced Tomina to pray.

She murmured in a low tone—

 "O God, protect us and send some one to our help. For Christ's sake.
Amen."

"It's such a pity," said Harry, after a moment's silence, "that God
'dosn't' want us to see Him before we go to Heaven, else He could just
step down out of a cloud quite easy and lift us down."

Tomina did not know how to reply to this assertion.

Louder and nearer rolled the thunder clouds, then came a vivid flash of
lightning, and then a crash of thunder overhead. Harry clutched hold of
Tomina in trembling fright.

"It's awful to be up so close," he said. "Don't you wish we could get
into a nest like the birds and hide our heads?"

Tomina's courage was oozing fast. She could but hold the child and keep
silence. Then her sense of humour came uppermost, and she began to
laugh.

When Harry looked up with a shocked face, she excused herself—

"I was only wishing some of my friends in London could see me," she
said.

For some moments after that, the storm was too violent for them to
speak. It seemed as if it were raging on the top of them. Then it
rolled on and away from them, but the rain came on faster than ever. It
shot down upon them in such sheets of water that, in spite of the thick
foliage above them, they were drenched through.

In about a quarter of an hour, it stopped as suddenly as it came.
Tomina lifted her face with a long sigh of relief.

"It's over, Harry sweet! Where do you think Thor can be? He has never
come back. What shall we do? I am getting the cramp, I believe. Let us
give a co-ee!"

So they co-eed, and the birds flew away in fright, and the rabbits
scuttled to their holes, and still there was no sign of any one coming.

"I'm so hungry!" said Harry, plaintively.

Tomina rose desperately from her perilous perch, determined to make one
more effort to descend.

"If only I had not broken that branch, I could do it," she said. "It
seems as if we shall never be discovered here. I wonder if I could let
myself drop, but it looks such an awful height from the ground!"

She had hardly finished speaking before Harry gave a joyful cry.

There was a crashing in the thicket a little way off, and in another
moment Thor came bounding to the foot of the tree, followed by a man in
a mackintosh. Harry gave a delighted yell, but Tomina's face changed as
she recognised the man.

It was Colonel Norman.

Some words she had heard read in church came into her mind—

 "'And hast thou found me, O mine enemy?'"

He looked up and their eyes met.

Tomina felt that her position did not warrant any show of dignity on
her part, so she said slowly—

"I suppose Thor fetched you. I am very sorry you troubled to come. You
see our predicament. Owing to a broken branch our descent has been
impossible. We want a ladder badly."

"Oh, I think we can dispense with a ladder!" said Colonel Norman,
briskly.

There was a little twinkle in his eyes, which happily Tomina did not
see.

He threw off his mackintosh, and said—

"I'm afraid you've been out in all this storm. I was caught in it, and
was swinging along home when Thor bounced up. It was fortunate I was in
this direction. Your dog has all his wit—he brought me straight here.
Now then!"

With a light spring, he commenced to climb, and in a very few minutes
had Harry in his arms and brought him down to the ground.

Tomina began to feel distinctly nervous.

"We aren't very far from the farm where I hoped to put up my horse and
trap. They are out for the day, but I should think you could find a
ladder about."

"That would be a very roundabout way of proceeding. I will have you
down in a minute."

"I will wait for a ladder," Tomina said.

He made no answer, but climbed up beside her.

"Now give me your hand. That is it. Now put your foot there. Don't be
afraid; I have hold of you. That's splendid! Now then, it is fairly
easy."

He had taken possession of her so quickly and thoroughly that she had
no choice but to follow his guidance. But she was angry when, on nearly
completing the descent, he had sprung down in front of her, and had
lifted her bodily down, and held her in his arms for a moment quite
unnecessarily.

"What a little bit of a thing you are!" he said. "You are lighter than
ever. There is nothing substantial about you!"

Tomina released herself very indignantly.

"You are far too officious. But I thank you. Harry and I will find our
way home all right now."

She turned from him, but Colonel Norman could not be so easily
dismissed. He placed his hand on her arm.

"You are wet to the skin," he said. "Now, look here, Tomina, treat me
like a stranger if you like. But a stranger would not go off and leave
you in this plight. I am coming with you to find your trap."

Then Harry spoke—

"I call her 'Tommie,'" he said. "Do you know her? I'm so glad you got
us down. We haven't had our dinner yet. And my socks is sticking with
the rain."

"Run along and see if our trap is where we left it," said Tomina.

Then as he obeyed her, she said to her companion: "It seems to be my
luckless fate to run up against you when I least desire it. I have
thanked you for your help, but if our trap is here, I beg that you
will leave us. I am quite capable of driving home myself. This sounds
ungrateful—"

"Oh, I didn't expect you to feel pleasantly towards me!" said Colonel
Norman, cheerfully. "Only I knew from your dog that you were in a fix,
and so I came along. And I am not going to let you drive home in that
thin, wet dress. You must at least put on my mackintosh."

Tomina made no reply. She quickened her steps towards the spot where
she had left her trap. Harry was scampering about, and now ran back to
her.

"The horse isn't there. He's runned away, and the trap too!"

Tomina gave a little gasp.

It was too true. Frightened by the storm, the horse had broken loose
from the fence, and had evidently found the road and bolted along it;
for the whip was lying on the ground some distance away.

"We must just walk home," said Tomina, bravely. "After all, it is only
five miles. I expect old Jim has made for home. They will think we have
been upset. What a comfort it is to think that it is only Susan who
will be concerned! We have nobody else belonging to us."

But she did not relish a five mile walk back with Colonel Norman. He
said very little, but shouldered their fishing rods and again offered
his coat to Tomina, who refused.

"How could I walk in a thing like that, trailing yards of it on the
ground, and being smothered in it? I wish you would leave us, Colonel
Norman; we have our lunch to eat, and it is fine now. I don't feel
inclined to hurry home."

"Then sit on this fence and eat it, and I will go over to the farm and
see if any one is stirring there yet. Perhaps we might borrow a trap."

He strode away. Tomina felt angry at her own ungraciousness, and still
angrier with the one who caused it.

"How could I ever have been in love with him?" she muttered to herself.
"But I must say he always took good care of me. Nobody has ever done it
since. I hated it then, but—"

She stopped, and resolutely turned her thoughts and attention to Harry,
who was gleefully and eagerly unpacking their basket of food. She and
he had a merry meal together, in spite of all their misfortunes.

Colonel Norman, returning, caught the sound of her happy ringing laugh,
which was checked directly he came in sight.

"Can't get a trap. The house is still locked up, and a young blockhead
in the stable told me the master had gone to town with the only horse
in. We must walk, and trust to meeting some one."

A little later, the three of them tramped along the road. Tomina was
very silent and very cross. Harry and the Colonel carried on most of
the conversation. Once Tomina was asked—

"You saw my mother the other day. Do you think you will be able to come
over and lunch with her one day—when I am out?"

"I don't go out to lunch," said Tomina, shortly. "I have given up
society. I am doing a rest cure. I wrote to your mother and explained."

"I don't believe in these rest cures," Colonel Norman said. "I suppose
they're a necessity of the age, and serve to distract and amuse a few
of our tired-out pleasure-seekers. It only has the effect of making
them fly round the faster when they leave them."

"You ought to have been in the Church," Tomina said.

A little colour deepened the Colonel's suntanned cheek.

"I don't want to gas," he said apologetically.

And then Harry claimed his attention, and however prosily he might talk
to her, Tomina acknowledged in her heart that he talked charmingly to
children.

Presently, as they came to four cross-roads, a trap appeared.

"Shall we hail it?" asked Colonel Norman, a little reluctantly. "It
looks as if it is going our way."

"Of course," said Tomina, eagerly. "I would get up into a tinker's cart
if it would take me home. You can't imagine that I enjoy this walk?"

So the trap was hailed, and it proved to be young Merryweather driving
home. He was delighted to take up Tomina and Harry. She was sweetness
itself to him, and Colonel Norman could not but note the marked
contrast in her manner. He insisted, however, upon her wearing his
mackintosh.

"You are still in your damp clothes, and you may get another heavy
shower. Clouds are blowing up again."

"Yes, we shall have rain before we get home," said Philip Merryweather.

And Tomina very ungraciously allowed herself to be enveloped in it. She
hardly thanked the Colonel for the loan.

"Good-bye," he said cheerily. "I do hope you will be none the worse for
your experience; and I hope your trap and horse will be found at home."

"I will send back your coat to-morrow," said Tomina.

"Oh, don't trouble! I will call round for it."

"I will send it by the postman. He goes by your place."

Tomina spoke quite firmly. She gave him a curt little nod as they drove
off. Harry waved his hand.

"Good-bye! And do come and see us soon. Tommie and me has such lovely
games in the garden, and you could help us to build our bridge over the
stream!"

Tomina talked away with feverish haste to young Merryweather, but in
her heart she was saying to herself—

"I've been simply beastly to him—outrageously rude, but he makes me so."

The rain came on again, fast and furiously, and she thought of Colonel
Norman plodding along the road without his coat. He had come to her
rescue, done all he could to help her, had stripped himself of his
mackintosh for her benefit, and had received from her nothing but
rudeness. She gave herself a little shake as she thought of it.

As they passed the inn from which she had borrowed the trap, Tomina
found that the horse had arrived safely home.

"I suppose you were rather alarmed about it?" she asked the landlord.

"Oh no, mem," was the reply; "we seed the halter round his head, and
knewed he'd broken from where he'd been tied to. We guessed you were
takin' shelter somewhere, and would find your way 'ome."

Tomina drove on indignantly.

"I shall never like that man again!" she declared. "He is callously
selfish. He would never have sent any one to look for me if I had been
pitched out on the road in a dying condition."

"Oh, Crake is rather a character! He says he never troubles about
anything, and I don't think he does. He's a fatalist, and says that
'what happens is bound to happen, and nobody can prevent it.'"

"Is it a comforting creed?" Tomina questioned.

"It seems to comfort him."

Tomina was silent.

Philip Merryweather insisted on driving her home, and Susan wisely put
both her and Harry to bed and gave them hot drinks.

It was surprising that the next day they seemed none the worse for
their misadventure.

Colonel Norman received his mackintosh back by the postman the next
morning.

It was done up neatly in a parcel.


He put it on almost affectionately when he walked out next in it.
And then it was that he found a tiny twist of note-paper tucked away
in the corner of one of the big pockets. He opened it with quickened
heart-beats, but it contained only two words—

 "Forgive me."

Colonel Norman kept that little peace offering locked up in his
writing-desk with other mementoes of wayward Tomina.

CHAPTER IX

THE FLOWER-SHOW

THE next thing that happened was a visit from a school attendance
officer to know why Harry was not at school.

Tomina received him with open-eyed amazement.

"You need not trouble about the little boy at all. He is under my care
now."

"But he must receive an education," insisted the attendance officer.

"There is plenty of time for that later on. He is only seven. I think
children learn too much at these Council schools."

"Then are you going to teach him yourself, miss?"

"I teach Harry! Why, he teaches me. I have learnt a lot from him."

"He must go to school," persisted the official.

"Well, I'll see about it. Good morning."

When Tomina had got rid of her visitor, she turned to Susan indignantly.

"I shall not be 'at home' to that man if he calls again, Susan."

"But, ma'am, you must. An attendance officer is like the police—you
can't shut him out."

Tomina laughed, but she did not feel quite easy in her mind, so she
wrote a note to the Doctor saying she wished to see him. And Dr. Merton
came, and was told of the incident. He laughed at Tomina's dilemma.

"Of course, Master Harry will have to go to school. You must talk it
over with Mrs. Francis. She and the Squire have just returned. I expect
she will come round and call upon you."

"Oh, I don't want any more visitors!" said Tomina, hastily. "She will
only come to see Harry."

"You and she will have to settle his future together."

"I never think about the future," said Tomina with half-shut eyes. "I
hate preparation of any kind."


The first time she met Mrs. Francis was after church on Sunday.

She was a tall, graceful young woman with a sweet sad face. In her
black shady hat, Tomina at first put her down to be a young girl.
But when she saw her closer, she knew that she was in the prime of
womanhood.

She had stayed behind to talk to the Doctor, but Harry had left
Tomina's side and dashed up to her.

"My Mrs. Francis!" he cried.

She stooped and took him in her arms. Then she turned to Tomina, and
the Rector introduced them.

"Miss Gavin is a thorough stay-at-home; we can't move her out of her
pretty cottage," the Rector said genially.

Mrs. Francis looked across at Tomina and smiled, and her smile softened
Tomina at once. She had been feeling a little aggressive towards
Harry's benefactor.

"It is very pleasant and unusual to find any one fond of staying still
nowadays," she said.

"Oh, I'm made to do it by doctor's orders!" said Tomina. "They've
buried me down here for a purpose."

"May I come and see you? Please say if you would rather not."

"When you put it like that, what can I say?" said Tomina, laughing.
"And Dr. Merton says you must interview me about Harry. So please come
any day but Tuesday or Friday. Would Wednesday suit you?"

"Perfectly. I will come then."

Harry interrupted here. Pointing his finger at Tomina, he said—

"And she reads to me every night, but she didn't know about the baby
king, and we haven't been able to find where his chapter is."

"Are you going to desert me now, Harry?" Tomina asked.

He sprang back to her.

"I lives with Tommie," he said, nodding gravely at Mrs. Francis by way
of explanation, "but I'll be very glad to come to tea with you soon."

"That's a very clever way of dividing your favours," said Tomina, and
then she shook hands with Mrs. Francis and walked away.


Susan was full of interest when she heard of the meeting.

"And don't you think her a beautiful lady, ma'am? If only she didn't
dress in such plain black always! I should dearly like to see her in
colours. I can fancy her in crimson silk with a white ostrich feather
curling round her dark head. She would look a reg'lar queen."

"Oh, Susan!" gasped Tomina. "I've never seen a queen dressed like that!
I think she is very sweet-looking, but she's a perfect stranger at
present."


On the following Tuesday, as Tomina was driving her van, she met three
riders coming along the road.

Sir Alfred Digby and Mrs. Francis were two of the riders. Colonel
Norman made the third. Mrs. Francis was a good rider, and she was just
now talking and laughing with much animation to Colonel Norman.

He was the only one who recognised Tomina, and lifted his hat to her.
Mrs. Francis never even glanced at the van.

And when they had passed, Tomina took herself to task for the wave of
annoyance that came over her.

"It's ridiculous of me to object to her making herself so pleasant to
him. Why should she not? He is not my property, and never will be now.
No—we should only disagree again. He has become prosy, and is just as
masterful and obstinate as ever. He would expect me to go his way, and
he would never come mine. But I don't like being left outside. I have
always been accustomed to be almost the centre of everything going on.
And now—I am a carrier, and my world is amongst the poor."

Then she added stoutly to herself—

"And I am very glad of it. The poor are much more interesting than the
rich. I'm sick and bored with people of my own class, and I shall take
good care to keep away from them here."

She drove on wrapped in thought, and then came an interruption in the
shape of a sweet-faced old woman waiting to be taken up.

Tomina's van was full this morning, but she got the old woman in the
corner beside her, and soon they began to talk.

"I'm on a sad errand, miss. My dear son, Peter, be taken mortal bad,
they say. New money, or some sich name as that, they call it. And I'm
going to see him afore he dies, if I can get there in time. He have bin
such a good son to me!"

"Is he married?"

"Yes; and he be quite well-to-do—keeps a small sadler's shop in St.
Martha's Cross. Would you b'lieve it, miss, ever since his father died,
eight year gone now, he have sent me ten shillin' weekly to help me
keep my little home together!"

"Splendid!" said Tomina. "But I suppose you get a pension?"

"No, for he supports me, they say, and he won't give of it up. But now
if he goes, the ten shillin' will go too, and if I get the pension,
'twon't keep me in the old home, and nothin' will indooce me to live
with his wife. She be a hard screw, and a scold by day and night. I'd
rather go to the House, that I would!"

She wiped away a tear, and then a smile came over her sweet old face.
She placed a trembling knotty hand on Tomina's arm.

"But I'm not frettin'. 'Tis human nature to wonder a bit, and the loss
of Peter will be far bigger than his money. He's my only boy, miss, and
such a dear good boy he have bin! But if 'tis God's will, I'll say,—

 "'Thy will be done.'

"And when us comes to think of it, what does it matter, when a body
gets to my age, where one lives? Every day lands me nearer the Home of
Joy above. That be something like a Home, now!"

Her eyes glistened as she spoke.

"Oh," said Tomina, "if you trust in God like that, He won't let
anything bad happen to you!"

"Ah, you know Him, miss? 'Tis good to have a talk together, isn't it?"

Tomina shook her head.

"No, I'm very ignorant. I only go by what Harry tells me. He told me
the other day that God counted our hairs, He was so interested in us."

"Yes," murmured the old woman, "that comes from the Book.

 "'The very hairs of your head are all numbered.'

"I will, I do trust Him. What else did the gentleman say, miss?"

"Who? Harry? Why, he's a baby of seven, but he and you talk alike."

Tomina laughed aloud at the old woman's puzzled face.

"He's at the beginning of life, you near the end. I wish I had the
faith of either of you! The only thing is to begin life over again like
a little child, isn't it? I am going to have a try at it. I wasn't
brought up like Harry. I got off the lines in my babyhood. I was never
taught that we were to walk through the world with our Creator."

"Ah, that's it, miss. We walk with the Almighty, and He'll settle where
I'm to live. Thank the Lord, no house can shut Him out. He's bound by
His promises to abide with His children."

Tomina looked at her thoughtfully.

"You are all very religious about here—I never heard people talk like
this in London."

"I've always heard tell that the Devil be very busy in Lunnon."

Tomina laughed again, and then they relapsed into silence.

When the old woman got out, Tomina shook her by the hand.

"I hope we shall meet again. How long are you going to stay at St.
Martha's Cross?"

"Just as long as my boy needs me, miss. I don't know what I should ha'
done if your van hadn't come along to-day. Thank you kindly for talk
and sympathy."

"I shall keep my eye on her," said Tomina to herself. "I should like
her to live with me. I wonder if she would?"

She wondered sometimes at the interest she felt in her passengers to
and from the market towns. And her gay spirits and simple friendliness
made her a very popular person amongst them.


On Wednesday, Mrs. Francis came to see her. Tomina had tea in her
garden by the sweet-briar hedge and under an apple-tree.

Harry was scampering about, but he sobered down when he saw Mrs.
Francis.

"Tommie says you'll make me go to school," he said. "Will you?"

"We're going to talk about it," said Mrs. Francis, smiling at him.

Tomina and she fought shy of the subject till tea was over, and then
Harry was sent away.

"Now," said Mrs. Francis, "what do you want to do with him, Miss Gavin?"

"I don't know," said Tomina, leaning back in her chair and looking at
her questioner with laughing eyes. "I want to keep him with me till I
get tired of him."

"Will you tire of him quickly?"

"I don't think so. Susan has all the bother of him, and he amuses and
interests me."

Tomina was showing her flippant side to Mrs. Francis this afternoon.

"The question is whether he is to be brought up as a labourer's child
or a gentleman. You might get him a governess. Won't you tell me if you
mean to adopt him?"

"Mean to adopt him!" exclaimed Tomina, almost to vehemence. "I adopt a
child! I shouldn't dream of such a thing! He is only staying with me
for a time. Do you want to adopt him?"

A shadow came into Mrs. Francis's soft brown eyes.

"No, I do not feel it would be wise. But his father was a clever man,
and I think he ought to be educated as his father would have wished.
There is a good Grammar School at Damerton, and I know a doctor's
daughter who has a small boarding-house for the younger boys who attend
the school. Why should he not be sent there? I will defray the cost of
it."

"He is too young yet," said Tomina.

"She has small boys of his age. She looks after them well. I suppose
you feel you cannot part with him?"

"There is no hurry. If he goes to school next autumn, it will be time
enough."

"Perhaps it will. And meanwhile, if you do not care for teaching him,
how would it be if he came up to me for an hour every morning? I love
teaching boys to read."

"I suppose we could do that," said Tomina, with a sigh of relief. "He
will be delighted to go. And Susan will be glad to get rid of him for a
part of the morning."

"Now I am going to beg a favour. Will you come to us this day
fortnight? We are having a village gathering in the afternoon, and a
cottage flower-show. You can be as quiet as you like. There will be
enough people to leave you entirely independent of them. I should like
