to have you there, and I am sure the villagers would. And may Susan and
Harry come too?"

"Of course they shall come, but I think I should like to leave it open.
I may not be inclined to appear when the time comes."

Mrs. Francis looked at her and smiled.

Tomina was quick to interpret that smile.

"Oh, I mean it! I am essentially selfish, and never do anything that I
do not want to do. I have cast all duties and social obligations to the
winds. Invalids are not to be coerced, and I am not out of the doctor's
hands yet, you know."

"There is no need to tell me that; you remind me of a thistledown when
I look at you."

And Tomina was looking very frail that afternoon. She had had a
sleepless night and a nervous headache. But the colour ebbed and flowed
in her cheeks as she talked, and it needed a woman's sharp eyes to see
that she was really feeling far from well.

"I suppose you will have all the county at this gathering?" Tomina went
on. "If I come, you must promise not to introduce me to any one, but
just let me wander about your grounds in my own fashion."

"You shall be as free as air, I assure you."

Conversation languished between them. Tomina was afraid of being
"preached" to, and Mrs. Francis knew it and wisely held her peace.

When she had gone, Tomina said to herself—

"If I was quite, quite sure that he would not be there, I would go. But
if there are crowds, I can lose myself amongst them. I know what these
country flower-shows are like."

Then she dismissed the matter from her thoughts.


Harry started his lessons at the Hall the very next day. He trotted off
by himself quite contentedly. His extreme independence was a continual
surprise to Tomina. She spoke to Susan about it.

"If he were in our class of life, he would be in the nursery with
nurses looking after him all day long. He would never be allowed to
cross the road by himself."

"But he's never had a nurse," said Susan. "He is quite accustomed to
run errands and go about by himself. You make too much of a baby of
him, ma'am."

"Oh no!" said Tomina, shaking her head. "It is his mixture of
babyishness and independence that I love. He tries to be so manly, and
his baby soul almost bursts in the trying. I hope Mrs. Francis won't
turn him into a little prig."

"Mrs. Francis never does naught but good to any human being," said
Susan, a little huffily.

Tomina laughed.

"Oh, I know she is a saint! But I am a sinner, Susan, and sinners don't
care for saints."

Susan backed out of the room. Tomina shocked her continually, and yet
she was her devoted slave.


When the flower-show day arrived, Tomina found that the whole village
was in a state of festivity. The flag was on the church tower, the
children in the village had a half-holiday, and the householders were
busy getting their daily routine over early, so as to dress themselves
in good time for the occasion.

Tomina first announced that she should not go, then she said she would.
When the afternoon came, she was irresolute, but finally sallied forth
in the blazing sun about four o'clock. She had on a white "crêpe de
chine" gown and a very ethereal-looking hat, and Susan thought her a
picture of dainty grace and beauty.

Susan held Harry by the hand, for Tomina had delivered him into her
charge.

"He will embarrass me," Tomina explained. "He will persist in being
friends with people whom I want to avoid. Keep him with you as much as
possible."

So Susan and Harry followed Tomina at a respectful distance.

A regimental band was already playing outside the big marquee when
they arrived. Tomina was greeted very warmly by Mrs. Francis, and to
her dismay was at once seized hold of by several town friends who were
staying in the neighbourhood and had been invited to the show.

There was a Captain and Mrs. Norton-Dynevor—the latter was an old
school friend of Tomina's—and a Lady Houghton and her daughters.

They all exclaimed at meeting her.

"So we've run you to earth! And this is your rest cure!" cried May
Houghton, a very outspoken young woman. "Why, you're a perfect fraud!
I'll tell Charlie of you directly I get home. He'll pelt down and make
it lively for you."

Charlie was their brother, and a very devoted admirer of Tomina's.

Tomina inwardly shuddered, and was much annoyed that her friends should
have come here and discovered her. But she responded to them with cool
sweetness, took their bantering in the same calm aloofness, and looked
about for escape.

She seemed hemmed in on all sides by people who wanted to talk to her.
The Rector and his wife, Alice Merryweather, her brother and his wife,
and Mrs. Norman severally claimed her attention. Then, Dr. Merton and
Sir Alfred Digby.

Colonel Norman was the only one who did not approach her. She was
introduced to a Mr. Tracy St. Vincent, the young Squire of Corring,
whom she had met when she was first driving up from the station to
Little Honeycombe, and he interested her, for he told her he had been
born and bred in London and had toiled in a City office for fifteen
years before he came into his property.

"And now I never intend to go near London again. I always did hanker
after the country. My mother died, I believe, of longing for it. She
never could cotton to London, and I take after her: but, oh, what a
lot one has to learn! I couldn't sit a horse when I first came down. I
didn't know the difference between a field of barley and one of oats.
I have been taken in right and left by these country louts. But I'm
learning their ways and customs fast. If you've got the liking for the
country life embedded in you, you'll soon graft yourself into it."

He stopped abruptly. Tomina's sympathetic eyes had drawn him out of his
usual reticence.

"I love the country people," said Tomina—"they are dears! I wouldn't
have come here to-day if I thought I was not going to mix with them."

"Oh, I have heard of your carrier's van!" said young St. Vincent. "I
thought it was the finest thing I've heard for ages! Shall we go over
to the tent and see some of your friends? They're most of them in there
looking at their piles of vegetables."

Before Tomina could reply, an imposing-looking lady swept down upon
them.

"Tracy, I am so sorry, but can you find my car? I must go on to Lady
Phillipa's. She is expecting me. Miss Gavin, surely you remember me?"

"It is Mrs. De Bullon," said Tomina, slowly.

She remembered her well. She was the wife of a Member of Parliament,
and was more admired for her powers as a successful philanthropist and
organiser than as a friend.

"Tracy is my nephew," she explained. "We've taken a house for the
summer to be near him. Poor boy, he was quite unprepared and unfitted
for this property! Three deaths in one year brought it to him. I help
him with his household. He wants it badly."

Tracy St. Vincent had walked off reluctantly. Mrs. De Bullon seated
herself on his vacant chair and commenced to question Tomina about her
health.

"I shall be seeing Lady Gavin next week, have to run up to town about a
children's 'fête' that we are organising together. What report shall I
take to her of you? A reassuring one, I suppose, for she told me most
emphatically you were unable for any social functions. A little bird
told me that you were in this neighbourhood, though I believe you have
tried to keep it dark. You must not play havoc with young men's hearts
down here. I must warn my poor Tracy. He is unused to the ways of young
women, and I should not like him to get bitten, especially as he is
virtually engaged to his cousin. It is our great wish that that match
should come off. Do you remember Connie Laurence? Such a charming girl!
And an uncle has lately died and left her a most handsome legacy."

Tomina mentally squirmed under Mrs. De Bullon's conversation. She
glanced eagerly round for a way of escape, and almost smiled when she
saw Colonel Norman approaching their corner.

"I'm 'so' sorry," she said sweetly to Mrs. De Bullon. "But I'm getting
quite faint. I expect it's the heat. I shall have some tea, and see
whether it will revive me. Colonel Norman, will you take me to the tea
tent?"

She rose as she spoke and greeted Colonel Norman with a bewildering
sweet smile.

He was so astounded that a little flush crept up into his tanned
cheeks, but he responded at once.

Then, as they walked across the lawn together, Tomina's tone was
petulant.

"Take me away anywhere—anywhere except into the society of those men
and women who are looking on and talking each other threadbare. I'm a
villager now and am happiest amongst my own set."

"I'll get you some tea under the trees here," said Colonel Norman,
quietly, but he wondered if his heart-beats could be heard. Here was
Tomina at last ready and willing to be alone with him. He added, "We'll
go and see the prizes given afterwards. You'll find most of your
friends at that."

Something in his cool possessive tone made Tomina say hastily—

"I can't conceive why Fate should continually send you across my path
when I am needing help. But I would make use of any one to save myself
annoyance. You know that, I suppose?"

"I think I do remember it."

"You need remember nothing," said Tomina, angrily, and as she spoke,
she encountered his gaze of tender amusement. It took her back in a
flash to the time when she had sought to draw that expression out of
his stern grey steel eyes.

He placed a chair in the shade for her, and whilst she was meditating
making a second escape from him, he was back with refreshments for her.

She leant back in her chair, and decided that it was cool and restful
and away from the buzz of people's voices. He left her a second time,
and then returned bringing a cup of coffee for himself. He seated
himself near her, but for a moment or two both of them were silent, and
Tomina determined that she would not be the one to break the silence.

"Your small satellite is not with you to-day?"

"He is with my maid," Tomina answered abruptly.

Another silence.

Then Colonel Norman leant forward and began to speak in a very
persuasive voice. When he abandoned his masterful tactics and
condescended to diplomatic measures, he generally won his way.

"Don't you think we might patch up a truce? You don't want me to
remember the past. As I told you the other day, I shall be more than
willing to start afresh. We'll bury our past and work for a new future.
Let us be friends. I won't worry you; I am so busy now that I have
little time for visiting my neighbours. But if we do come across each
other, as we have done to-day, let us be pleasant to one another, as
mere acquaintances are."

Then Tomina looked at him.

"As a 'mere acquaintance,' then, I'll be civil, and
occasionally—'perhaps' pleasant."

Here a smile stole across her face in spite of herself.

"May we shake hands on this agreement?"

He held her hand in his longer than was necessary. Tomina frowned as
she withdrew it, but she said nothing for a moment. Then she rose from
her seat.

"And now we'll go and look at the flowers and vegetables."

If Colonel Norman was disappointed, he did not show it. He was too
pleased at the success of his move to be downhearted.

CHAPTER X

A STRICKEN WOMAN

"HERE'S our carrier!"

This joyful cry came from a little group of women standing at the door
of the big marquee.

Tomina beamed upon them, and they pressed round her eagerly.

"Miss, my George has got second prize for his 'taties.' Them were what
you brought back last market day!"

"And Mrs. Grabbs be carryin' on disgraceful inside, because her husband
who have took first prize for veg'tables four years runnin' has only
got a second this year!"

"Me dear, this is my darter to law—married my Ben last Thursday—him
that you took up on the road when his horse had cast a shoe!"

Colonel Norman saw at once that he was not wanted, so he raised his hat
and strode away and Tomina heaved a sigh of relief when he did so.

She went into the tent and amused herself with her humble friends for
quite a long while. Then Harry found her out.

"Oh, Tommie, let me stay with you, and do come and see some darling
little ducks that are going to be sold. Do you think we had better buy
just a little one? I think Susan would like it."

"I'm going home, dear; will you come with me or stay with Susan a
little longer?"

Harry looked wistfully round, and Tomina shook her head and laughed at
him—

"You know you want to stay, and I think you had better. I don't think
we'll try any ducks at present."

"I wish you'd stay a little longer," the child said.

"I couldn't. I've had enough. I'm longing to get away. Will you take a
message to Mrs. Francis from me?"

Harry was only too delighted.

"Tell her that Miss Gavin did not feel very well, so has gone home.
Don't forget! Run up to the Terrace and tell her now."

Tomina sent him off, then slipped across the lawn, and was making her
way down the drive when she heard rapid steps behind her, and Dr.
Merton overtook her.

She coloured up a little when she saw him.

"I heard you were ill," he said, "so felt it my duty to pursue you."

"'Not very well' was my message," said Tomina; "I suppose Harry gave it
in exaggerated form."

"Are you tired out?"

Tomina looked at him and laughed.

"Your face is convicting me of fraud, but you yourself taught me that
we are made up of parts, and my spirit part is sick and tired of the
chatter and inquisitive inquiries of old acquaintances. I could not
face them again, so I left without wishing my hostess good-bye."

"I'll walk a bit of the way with you. I have to see a patient in
Honeycombe."

"Who? Is any one ill?"

"Old Martha Tubbs. It's a heart case."

"I know her. She came in my van only last Friday, and beguiled a very
warm journey with delightful reminiscences of her youth."

"She's subject to heart attacks. You might go and see her. She would
like a visit from you."

Tomina made a slight grimace.

"I don't like sick people. I'm not a parson or even a parson's wife."

"No, but you have been given the wonderful and yet dangerous gift of
winning hearts. I hear a good deal as I go round of yourself, and your
van. Don't disappoint any poor soul who trusts in your friendship."

"But I assure you it isn't my fault if they place their trust in an
untrustworthy person! Every one who knows me will tell you that I am
absolutely unreliable. My old women amuse me, and I try to amuse them.
I consider them my guests as long as they are in my van, but when they
leave it, I forget all about them!"

"Are you sleeping better?" Dr. Merton asked irrelevantly.

"Is this a professional walk?" Tomina asked, laughing. Then she added:
"Yes, I am. The rest cure is doing me good. My body performs all its
functions as it should do—I sleep, I eat, I walk as any other healthy
person does, but the rest cure hasn't touched my mind—my spirit, shall
I say? And if I treat you as my father confessor, Dr. Merton, I must
admit that I am restless, and discontented, and dissatisfied. I'm
beginning to wonder what my part is in life; why I was born, if the
world wants me. I told you I was self-centred. I go round and round
myself and turn myself inside out, until I get perfectly giddy. But as
yet, I can find no solution to the problem.

"I may be supposed to be living in a grave, to be buried in the
country, but I've never done so much thinking in all my life as I have
done lately. I thought I was ready to stagnate, to live as a vegetable,
to take each day as it came, but my brain gives me no rest. It's at it
morning, noon, and night! Do you think now, truthfully, that I've any
right to go on living? Harry has been making me read the Bible. What an
awful Book it is for making you see what an infinitesimal atom you are
in the Universe!"

"I know quite well what is your vocation," said Dr. Merton, briskly;
"and strangely enough, you have discovered it yourself. Do you know
what you are called about here?"

"Do tell me."

"Tomina the Carrier."

"Well? You don't think a carrier's van fills my horizon in the
Universe?"

"I think to be a carrier fulfils the very highest purpose in life.
Think it out for yourself. And remember Who was our great Example in
that way."

"You are a very extraordinary man!" Tomina exclaimed. "I really don't
see at present what you are driving at!"

She knitted her brows, and walked on in silence for some minutes, then
she said with emphasis: "I believe that I am the object of a deep
and wide-laid conspiracy down in this part of the world. Every one
with whom I come in contact determines to reform me. I've had a Bible
pedlar, and Harry, and an old saint of a woman, and you, and even Alice
Merryweather. And you each have a dig at me in turn. I suppose I am too
unused to such tactics to be prepared beforehand to avoid and resist
them."

"Well," said Dr. Merton, smiling, "did you not come down here to be
made a new creature? I'm sure you were a wreck when I saw you. All eyes
and bones! You are putting on weight, and losing your wrinkles, but
body and soul are so closely united that I want you to be a thorough
cure. You do need reforming. I could wish you no better fate than to be
reformed and transformed, inside and out. Good-bye. I am going in here."

He turned in at a cottage gate, leaving her very abruptly.

Tomina walked on, slowly and thoughtfully, home.


Life went on very quietly with her after this. She had a visit from
Mrs. De Bullon and from Mrs. Norton-Dynevor. But she told them frankly
that she acknowledged no social claims at present, and would not be
able to return their calls.

One call she did make, and that was to see old Mrs. Cayley in her own
home. Her son had died, and had left no money to her. She was in great
trouble at having to leave her cottage, but would not be able to afford
to keep it up. She confided all her trouble to Tomina. And as she
sat in her sweet old kitchen, she made a pathetic picture of patient
resignation. Looking round at all her quaint old bits of crockery and
furniture, she said—

"I've had these for fifty years or so, miss. It do seem sad to say
good-bye to 'em, but my time is gettin' short, and I shan't have no
frettin' about makin' a will, or such like, if I have nothin' to leave.
My daughter-in-law be wild to have my bits in her house, but I'd rather
see them sold right out than see 'em lose all their gloss and shine in
her dusty rooms. She be one who never was taught to use elbow grease,
and a wipe with a greasy cloth be quite enough in her eyes. She be too
grand to work, and her young girl that cleans be just one like herself,
or would be if she could!"

"But, Mrs. Cayley, you mustn't go away from your dear old cottage,"
said Tomina. "I shall see you don't. Just leave it to me. Don't you
give another anxious thought. I can give you ten shillings a week just
as easily as your son did!"

Mrs. Cayley stiffened in her chair, and her old head held itself very
erect.

"My dear miss, I've never taken charity from any gentlefolk yet. My son
was different. It's more than kind of you, and I give you my grateful
thanks for thinking of such a thing. But I couldn't think of taking all
that money from you!"

Tomina looked quite taken aback.

"Oh, well, I'm sorry; I've made a blunder, but nowadays there are
always funds for elderly people. I know heaps of societies—public money
you know; there are lots for poor gentle-people. I have a governess who
thankfully receives enough to pay her rent. You wouldn't mind money
through a fund, would you? I'll work it somehow, and if the neighbours
are curious, don't satisfy their curiosity. Ask them to ask me, and
I'll deal with them."

Tears came into Mrs. Cayley's eyes.

"I've no business to be proud, miss. You'll lift a heavy burden off me,
if you can manage it. 'Tis just an answer from my Heavenly Father!"

"There, that's right. I'll let you know in a day or two about it. I
shan't forget. And perhaps one day you'll ask me to tea. I feel, when
I'm sitting in this sweet old room, that we've gone back a hundred
years. I wish my cottage was like it!"

As Tomina walked home, her heart was aglow.

"I'm so thankful I knew of her difficulty. Why, it will only mean £26
a year. It's a trifle to keep her and her treasures together. I can
easily work it through a society if it will be a sop to her pride.
She's an old dear! I begin to wish I had been born in her class of
life!"

And before a week was over, it was all arranged, and old Mrs. Cayley
was ensured peace and comfort for the remainder of her years.


One afternoon, Tomina was standing at her gate, looking dreamily across
at the rookery opposite. There was a disturbance of some sort going on.
She fancied some jackdaws who built in the old church tower had come
across to pilfer, and had been caught and punished.

She quite started when Mrs. Herbert's voice broke in upon her.

"May I come in?"

Tomina unwillingly moved aside, and held out her hand, but she smiled a
welcome. Tomina would smile at her greatest enemy, she could not help
it, and when once accused by one of her friends of insincerity, she
responded—

"Oh, what is a smile? Just a movement of the lips. It doesn't express
my feelings. I take good care of that."

She took Mrs. Herbert into her sitting-room.

"I have really come to ask you if you would stop at the Rectory gate
on your way to Damerton to-morrow. My parlourmaid is in agonies with
toothache. She wants to go to the dentist's, and our horse is lame, so
I cannot drive her."

"I'll take her with pleasure," said Tomina. She could not resist
adding: "You have become reconciled to my van, have you not?"

"Well, yes, I have," said Mrs. Herbert with a little hesitation. "I see
the boon it is to the village. The idea shocked me, but I suppose it is
all right; my husband thinks so."

"I don't see that it has worked any harm," said Tomina.

Mrs. Herbert began to talk then about the flower-show at the Hall.

"You disappeared so suddenly that we feared you were not well. But
your maid told me the next day when I called that you had gone out for
a walk, so I was relieved. I suppose you have not heard the sad news
about Mrs. Norman. I think you just know her, do you not?"

"I hope nothing has happened to her?"

"Mrs. Francis came in to tell me about it yesterday. She is an old
friend of hers, and we always think between ourselves that she and
Colonel Norman will come together. They have been seeing a good deal of
each other lately. Poor Mrs. Norman has been doing too much, rushing
about from one place to another. She was dining at the Hall with her
son two nights ago, and just after dinner insisted on going home to
water her garden, she said. Mrs. Francis said the Colonel seemed
anxious about her, as she had been complaining of severe headache that
day, and tried to dissuade her from leaving. She insisted on gaining
her point, as she always does. And yesterday, she had a seizure of some
sort. She lies helpless and speechless now; and Dr. Merton is doubtful
whether she will ever properly recover speech."

Tomina was shocked.

"How dreadful! It's a living death. But people always get better after
a time, don't they?"

"Not always. And her extreme restlessness and excitability has worn out
her constitution, I should say."

"Her poor son!" murmured Tomina.

"Yes, we pity him. He has never left her bedside. She knows him, but
can't speak. They have sent for two nurses and are going to have a
London opinion down, but Dr. Merton is clever enough to undertake the
case single-handed. Well, I must be going. I have my club accounts to
do."

Mrs. Herbert bustled off.

Tomina sat still in her chair and thought of the gay vivacious little
mother and the stern sombre son. Her heart ached for them both. She
wondered why such a blow had been dealt them, and wished she had been
to return Mrs. Norman's call.

"I haven't been nice to her. Suppose that it had been me stretched out
helpless and speechless on a bed? What should I do? Oh, it's awful,
awful to think of!"

In almost a panic, Tomina got up from her seat and paced the room.

"Poor old Gerald! I should like to send him my sympathy—I will write
him a note!"

So she sat down to her writing-table, and dashed off the following:—

 "DEAR COLONEL NORMAN,

 "It's horrible for you! And of course, worse for her. I am so sorry
for you. But she'll get over it, I'm sure she will. I should like to know
how she is. Why do such things happen?

 "Your very sympathetic

 "TOMINA."

Then she called Susan.

"How can I get this note to Yockley Court?"

"I really don't know, ma'am. 'Tis a long way. Shall I run out in the
village and see if I can get a boy to take it? The milkman told me that
the poor lady was taken ill there."

"See if you can get some one to take it. I will give them half a crown."

The half-crown won its way, and a lad started off with Tomina's note.

She spent a restless and preoccupied day. Harry was spending the day at
the Hall. When he returned, she welcomed him with extra warmth.

"I am feeling so dull, Harry; come and talk to me. What have you been
doing?"

"Finding snails in the ivy with Mr. Drake," Harry answered quickly.
"Mrs. Francis went away to see a poor sick lady, and then the Squire he
came into the garding and scolded me for turning the hose on a tabby
cat. I just had to, when she got on a branch of a tree and looked at me
so proudly. You would have done it, Tommie, I know you would. And I had
tea in the housekeeper's room, and Pratt said to Mrs. Wood that 'twas
a pity the Squire didn't marry, for he'd be left alone soon, and Mrs.
Francis would be at Yockley Court. Where is Yockley Court, Tommie, and
who lives there? They wouldn't tell me—"

"You shouldn't listen to servants' talk, Harry, and if you do, never
repeat it to me," said Tommie, severely.

And then she said to herself—

"I wish I hadn't sent that note. He will have quite enough sympathy
from her. Perhaps he had reason for talking as he did when I last saw
him. He wanted me to see that he was quite indifferent to me now. I am
very glad."


But in a few days, she received a little note from Colonel Norman.

 "MY DEAR TOMINA,

 "Forgive me, but I cannot address you so formally as you do me. Thank
you for your dear little note. My mother is going on well, they say,
but she has not quite recovered her speech yet, and will be helpless
for a long time, I fear. Is it too much to ask you if you would come
and see her one day? She loves visitors even now, and the days are long
and weary to her.

 "Always yours,

 "GERALD."

"I cannot face a sick-room," was her instantaneous thought.

But in a fortnight's time, she went.

She drove over one bright afternoon in the hired trap. She had never
seen Yockley Court, and was surprised to find it such a beautiful old
place. It was a grey-stone manor-house with a stone roof of the Tudor
period. There were stone-flagged walks and terraces, and the cold
grey-stone was brightened by the brilliant display of summer flowers.

The entrance-hall was very handsome with its carved oak panelling, and
tubs of bright begonias stood grouped round a fountain at the farther
end under a glass cupola. She was led upstairs by an old butler, who
told her with a shake of his head, that the mistress was very ill.

CHAPTER XI

IN A SICK-ROOM

WHEN she reached Mrs. Norman's room, the Colonel himself opened the
door. He looked very grave and sad, but his eyes brightened a little at
the sight of Tomina.

"Come in," he said. "It is good of you to come. She will like to see
you. And remember, if she can't answer you, she can hear you."

"Oh dear!" gasped Tomina. "I'm frightened. I shall not be able to talk!"

But she followed him into the big room, which was bright with flowers.
The windows opened down to the ground, and near one of them was a large
and comfortable couch, upon which the sick woman lay.

Tomina had seen her last flitting about at the flower-show. Now she
looked upon her lying flat and helpless, and she gave an involuntary
shiver as she stepped up to the side of the couch.

"Mother dear, this is Miss Gavin; she has come to see you."

He pulled forward an easy-chair, which Tomina took.

Mrs. Norman's bright, restless eyes fixed themselves on Tomina's face.

Tomina began to talk recklessly—

"I meant to come before, Mrs. Norman, but you know I'm never good at
visiting, and the less one has to do, the less one does. I'm so lazy by
nature, and the days seem to fly. You wouldn't believe how busy that
carrier's van keeps me. And I am seriously considering what I shall do
when the cold weather comes. Do you think I could have it heated with
hot water pipes? Or would that be too stuffy?"

Colonel Norman stood leaning against the side of the window.

"How would you heat it?" he asked.

"Oh, I don't know—by gas or electricity."

"Then you would have to have a stove or boiler. It would be rather an
expensive idea. You might in that case just as well turn your van into
a motor. I wonder you don't do that!"

"Perish the thought! I've come down here to get away from motors. It
would spoil everything. I should get to Damerton in ten minutes instead
of two hours, and all my road adventures would disappear. Fancy, Mrs.
Norman, on my last journey to market, I took a bride and bridegroom to
church! My whole van was convulsed with excitement. The bridegroom was
sixty, the bride fifty. They were old sweethearts, had outlived all
their near relations, and had been waiting for forty years for 'better
times' to be married. And at last, the bridegroom was lucky enough to
be offered the post of lodge-keeper by Mr. St. Vincent at Corring. I
had a finger in the pie, for the bride had made several trips in my
van, and somebody told me her story, and I thought the long waiting was
so pathetic that I happened to mention it to Mr. St. Vincent when I met
him out one day, and he found a niche for them at once. I'm invited to
tea at the Lodge to-morrow. You should have seen their dear old faces
in my van! They sat hand-in-hand like children. But, oh, it seemed such
a waste of years, all their youth and strength gone!"

"Yes," said Colonel Norman slowly, "waiting is weary work. But a man
feels it is worth it, if he knows that he will obtain his desire in the
end."

The pink colour rose in Tomina's cheeks; she moved in her chair and
addressed herself pointedly to Mrs. Norman.

"And so, you see, my van gets very interesting with such things
happening in it. But last Tuesday a dreadful thing happened. A lad
came in with a sack over his shoulders, and the sack was heaving and
squealing to such a degree that I told him he must put it in the rack
outside. He was trying to do this when it toppled over, came undone,
and three small pigs fled squealing down the road. My dog Thor gave
chase, a motor came by, and in the confusion, two pigs were run over
and killed. The lad got two pounds from the motorist, and he came back
delightedly swinging the dead pigs by the legs, and saying he only gave
fifteen shillings each for them, and now his mother would have bacon
enough to last for a month!"

Tomina laughed as she ended this story.

But Mrs. Norman's eyes never changed their eager, intent expression.

She went on talking about the villagers and her van. Colonel Norman
helped her by his responses. But at last, Mrs. Norman's eyes wandered
to her son's face, her lips moved. Tomina only heard an incoherent
murmur, but her son, bending his head, nodded and smiled and seemed to
understand.

Then she rose to go.

Mrs. Norman's eyes turned pleadingly to her face.

"Miss Gavin will come again, mother, I hope. Perhaps next time you will
be able to talk to her. You're getting on splendidly, you know."

A nurse who had been in the inner room came forward, and Colonel Norman
accompanied Tomina along the broad corridor. He was surprised and
distressed when suddenly Tomina burst into a violent fit of tears.

"Oh, it's awful! I can't help it! She's like an animal fixed in a trap.
I saw a rabbit once. And her eyes! Oh, it's dreadful! Why do such
things happen? I was—beginning—to feel—that God did care for us, and
now how can one feel it?"

Nobody, to hear her chattering away, would have realised the strain it
was to her to do so; now the reaction had come, and she seemed to lose
all self-control. In her abandonment of fright and grief, she caught
hold of the Colonel, and the next moment his arm was round her, and her
head was resting on his shoulder.

It seemed the natural refuge to her. Years ago she had found the
comfort of his quiet strength.

"Poor little girl! It was too much to ask of you! But you have
entertained my mother so well. She said to me that she wanted you to
talk on. I did not realise you were feeling it so. It is hard to see
her so helpless, but the doctors say that she is not in pain, and I am
very hopeful that she will recover her speech."

Then Tomina remembered herself, and drew away from him. Her effort to
regain her lofty dignity was almost comical.

"I don't know what I am doing! I am so upset. I never have been
accustomed to sick people, and she looks as if she is dead except for
her eyes. They haunt me!"

She shuddered and sobbed.

Colonel Norman drew her into his mother's boudoir, placed her in an
easy-chair, and left her.

That moment when she rested in his arms had been precious to him, but
he knew that he must not take advantage of her helplessness now. He
soon returned.

Tomina by this time had become her normal self again, though she looked
white and shaken.

"I am a nice person to visit an invalid!" she said as she stood up, and
declared she was quite well and must be going.

"Are you driving yourself?"

"Oh yes, of course I am. I hate being driven by any one else."

"Then I am afraid you will hate my doing it, but I am not going to let
you drive yourself back. I will speak to the nurse for a moment."

He went back to his mother's room.

"Now he's getting masterful!" said Tomina. "What a poor fool I am, to
behave like this!"

She looked round the pretty dainty room. There was Mrs. Norman's
writing-desk still open and her notes and letters still scattered
as she had left them. A piece of needlework was hanging out of her
work-basket. A novel was lying open where she had left it. Tomina
wondered that maids had not tidied the room; she did not know that
Colonel Norman had given orders that this room was to be untouched till
he had time to put things away.

"It's so awfully, so frightfully sudden," she murmured. "Her whole life
has been shattered and altered in a moment! And yet her son takes it
quite calmly. Can he really feel? I suppose men don't feel like women.
And it's a good thing that they do keep their heads. What must he have
thought of me losing mine so completely!"

She did not look at him as he re-entered the room. She had pulled down
her veil and felt quite composed, though her hands still slightly
trembled, and the Colonel was quick to note it.

"I will drive you back," he said. "You must let me do it, please."

Tomina said not a word. She meekly followed him downstairs. And when
they were driving off, he with the reins in his hand, she said slowly—

"I own I have brought this upon myself; if my nerves weren't all to
pieces, I should never have given way so ridiculously. But I really
ought not to have come."

"Oh, please don't say that! Don't rob my poor mother of the hope that
you will come again. She did enjoy your visit so."

"I can't come again," said Tomina, shortly. "I wonder that you can
expect it after the exhibition I have made of myself!"

"I am afraid we are all selfish," said Colonel Norman. "You are looking
at it from your side. I am looking at it from ours—and—" here a fit of
recklessness took possession of him—"I only wish you would make 'an
exhibition' of yourself, as you call it, a little oftener. I had a
happy five minutes, I can tell you!"

Tomina's eyes glowed.

"You ought to be ashamed of yourself!" she said passionately.

There was a tense silence between them.

Then he said very quietly—

"If tears have to be shed, Tomina, my only wish is that I could have
the drying of them. But we won't quarrel. You have been your sweet
little self to-day, and the memory of you in my mother's room will be
a pleasant one. If it pleases God for her to make a good recovery, I
think we may possibly go abroad. The doctor advises change of air and
atmosphere as soon as she is able to move. It will not fret her so much
if she is away from her surroundings at home. She has always been so
active—my poor little mother!"

"And who will look after your place?"

"I may let it and put an agent in; I have pulled things together a bit,
but it seems hopeless."

Tomina was a strangely contradictory person. After having carefully
kept out of Colonel Norman's way all this time, and refused to have
anything to do with him, the knowledge that she would soon see him no
more brought a forlorn, miserable feeling into her heart.

"I suppose," she mused, by way of explanation to herself, "that in a
quiet monotonous life like mine at present, even disagreeables have a
certain amount of excitement and zest!"

Colonel Norman went on talking.

"Mrs. Francis was telling me yesterday that she has some nieces who are
looking out for a house in this part. Their father has just died and
left them a good deal of money, and they want to take a furnished house
till they can find a place that suits them. She is going to write to
them about it."

"Oh," said Tomina, indifferently, "I dare say she'll be glad to have
them settled near her. I really think that if your mother has Mrs.
Francis to go and see her that will be quite sufficient company for
her. You mustn't expect me again."

"But I do," said Colonel Norman. "My mother and I will both count the
days till you come again."

"That is nonsense. Oh, here we are! Well, I'm much obliged to you. I
won't ask you in, because I know you will want to get back quickly."

Tomina sprang lightly out of the trap before the Colonel could get a
chance of helping her; then a blank look swept over her face.

"How are you going to get back? You'd better take the trap and get the
man at the inn to send for it."

"Oh no. I shall walk. I shall enjoy it. I haven't taken a walk for
three weeks or more. I'll leave the trap at the inn on my way."

Then he held out his hand.

"I can't thank you enough. I'm so sorry it distressed you, but it's
your warm heart that makes you feel it so. If you could come one day
next week, I should be grateful to you."

Tomina's objection died away on her lips.

"I'll try to come," she said, a little unsteadily.

And then she went indoors.

"He has to bear seeing her lying like that all day long, and she is his
mother. It won't hurt me to see her for half an hour; and I shan't have
a chance of going much longer."

"Why, ma'am, whatever have you been doing with yourself?" exclaimed
Susan.

Tomina dropped herself upon her comfortable chintz couch.

"I've been visiting the sick, Susan. It's the most awful thing that
I've ever done in my life. And if I had my way, I'd never do it again!"

"Oh, ma'am!" gasped Susan. "Why, some ladies just adore doin' it! My
Miss Wadhurst, she would be near as good as Mrs. Herbert herself in
goin' to the sick. You'll be meanin' Mrs. Norman, I reckon. Is she so
bad, ma'am?"

"I don't think she could be worse. Don't let us talk of it, Susan. She
haunts me. Has Harry come back yet?"

"Yes, and I've taken the liberty of locking him in his bedroom. He
put out his tongue at me and grabbed hold of two fresh buns I'd taken
straight out of the oven, and he ate them before my eyes when I told
him not to!"

"Very naughty!" said Tomina.

But she felt a longing for the child's merry talk and laughter, and
released him.

A little later, she and he were playing ball in the garden, and when
Susan begged her not to tire herself, she said—

"Anything to keep me from thinking, Susan! I do too much thinking for
my own comfort. Harry is my salvation."

So far Tomina had not been troubled with many visitors; if they came
once they did not come again, but young Tracy St. Vincent took to
calling very often now. He generally had some excuse, and because he
was different from the majority of the country squires, and because he
so humorously described and bewailed his various difficulties, Tomina
did not snub him, and he began to hope that his visits were more than
welcome. He came up to her one afternoon in tearing haste.

"Miss Gavin, take pity on me! My keeper wants me to preserve my
pheasants. My predecessor never did. The villagers have been making a
way through my woods to the church. It's a short cut, and my keeper
says I must close it. Do you know anything about the rights of way? My
gardener says I'll earn the ill-will of all my tenants if I do it. He
and the keeper are at daggers drawn—I don't know whom to please. My
life is becoming one big struggle to please everybody, and I end by
pleasing no one."

"Oh," said Tomina, carelessly, "I shouldn't worry! Please yourself
first; that is what I do. It answers best."

"I begin to find that you are a fraud," said Tracy, taking a seat on
the grass beside her chair.

She was sitting in the garden as usual. An open book was in her lap,
but she had not been reading. She found it even difficult now to bring
back her thoughts to her present company.

"You remind me," went on Tracy, "of a naughty child bent on
shocking her elders. Have you been brought up in a very strict
and priggish atmosphere? And don't you know that your character
in this neighbourhood is that of a very generous and self-denying
philanthropist?"

"It's a pity you believe all you hear," said Tomina. "My atmosphere
from childhood has been that of pleasure seeking and pleasure finding.
Do you mind steering clear of personal subjects? I'm rather sick of
myself to-day, and would like to be taken miles away."

Tracy felt snubbed.

"I want your help."

"But I'm afraid I can't give you it. You will never have any peace
until you decide upon your own course of action, without any reference
to outsiders."

"I believe you're right!" Tracy sat up. "It's hard lines on me! I'm not
at home in my surroundings. There's no getting away from that fact, and
that makes me diffident. Are you going to stay the winter here?"

"Oh yes, I think so," said Tomina. "I'm making an experiment, you know.
I must try a year at least."

"I want you to come over to lunch with me one day."

Tomina looked at him with twinkling eyes.

"A luncheon party?"

"No, of course not. I don't want anybody else."

"But there are conventions to be considered," said Tomina, "and I find
that if Mrs. Grundy has died in London, she's very much alive in the
country. I have learnt that in listening to my passengers' conversation
in the van."

"Oh, dash conventions! I'll ask Mrs. Francis to come, too. I wonder if
she would? I fight shy of her brother. He looks at me out of the corner
of his eye, as if I'm a counter-jumper."

"Do ask Mrs. Francis," said Tomina, sweetly; "she will be able to
advise you about everything. She knows this part. It is her county, but
it is not mine."

"All right, I will. Now, when can you come? Next Wednesday?"

"But I am not coming at all. I don't go out to luncheon parties."

Tracy stared at her. Then he stood up.

"You're annoyed to-day. I wonder who has been vexing you?"

Tomina laughed.

"I am dull, I own. I am not in a talking mood, but I am not annoyed
with any one. I never find it worth while."

"Oh, I wish I had an even temper!" said Tracy, a little viciously.
"But I haven't. I lose my temper fifty times a day. And I shall soon
wish for my town life again. Possessions and property bring a horrible
lot of worry. I'm not cut out for this job, as I've told you before. I
envy you your nest of a place here. I think a man can't run a big house
properly. It needs a woman."

"But you have a housekeeper."

"Yes, she's just arrived, and is a terror. She means to take me in
hand and reduce me to a state of pulp. I was very cross with her this
morning. I think it did her good. She altered my arrangements in my
study, the only room in which I feel at home, and said no gentleman
would live in such a pig-sty. I lost my temper and let her have it. I
do think you might come over one day. I want to alter the gardens. I'd
give anything for your advice."

"I'm not a gardener. Don't be huffy with me. I'm a regular
stay-at-home. Doctor's orders. If you get Mrs. Francis, she will be a
real help, for she's up in all that sort of thing and I'm not. I have
no hobbies. I sometimes wish I had."

"I don't want Mrs. Francis. I want you. I met her, by the way, as I
came along just now. She was walking with that stiff-starched Norman.
You know him, don't you? I somehow can't cotton to the men down here,
they're all such stick-in-the-muds!"

"Colonel Norman used to be a very keen soldier," said Tomina, with
rising colour, "and he is quite a town man. He was always up there a
few years ago."

"Yes, but this country makes us all go to sleep, doesn't it? All my
suggestions as regards innovations are squashed at once. If you live in
the country, you must be a sporting man; anything but sport is ejected
at once—"

Tomina stifled a yawn, and young Tracy saw it. He got up reluctantly.

"You are going to snub me now, like the rest of them," he said, trying
to look pathetic, "you won't come to my place, and you don't want me at
yours."

Tomina laughed.

"I own I am miserable to-day, but I assure you that you are a favoured
visitor. I generally say that I'm not 'at home' to any one, and you've
been to see me twice within the last week."

Tracy took his leave partially comforted by this view.

And when he had gone, Tomina heaved a sigh of relief.

"The only point of interest in his conversation was a disagreeable
one," she murmured to herself. "Why is every one bent on acquainting me
with the fact that 'she' is with 'him?' They are always together!"

CHAPTER XII

DAWN

TOMINA paid Mrs. Norman another visit. Colonel Norman was out, but she
got on better with the invalid when she was quite alone with her. Mrs.
Norman was decidedly better, she could nod or shake her head, and say
"Yes" and "No." It was not quite such a trying experience to Tomina as
her last visit.

And then, as Tomina was getting up to go, Colonel Norman came in, and
begged her to stay a few minutes longer.

"A storm is brewing," he said. "I conclude you have driven over."

"If a storm is coming, I mustn't wait a moment. I hate driving in the
rain."

As she spoke, a low rumbling roll of thunder in the distance was heard.
And then down came the rain. It reminded Tomina of the time when she
and Harry had climbed the old oak tree, and could not get down.

"Now you will have to stay," said Colonel Norman, firmly. "I see you
have had tea; is there a cup for me, I wonder?"

Tomina was still seated at the small tea-table which had been brought
her. She at once peeped into the teapot, and proceeded to give the
Colonel what he needed.

His eyes followed her every moment.

"I suppose," he said, "my mother has not been able to tell you about
our arrangements. We leave this next Tuesday. We shall have an invalid
carriage to town, and then will go straight through to Bordighera by
the 'Train de Luxe.'"

"Isn't it very soon for the Riviera? Surely you will find it very warm
there?"

"We may, but the great thing is to get to a place where we shall not be
obliged to move again. And it will be September next week."

"Yes," said Tomina, "the time has flown. I had no idea that a quiet,
monotonous life went so much quicker than a full one."

"Do you mean to stay the winter here?" asked Colonel Norman. "I can't
fancy you doing it."

"Yes, I am going to try a year of this country life."

"And then?"

Tomina looked at him and laughed.

"By that time, I am to be made into a new creature altogether. That is
my doctor's theory. If he is right, how can I tell you what the new
Tomina will do? Something quite different from what she has ever done
before, I assure you! And if my doctor proves wrong, and I continue to
be my old self, I shall pronounce all doctors and all country to be
frauds, and go back to my old life again."

Colonel Norman's eyes took the tender protective look in them that
Tomina could not bear to see. She turned her head away towards the
invalid.

"I hope you will continue to be your old self," he said.

"Now, aren't men contrary, Mrs. Norman. He couldn't bear me when I was
in town. Everything I did and said rubbed him up the wrong way. I could
never do right in his eyes, and we were both frantically anxious to
remove our paths in life as far from each other as we possibly could.
Fate has shaken us near each other again, and he pretends he likes it.
But Mother Fate knows him better than he knows himself, and is going to
be truly kind to us. Before you come home again, Colonel Norman, the
new or old Tomina will have vanished from this part, I promise you!"

"Don't make rash promises," said Colonel Norman, apparently quite
unperturbed. "'Fate' as you call her, is going to be on my side, I
fancy. Time flies fast, as you say, and the winter will come and go. It
cannot last for ever."

The storm increased in violence. Presently Colonel Norman took Tomina
down to the library. The drawing-room was an unused room since his
mother's illness.

Tomina sat in a big lounge chair, and he in another. She kept reminding
herself that as this would be the last time she would see her old
friend, she must be kind and civil to him now. And there was no lack of
conversation between them.

But when an hour passed, and the storm still raged, Tomina began to get
restive.

"I really must go home. Susan will wonder what has become of me."

"She will know that we have kept you. She knew, I suppose, where you
were coming?"

"I should rather think so. Susan considers that her first duty is
to look after me, and to be aware of all my plans and purposes and
correspondence. I never can be secretive, you know; I am too careless
and forgetful. She did not approve of my coming here at all, for she
knows that I am not a good visitor to sick people."

"Have you minded it? My mother does so enjoy your visits."

"Oh, I haven't minded it at all. I like it when I'm alone with her.
I can talk any nonsense I like, and there is nobody listening with a
critical ear to my poor little jokes and stories!"

"Don't mind me, Tomina, to-day. Let us do nothing—either of us—to spoil
the memory of this time."

Tomina was silent: she walked to the window and looked out. The rain
was coming down in a ceaseless pour.

"You will have to stay to dinner, and if it doesn't clear, we shall
keep you for the night."

Colonel Norman could hardly keep the glad exultancy out of his tone.
Fate was indeed treating him mercifully on this last day. His words
proved partly true.

Tomina did stay to dinner, and she sat opposite Colonel Norman at the
big table in the dining-room, with a queer feeling that it was her
rightful place.

He, poor man, was in the seventh heaven of delight. Tomina with her hat
off, and the little soft curls that he remembered so well half shading
her mischievous eyes, with her slim, graceful young figure in the old
carved oak chair, and her pretty gestures with her delicate little
hands, her gay laugh, her proud poise of head, and bewitching smiles,
it was enough to turn any man's head. What a fool he had been, he
assured himself. How bitterly he had repented of his rash injustice and
masterful intolerance!

But when dinner was over, Fate favoured him no more. The storm had
passed, and it was a still quiet evening. Tomina insisted upon having
the trap round. Colonel Norman eagerly said he must drive her back,
then remembered that this was the hour he always spent with his mother
before she retired for the night. For a moment, his mother and Tomina
were weighed in the balance. But Tomina decided the point herself.

"Your mother's nurse told me you always sat with her now. Do you think
for a moment that I would let you leave her in the lurch?"

Colonel Norman yielded.

"I will get my groom to go back with you, I shouldn't think of letting
you drive alone at this time of night."

They were standing together in the library. Tomina had already put coat
and hat on.

"This is our good-bye for some months, perhaps," said Colonel Norman,
very gravely, "but I shall not be always outside your life, Tomina,
shall I?"

His masterfulness was gone, and such wistfulness was in his voice and
eyes, that Tomina's eyes drooped.

When she raised them to his a moment later, they were misty with tears.
Both her hands were in his; she felt like a prisoner, but her pulses
throbbed and trembled, and Colonel Norman felt them do so.

"I don't know," she wavered. Then her flash of mischief came uppermost.
"You really are a little nicer than you used to be, but I'm in a
metamorphosis stage, as I told you, and I do not know what in the world
I shall be or what I shall be doing this time next year."

"Will you write to me?"

"Oh, how dreadful! I hate letters. You know what a bad correspondent I
always was!"

"Sometimes—once a month—let me hear."

"I couldn't. You are asking too much. Perhaps at Christmas I may send
you a letter. That is all I can promise."

"Thank you."

Colonel Norman raised her hands in an old-fashioned way to his lips.
Then he released her, and with swift, silent steps followed her out
into the hall and saw her comfortably wrapped up for her drive home.

"Good night," Tomina cried, as she started of down the drive; "and I
hope you'll have a pleasant and safe journey."

"I'll send you a line to tell of our arrival," called out the Colonel.

And Tomina's soft voice was carried through the evening air—

"Do!"


The next day, Tomina felt very depressed and irritable. But the day
after that was Friday, and she started to Damerton in her van, feeling
that there was something still left to live for.

When Tuesday came, and she knew that the objectionable Colonel was for
the time removed out of her sphere, she tried to make herself believe
that she was relieved and thankful. But after a time, she gave up the
struggle.

"Why should I keep up pretending lies to myself? I feel awfully sorry
for him. He will be such a devoted son, but it isn't natural for a man
to turn into a sick-nurse. He might just as well have stayed on here. I
think I should have become quite clever in talking to sick people. It
would have done me a lot of good. He was almost pathetic that stormy
evening, but it's just as well he went away. He might have persuaded me
against my will. And I should have lived to rue it. Once bitten, twice
shy! I am not going to place myself in abject subjection to any man on
earth, and that is the only 'rôle' that Gerald's wife would and could
play."


Time slipped quietly by. Summer went, and autumn came.

When the short dark days set in, Tomina decided that Harry would be
best at school. The house was small, and his restless activity began to
get upon her nerves. She and he parted with some tears, and his last
Sunday was quite a memorable day. He would not leave her side, and in
the afternoon cuddled up beside her on her roomy old couch, and talked,
as Susan said, more like an "angel child than a roguish scamp of a boy."

"If I never come back to you, Tommie," he said solemnly, "will you
promise faithfully you'll keep the next-door seat to you in heaven?
Because I can't never forget you, you know."

"But, you silly boy, you're coming to me in the holidays, or for a part
of them, I expect."

"Susan said you'd had enough of me."

"Susan didn't mean it. You've helped me a lot, Harry. If I ever get to
heaven, I'll tell you how much you did towards getting me there."

Harry opened his eyes wide.

"There's only One Person gets us there, as my 'Green Hill' says. Don't
you love the 'Green Hill,' Tommie? I learnt the last verse yesterday
and said it to Mrs. Francis. Shall I sing it to you? It sounds lovely
when it's sung."

Harry had a sweet little voice, and he lifted it up now, and the old
simple words of the child's hymn came home with a new power to Tomina's
heart, and stayed there.

When he came to the verse—

 "There was no other good enough
 To pay the price of sin;
 He only could unlock the gate
 Of heaven, and let us in,"

he paused and shook his curly head reflectively.

"You see," he said, "only Jesus keeps the key of heaven. I suppose,
Tommie, He could never lose it, could He? Wouldn't it be awful if it
couldn't be found when you and me wanted to get in?"

His eyes grew round with fright, as he imagined the terrible tragedy.
Then his brow cleared.

"Of course, Jesus could never lose anything, and if He does, He knows
just where to find it. He's much cleverer than the devil, isn't He,
Tommie? Granny used to say, I re'mber, that the devil himself must hide
her spectacles, for no mortal person could find them. But when I lose
my knife, I go straight to Jesus and ask Him to tell Satan to put it
back at once where he found it. And it always comes, always—"

"That's very wonderful!" said Tomina, smiling into Harry's earnest face.

"Yes, isn't it? But didn't Jesus lose a sheep once, Tommie? I think I
re'mber you reading me about Him finding a lost sheep?"

"Yes, we did read about it. It was a little parable of how people get
lost."

"Yes, but we simply can't get lost from God, for He's always close to
us."

Tomina did not answer; she was conning over in her mind the lines of
the child's hymn.

A dim and far-away light was beginning to dawn into her soul.

She sat still and watched its beams coming nearer. At last she roused
herself.

"I never learnt that hymn when I was a little girl, Harry. I was
brought up like a little heathen. I wonder if I could learn it now?"

"I'll fetch you the book," said Harry, with alacrity. "It's very easy
if you give your mind to it."

Tomina laughed at his old-fashioned air. But she took the hymn-book
from him.

And that night, when Harry was fast asleep, she scanned the verses over
to herself.


Harry left her early the next morning. Mrs. Francis came in her motor
for him, and drove him into Damerton.

And when he had gone, Tomina felt desolate indeed. She hardly touched
her lunch, and when Susan came in and remonstrated with her, she said—

"Eating is such a bore, Susan, and I'm feeling so low and flat that
nothing seems worth while to do."

"You're missing Harry, miss, but in a day or two, you'll get over that.
And if I may suggest it, a walk in the air would do you a power of
good. It's not often now we'll be getting such sun as there is this
afternoon."

Tomina looked out of her window. The sky was a bright blue with flecks
of white cloud chasing each other across it. The elms and beeches round
the church wore their warmest coats of red and yellow. A flock of
starlings was flying past.

"I'll go and see somebody, Susan. I'll go to dear old Mrs. Cayley.
She's the only good person that doesn't scare me."

She ran up the stairs. For a moment, the oppression of her spirit
seemed to lighten. Then, in a very short time, she came down in hat and
coat, and, whistling for Thor, she started off.

It was a mile's walk, but there was a touch of frost in the air and
it braced her. Twice on the way she stopped to speak to some country
people. There were very few of the poor who were strangers now to
Tomina; and she generally had a little joke and a laugh with them all.

She found Mrs. Cayley sweeping up the autumn leaves from the flagged
path outside her cottage.

"Mrs. Cayley, I'm in the blues. Do you think you can get me out of
them?" Tomina asked, as she leant her arms on the little wooden gate
and watched the old woman's quick, deft movements.

Mrs. Cayley had not noticed her approach. She started, then dropped the
old-fashioned curtsey that Tomina so loved to watch.

"Why, ma'am, 'tis nice to see your bright face. Come ye in. Ye be more
than welcome."

Tomina followed her into the bright little kitchen. It was as usual a
picture of neatness and cosiness.

Tomina took an old-fashioned rocking-chair by the fire, with a
wonderful cushion of patchwork upon it, and Mrs. Cayley went to her oak
dresser and began to lay the cloth for tea.

"I'm just going to have a cup, ma'am, and you must join me."

"I shall be delighted."

Tomina leant back in her chair, and watched the old woman with
thoughtful, wistful gaze.

"Oh, Mrs. Cayley, I wish—I wish I were you, with my life nearly
finished and all my troubles over, and my—my faith so untroubled and
sure!"

Mrs. Cayley was taking the kettle off the fire and pouring some water
into her brown teapot. She did not answer for a moment, then she said—

"Are things not going well, ma'am, with you?"

Tomina clasped her hands round her knees.

"I'm tired of everything and everybody, and of myself most of all, and
I'm 'bitterly' disappointed!"

"With what, ma'am?"

"Oh, with this doctor's experiment. I was sick and tired of life in
town, and had come to the end of it all. Then I was brought down here
to be buried for a time in view of beginning afresh—having a new
start. And the peace and the novelty and the quaintness of the country
satisfied me. My body has certainly benefited, but lately my mind has
been tormented with religious misgivings. They wear me out. And now
they're looming so big and so black that the peace of the country is
gone. Instead of feeling fresher, and beginning life anew, I'm feeling
a blot in creation. And I'm alone. I have nobody to help me. Harry has
gone, and—and others, and I've come to the end of everything. I just
felt this afternoon that I must come off to you and get help."

Mrs. Cayley looked at her in a puzzled way: but she never stopped her
occupation. She was now cutting thin slices of bread and butter from a
home-made loaf. And her next words were a request that Tomina should
have some tea before any further talk.

Tomina drew up her chair to the table, some golden honey in the comb
was produced, and she thoroughly enjoyed the repast.

At first, Mrs. Cayley was going to wait upon her, but Tomina insisted
upon her joining her at the table.

Presently, Mrs. Cayley said—

"Didn't you like the Rector's sermon yesterday morning, ma'am?"

"I don't think I did. I don't care much for sermons. In London, I
always used to leave when the sermon began, and yesterday my mind
wandered. I can't even remember the text!"

"I always think the sermon be the best part of church," said Mrs.
Cayley. "'Tis the best half, surely. The first half I always think is
we speakin' to God, and the last half be God speakin' to us."

Tomina paused in conveying her cup to her lips to consider this.

"You think that God speaks through the preacher? It never struck me so."

"He spoke to me. I come home yesterday and sat here thinking it out.
'Twas beautiful, so clear and simple. The text was, 'Ask, and ye shall
receive.' It do seem to take in all we need. And I think you would find
it so, ma'am. I don't rightly understand your feelin's; young ladies
like you have a bigger life and more wants than we. But your askin'
can't be too big for the Almighty."

Tomina faced her old friend with earnest eyes.

"It's like this, Mrs. Cayley. Every one has been hammering away at me
since I came here, and I see that there is quite a different life to be
lived in this world from that which I have been living. So the long and
short is, I'm going to live it, if I can. Harry has been making me read
the New Testament a good deal; I suppose it has a fascination of its
own. I know I've learnt to love the—Chief Character in it. I want to
follow Him as they did in those times. But I don't seem to get at the
way to do it. And I've a wasted good-for-nothing life behind me. Not
worth much, am I?"

"Worth nothing at all," said Mrs. Cayley, sitting with clasped hands in
her lap and with almost an inspired glow in her eyes. "That's why we
come to the dear Lord, and ask Him to take us and make something of us.
'Tis just 'Ask, and ye shall receive,' after all."

Tomina drew in a long breath.

"That sounds simple. Harry's 'Green Hill' hymn explains it all
beautifully. I know that 'He died to save us all,' and that 'He died
that we might be forgiven.' 'That we might go at last to heaven, saved
by His precious blood.' It's all there convincing to one's head, but
I've got no further."

"'Ask, and ye shall receive,'" repeated Mrs. Cayley again, with a
smile. "You've never come near enough to Him, ma'am, perhaps. I minds
years ago, time of my confirmation, when I come to see that I must come
myself to the Throne o' Grace, and ask for all my needs. Just try,
ma'am, ask for what you want, and you'll get it without any waiting. If
'tis anything concernin' our souls, don't you think the Loving Father
be more in a hurry to give it to us than we are to get? Ask and take,
that be our part. He does all the rest."

Tomina sat very silent, drinking in from this poor hard-working old
woman the truths that no clever divine could put more forcibly and
clearly. She did not say much more about her difficulties, but began to
talk of other subjects.

When she wished Mrs. Cayley good-bye, she said—

"You've helped me tremendously this afternoon."

"'Twas only the text our Rector spoke on, ma'am."

"Yes, I missed a lot by not listening. It's a very practical command,
Mrs. Cayley. I see light now."

She walked home, and every step of the way she was repeating to herself
with increasing emphasis—

"'Ask, and ye shall receive.'"

CHAPTER XIII

THE FIRST EFFORT

"IF you please, ma'am, are you at home?"

Tomina was writing a letter to her beloved brother.

"Who is it, Susan?" she asked.

"'Tis Miss Merryweather."

"Oh yes, let her come in."

And Tomina greeted her visitor with unusual warmth.

"I haven't seen you for ages."

"No, I respect your wish for solitude, and I'm that way myself. But as
I'm going back to town next week, I thought I would like to wish you
good-bye."

"Then you're a perfect cure?"

Alice Merryweather laughed.

"Yes, I can say I am. An idle summer ought to have cured me. I sleep
better, and feel able to tackle all my work again."

"And do you like going back?"

"I love it. Sweet stagnation does not suit me. I chafe and get restive.
I am tingling to be up and doing. How are you? You are looking very
well."

"Yes, but I am not ready to go back to town. You see, I have no work
awaiting me there, only pleasure. It makes a difference."

"So you are going on with your carrier work? Won't you find it very
cold in the winter?"

"I dare say. I'm going to begin to court difficulties now, having
evaded them all my life."

There was something so radiant and alert in Tomina's tones, that Alice
looked at her in a puzzled sort of way.

"Do you remember advising me to cultivate my spiritual part?" asked
Tomina, suddenly. "You told me how to do it."

"Oh, surely not!"

Alice looked quite astonished.

"Yes, you told me to read the Bible, and I did—and I have been slowly
learning ever since. I don't seem able to talk about these things yet,
but I'd like you to know that I've never been so happy in my life as
I am now. It seems as if God has reached right down from heaven and
taken me by the hand. I've been in a great worry about myself for a
long time, but dear old Mrs. Cayley gave me a very simple solution to
my worries. I was worrying over my worthlessness and inability to be
unselfish, and she told me just to ask for what I wanted, so I asked
for a few big things—forgiveness for the past, and for my soul to be
given fresh life, and to be helped to be, as the children put it,
'good.' It has all been given me, I do believe. Isn't it wonderful?"

Alice sat silent. Tomina's glowing enthusiasm touched her.

"I'm so glad," she said slowly.

"I knew you would be. I never thought I would ever be enthusiastic
about religion. But it's a new life, and I love, I love our Master."

Tomina's eyes were shining softly through a mist of tears as she spoke.
Then she said more lightly—

"I've confided in you, because you are one of those who have helped me,
and I'm sorry you are going."

"You won't stay here very long," said Alice. "I am sure the winter in
the country will prove too much for you."

"Perhaps it may," said Tomina, quietly; "I don't feel so sure of
myself as I used to do. And I shall want to make up for a good deal of
lost time. But I'm not going to give up my vocation. Dr. Merton told
me I could have no better one. I hope I shall always be 'Tomina the
Carrier.'"

Alice knew there was meaning underlying her words, but she did not
remark upon them, and her visit was very short. She and Tomina parted
with regret, for though they seldom met, they mutually liked each other.

When she had gone, Tomina sat by her bright little fire with rather a
sober face. One by one her friends seemed leaving her—Colonel Norman,
Harry, and now Alice Merryweather.

She was thinking of her 'rôle' as carrier, and wondering in what way
she could widen and deepen that vocation, when her eye fell upon a
picture that hung upon the wall and which belonged to Harry. It had
been given to him by Mrs. Francis, and represented the Christ Child
upon the shoulders of St. Christopher as he waded through the river.

A sudden light flashed into her face.

"Delicious! St. Christoforos, the Carrier, for that is what he was!
Giving up his great strength to the humble service of carrying the
feeble over the stream of life. And in doing it, he found he was
carrying his Master. And I can copy his example, and try to carry some
burdens for the weak and heavy-laden ones, and the children; I haven't
to go away from here to do that. My passengers in my van alone will
give me plenty of scope."

She still sat on by the fire, but there was a happy glow in her heart,
and she longed at once to put her thoughts into practice.


The very next Friday, as she went into Damerton, something occurred
which made her wonder if it possibly might mean an opportunity for her.
About three miles out of Damerton, they overtook a girl plodding along
wearily in front of them.

Tomina called out brightly to her—

"Would you like a lift?"

And then she saw as she turned round that she was not an ordinary
country girl. She had the appearance of a lady, and her voice was as
soft and refined as Tomina's own.

"I should very much," she said.

Tomina made room for her on the front seat by her, and asked her if she
had been walking long.

"Only from Dutterton. We have lately come there, but it is nearly four
miles from Damerton, and I was wondering how I could walk both ways. My
cycle is broken, and I am obliged to come."

She was a nice-looking girl, but was evidently in trouble; her eyes
were red with crying, and she was very unhappy.

"Is your home near here?" Tomina asked idly.

"Oh, I wish it were! I live in the north of Yorkshire. My father was a
clergyman, and he died last year. I am the eldest, and I was engaged to
be a companion to a Mrs. Cuthbert. She has lately taken a house near
here. I have been with her over a year, and I was looking forward to
going home for a month's holiday. Two of my brothers are going out to
New Zealand. I shall not be able to say good-bye to them now."

"Why?"

"Mrs. Cuthbert's maid has gone home ill; and she can't spare me, she
says."

Then Tomina's sympathetic face drew out more of the girl's story. She
had a large family of brothers and sisters, and they had been much
looking forward to being all together before the brothers left home.
Mrs. Cuthbert was a hard and exacting employer, but she paid her a
good salary, and in the absence of her maid expected her to do all her
duties as well as her own.

"I am now going into Damerton to get a library book. I went in
yesterday, but Mrs. Cuthbert did not like my choice and I have to
change it. I sometimes feel that I cannot go on. I never seem able
to please her. Please forgive my grumbling, but I am so bitterly
disappointed that I cannot get home. I am just homesick!"

"I wish I could help you," said Tomina. "Can't you get somebody to take
your place for a month?"

"Oh, I wish I could! But who would do it? And Mrs. Cuthbert might not
allow it."

"I'll see if I can hear of any one," said Tomina, cheerfully. "You
don't know how many people I meet, or know through this van. Let me
have your address, and I'll write if I hear of any one. I don't know
your name yet. Christabel Avenel? What a pretty name!"

When they reached Damerton, Tomina asked her if she could wait till the
return journey. But she could not do that, and they parted the best of
friends.

"Susan," said Tomina that evening just before she went to bed, "if I
went away for a month, could you carry on here, and take care of Thor?"

Susan looked very surprised.

"But what about the van?"

"Ah!" said Tomina. "That's a difficulty, isn't it? But I could trust
Joe to take my place for a few weeks, and you could go in the van
occasionally just to see that everybody was comfortable."

Susan smiled.

"You do be an uncommon carrier, ma'am. I'm sorry you're going back to
London."

"I'm not going near it. I'm—I'm just going to pay a visit in the
neighbourhood—if I'm allowed."

Susan asked no more questions. Tomina continually astonished her by
her rapid movements. But when she carried two letters to the post that
evening in time for the local morning delivery, she could not forbear
glancing at the envelopes. One was addressed to—

 "Mrs. Cuthbert,
 White Gables, Dutterton."

The other to—

 "Miss Christabel Avenel,
 White Gables, Dutterton."

Susan shook her head, for she was none the wiser. And the post-office
woman could not enlighten her.

"New town people, I reckon. There be so many of them taking up houses
nowadays in this part of the country."

In two days' time, Tomina received answers to her letters. Mrs.
Cuthbert was willing to accept her services, provided she gave her a
personal interview, and it proved satisfactory. Christabel wrote in the
seventh heaven of delight.

Then Tomina took Susan into her confidence and told her what she meant
to do, and Susan did not know whether to laugh or to be horrified.

"My dear ma'am, you can't even lace your own boots, or mend your own
stockings. You've always been waited on, hand and foot. How on, you
wait on a lady? It would be so demeaning."

"But I am not going as her servant; I am going as a companion and
friend."

Susan gave a little sniff.

"Those poor young ladies are worked harder than any servant. I've seen
ladies' companions, and I know how they're worked."

"Well," said Tomina, "if work has been strange to me all my life, the
sooner I make acquaintance with it the better. So you must wait upon me
no more, Susan. I must get into training. I shall not be able to take
you with me, so I must learn to do without you now."

"But I love waiting on you, ma'am, and you really can't do things like
my Miss Wadhurst could."

"I can try."

And "try" Tomina did. She laughed at her own clumsiness, and more than
once Susan had to come to her aid. But day by day she grew more apt
in waiting upon herself and in tidying and mending. Then she went off
to Dutterton to interview the old lady. She dressed in her plainest,
neatest attire, and began to enjoy the novel prospect before her.

She was shown into a library by a very important old butler, and
presently Mrs. Cuthbert sailed into the room. At the first glance,
Tomina's spirits quailed, for here was no aristocratic old lady, but
one who had evidently acquired her wealth late in life.

Mrs. Cuthbert was the widow of a City man who had once been Lord Mayor
of London. And she lived still on the past glories of that wonderful
year.

"I understand you're a friend of my young companion. Have you brought
any references?" was her opening inquiry.

"Do you mean a character?" asked Tomina, with a blank look of dismay.

"I mean I can 'ardly take a young person into my household without the
assurance that she is honest and respectable."

"Oh yes—yes, certainly. I have several friends—people who will write to
you to that effect."

"I want a good-tempered, obliging girl, who will do 'everything' she is
told."

"Yes," murmured Tomina, not liking the small black eyes that were fixed
so unwaveringly upon her.

"My maid has left me, so I shall want extra assistance with my toilet.
Do you understand dressing the coiffure?"

Tomina glanced at the coils of black plaits that mounted in pyramid
form upon her head surmounted with a handsome tortoiseshell comb and a
pink ribbon bow.

"I should try my best to suit you," she said meekly.

A few more questions were put to her, and then the old lady rose.

"I 'ope your references will be satisfactory, and then you can commence
your duties with me next Tuesday. It is a great kindness, I consider,
in allowing the young person who is with me to leave at this juncture;
but I always was soft-'earted to my own detriment."

Tomina bowed and withdrew, but she was seized hold of by Christabel on
the way down the drive.

"Oh, how can I thank you! I do hope you will be able to bear with her.
I never, never thought you would be willing to take my place."

"It will do me all the good in the world," said Tomina, brightly. "I
shall be on my mettle to supply your place satisfactorily. But I am
rather frightened of your old lady."

"As long as you never object to anything she says to you, you will get
on," said Christabel, earnestly.

Tomina looked, and felt more than doubtful about her capability in that
way, but she said nothing to damp Christabel's delight.

The next step was to get a reference. Tomina wrote to Dr. Merton that
same night.

 "MY DEAR DOCTOR,

 "Will you just sit down and write me a testimonial for honesty,
sobriety, and respectability? I assure you that you can safely do it,
but I should be glad of it by return of post.

 "Yours sincerely,

 "TOMINA GAVIN."


And the next morning, before she had finished breakfast, brought the
doctor in person to her gate.

"I really must have a few more details," said Dr. Merton. "Will you
kindly tell me what you propose doing? For I still consider you as
under my charge."

"I think I'm a cure by this time," said Tomina; "I feel a new creature
at last—through and through."

There was meaning in her last words. Dr. Merton looked keenly at her.

"I congratulate you," he said.

Then she told him quite simply of her plan.

"It's folly!" he exclaimed. "You are the last person in the world to
carry through a thing of that sort satisfactorily. It will be a dead
failure."

"You are very unkind. Did you not advise me to become a carrier and
stick to that vocation through life? I'm only going to try to carry a
burden of another girl's for a month!"

"But it is too big a one for you," said Dr. Merton.

"She is no stronger than I am; she bears it all the year round. Don't
dishearten me. In my heart of hearts I'm so afraid I may prove a
failure, and I do want to be a success."

She spoke so sweetly and wistfully that Dr. Merton surrendered at once.

"Let me have a sheet of paper," he said. And sitting down, he scribbled
off the desired reference.

Then he looked at her with a smile.

"You have found your feet," he said, "but don't try to run before you
can walk."

She laughed, then grew grave.

"I do feel in some ways just born. If it were spring, I should say my
sap was rising. Oh, Dr. Merton, what a lot I have to unlearn, and to
learn! Do you remember your wish for me a short time ago—to be reformed
and transformed inside and out? Rather a big wish, wasn't it? But not
too big for God Who made us. So I asked Him to make a new creature of
me altogether, and—and I really believe He has begun to do it."

Dr. Merton stood up; then he took her hand in his.

"No wonder you look so happy!" he said. "If you've got a new centre in
your life, you'll be all right where you're going, and don't forget to
pass your good things on."

His grip of her hand left it aching, but Tomina's heart glowed afresh
at his words.

She sent off her reference, and heard from Mrs. Cuthbert that she would
be expected in two days' time.

Then Tomina completed her arrangements for leaving her cottage. Joe was
