proud to under take the carrier business without her; and then Tomina
tackled Susan.

"Now, Susan, you must please keep your mouth closely shut about my
absence. Just say I have gone away for a visit, for that is strictly
true. I won't have Mrs. Herbert or any one else know the reasons of my
going."

"You may come back at the end of a week, I'm hopin'," said Susan,
cheerfully. "I'm quite certain, ma'am, 'tis an impossible place for
you."


It was a bright cold afternoon when Tomina had herself and her luggage
driven over to the White Gables. She arrived there as it was getting
dusk. The portly butler looked at her a little disapprovingly, but
showed her into the drawing-room, where Mrs. Cuthbert was sitting
dozing over a huge fire. She put out a fat and limp hand.

"Glad to see you. Miss Avenel left me this morning, and I almost wished
I had detained her. She knows where all my things are. I want some
more wool for my knitting now. You must go up to my room and find it
somewhere. In my boudoir it is, I think. Ask the housemaid to tell you
where it is. She'll show you your room, but I'm wanting that wool, so
as soon as you 'ave removed your walking things, come down with it to
me."

Tomina obediently made her way up the soft-carpeted stairs. Everything
on all sides betokened money and luxury, but everything was too new to
obtain favour in her sight. Her bedroom was, thoroughly comfortable,
but its glaring paper and gaudy cheap coloured prints on the walls
offended her eyes, and the furniture was showy and pretentious. The
housemaid who showed her upstairs was rather pert.

"I wish you joy of your job," she said. "I wouldn't be Mrs. Cuthbert's
companion for a hundred pounds. This door behind the curtain here leads
into her room. It's never locked, so as she may come in and catch you
out if she can."

"Where do you think I can find some wool for her?" Tomina asked
quietly, turning round upon the girl with one of her smiles.

Gertrude from that moment became her staunch ally.

"I can make a good guess. Didn't she give you her keys? Then there's
only one drawer unlocked in her room."

She led the way into Mrs. Cuthbert's handsome room. A roaring fire was
in the grate. Half the room was taken up with a massive carved mahogany
four-post bed, draped in crimson satin; a couch upholstered in blue
cretonne covered with large pink roses stood at the foot of it. The
amber-coloured lace curtains to the three windows were tied back with
crimson satin bows. There were two big wardrobes, and in the bottom
drawer of one of these the wool was found.

"Thank you very much," Tomina said to Gertrude, as she pulled open the
drawer. "You have saved me a search."

"All right, and you just give me a call down the back stairs if you
want anything. 'Tis rather awkward for you at first. You have a
dreadful time in front of you, I warn you!"

"Oh, I mean to be very happy and comfortable here, I assure you," said
Tomina, and she laughed gaily as she spoke.

Gertrude stared at her, then muttered as she left the room—

"You'll sing a different song by this time to-morrow."

Tomina ran lightly downstairs to the drawing-room with the wool. Her
bright smiling face, as she came into the room, struck Mrs. Cuthbert
as unusual. She looked at her suspiciously, ready to discover, if she
could, the reason for her smile.

"Sit down in that chair, and wind me off two skeins as quick as you
can. And I'll tell you some of your duties. We have tea at five
o'clock, after which you read aloud to me till the first dressing-gong
sounds. Then you go up and put out my dinner dress. To-night I 'ave the
rector and his wife dining here. I like to give them a good dinner now
and then, poor souls! Not that I feed myself down in the country as I
used to do in town."

She gave a fat sigh.

"If you give me satisfaction, I'll let you look at some of our 'menoos'
the year I was Lady Mayoress in London. I've kept them all. We 'ad
the highest in the land to our dinners, I can tell you, and the gold
plate and the powdered footmen, and all the ceremonial pomp was beyond
description! What was I telling you?—Oh, you go up and get ready for
my toilet. When I have visitors, you have your dinner in the library,
that is where I allow you to sit, and there's generally some mending or
making to be done. Miss Avenel began to embroider my initials on some
sheets, but she is a slow worker. I 'ope you will be quicker."

Tomina was both interested and amused. At present, this was but a game
of play to her. She was awkward at the wool winding, but was quite
ready to talk.

"I'll do my very best to supply Miss Avenel's place satisfactorily,"
she said, "but I am not a very clever needlewoman. And I shall be
very glad to dine alone when you have visitors. I'm not very sociably
inclined. I—"

"Perhaps you'll kindly listen to the list of your duties, instead of
talking."

Mrs. Cuthbert's tone was crushing. Tomina was mute.

"I retire to bed at ten o'clock. I take a cup of Benger's food the last
thing, and my back is rubbed with liniment. I am a great sufferer from
rheumatism."

"Do I rub your back?"

Tomina's dismayed tone brought an extra touch of sternness in her
employer's voice.

"Most certainly you do, and anything and everything that I tell you to
do whilst you are in my service."

There was a moment's silence, then the butler appeared with the
tea. Tomina poured it out and waited upon Mrs. Cuthbert with pretty
deference, but all the time she was saying to herself—

"I draw the line at the liniment. How can I stay here?"

CHAPTER XIV

TOM'S NEWS

TOMINA found, before many days were over, that she had to do many
things that were most distasteful to her. It was a startlingly new
experience to her to have to stand when she was tired, and to have her
night's rest disturbed by having to keep in Mrs. Cuthbert's fire.

To go to the village on an errand when it was pouring with rain, to
wash and get up lace, to mend stockings, to wash hair brushes, to
be on her feet up and down stairs for sometimes an hour at a time,
looking for something that reposed safely in Mrs. Cuthbert's pockets.
And through it all, to meet with real rudeness and despotic tyranny.
She took it at first lightly, treating it all as a huge joke; then she
accepted it as wholesome discipline; and finally found herself praying
hourly for grace to control her tongue.

Mrs. Cuthbert was an adept at keeping her young companion busy. And
it was doubly unfortunate for Tomina that the maid should be away, as
she was obliged to perform the latter's duties as well as her own. She
confessed to herself that she was an awkward substitute to Christabel
Avenel, and often felt that Mrs. Cuthbert was justified in her anger.
But she had never before come in contact with any one who was so openly
rude and abusive as Mrs. Cuthbert, and sometimes even her serene temper
was ruffled.


One wet afternoon, Mrs. Cuthbert called her into the drawing-room.

"You can bring your needlework with you. I sometimes feel I shall get
an imbecile if I live 'ere much longer. I never thought the country
could be so deadly dull, or I would never have listened to my doctor
when he advised me to try the country for my 'ealth. I always fancied
country neighbours dropped in upon each other in a friendly 'omely
way, but leaving out our Rectory folk, and the doctor, I 'aven't
had a single caller this month. When I think of the 'undreds who I
entertained at the Mansion 'Ouse, and 'ow pleased they were to make my
acquaintance, I could have a good cry over myself 'ere. 'Tis only an
experiment; I shall move back to town. I'm at 'ome there."

"It's funny how many people have come down here for their health," said
Tomina. "Miss Merryweather and I were both supposed to be invalids."

Mrs. Cuthbert ignored this remark. She was in a conversational mood,
and went on grumbling at the quiet and dullness of the country.

"Well," said Tomina at last, "I know there's one thing that could make
you happy. And then it wouldn't matter where you lived. If you were on
a desert island, you would be quite cheerful and contented."

"Don't beat round the bush so," said Mrs. Cuthbert. "Say right out what
you mean."

"It's just to take God at His Word," said Tomina, letting her work drop
in her lap and looking at Mrs. Cuthbert with soft dreamy eyes. "I've
done it, and it's like opening dark shutters and letting the sun flood
you with its warmth and brightness. What does it matter where we live,
when we are surrounded by His love!"

Mrs. Cuthbert gazed at her with astonished eyes.

"I consider it's very bad taste to talk religion in a drawing-room, so
don't you do it, Miss Gavin. And most impertinent of a young girl like
you to be setting yourself above those twice your years and experience.
When I was Lady Mayoress, I used to go to numbers of religious
meetings, and some of the 'ighest preachers in the land have dined with
me. Bishops I've 'ad, and they never presumed as you have done."

"I wanted you to be happy," said Tomina, earnestly. "I have been so
discontented and restless myself that I want everybody to know the cure
for it."

"Are you meaning to insinuate that I am discontented and restless?"
said Mrs. Cuthbert, rearing her head haughtily. "I never heard such
insolence in my life! And this is what I get by being a bit sociable
with you! You'll please to hold your tongue in future, Miss Gavin, and
give me no more of your impertinent allusions and opinions!"

Tomina sighed.

"What a hardened old piece of pomposity!" she murmured to herself. "And
what a bully she is!"

Mrs. Cuthbert's rude speeches never touched Tomina, she felt above and
beyond them. But a wave of sympathy and pity for Christabel Avenel came
over her.

"How I wish I could get her some other post! But if she did not come
back, I suppose some other girl would take her place, and I dare say
Christabel wants the money."

Tomina had been with Mrs. Cuthbert three weeks, when one evening a
telegram came to her. It was from her brother Tom, saying he had just
arrived home for three weeks' leave, and asking her if she could meet
him in town. She was beside herself with joy and excitement. Her first
impulse was to rush to Mrs. Cuthbert and say that she must leave her
that very day. Then she thought of Christabel, who would be so bitterly
disappointed if she would have to return and be robbed of her last
week's holiday.

For the first time in her life a battle waged in Tomina's soul between
duty and inclination.

"I must see Tom. I wouldn't waste a day of his leave. We haven't seen
each other for four years. But I mustn't fail Christabel. It's the
first effort I have made to do something unpleasant for the good of my
neighbour. It's my first burden that I am carrying. St. Christoforos
would not have dropped his first passenger in the river because he
wanted to do something else. But oh, my darling Tom, I cannot give up
one of our precious weeks together! What am I to do?"

She sat with the telegram in her hand, and then determined to appeal to
Mrs. Cuthbert. She found her in her conservatory, walking round it and
talking to the gardener there.

"May I speak to you?" Tomina said meekly. "I have had a wire from my
brother."

Mrs. Cuthbert stepped back into the drawing-room.

"What is it?" she asked curtly.

"He has come home from Australia and has three weeks' leave. He wants
me to join him at once in London. Do you think you could manage without
me for just this week? Isn't your maid coming back soon?"

"I cannot possibly let you leave me. I wonder at your asking such a
question. My maid is not returning at all. She has written to tell me
so. I should think your brother can do without you for a week."

"He is my only brother, we haven't seen each other for four years.
Oh, do let me go! Of course, I wouldn't be the cause of bringing Miss
Avenel back for worlds, but I really think you could manage without
either of us for just this week."

It was absolutely useless trying to make Mrs. Cuthbert give way. She
broke into a torrent of angry declamations, and almost drove Tomina out
of her presence.

And so with a few tears, Tomina wrote as following to her brother:—

 "MY OWN DEAREST BOY,

 "When I had your wire I nearly stood on my head for joy, and now I am
in the deepest depths of woe, for I can't come to you, Tom dear, for a
week, and I can't ask you to come to me. I am staying with an irascible
old lady, taking the place of her companion, a friend of mine, and she
won't let me go. My time is up to-morrow week. Till then I must curb my
impatient spirits. I have told you all about my retreat, and I really
think it would be more fun for both of us if you came down to my cot.
I've promised the doctors to stay in the country a year—and you and I
would be very snug together. How I wish I could leave my body with my
old lady, and betake my soul to you, but a week will pass, won't it?
Till then. Your own

 "TOMINA."

She got another wire after the receipt of this letter:—

 "All serene. Will come down next Wednesday.

 "Tom."

Tom was never good at letters. That week was the hardest and longest
that Tomina had ever passed. She performed her duties in a dreamy
mechanical way. Even Mrs. Cuthbert wished she would be more talkative.
Tomina's bright serene temper was valued more than she thought, and
when at last the day for her departure came, Mrs. Cuthbert was almost
gracious. Then, like a bolt from the blue, came Tomina's parting words—

"I've done my best, but I know I have been very awkward in many ways.
You will be glad to have Miss Avenel back again. Now, will you, dear
Mrs. Cuthbert, try to be kinder to her? Remember she is flesh and blood
like yourself, and has feelings. It is not a happy post to be always
trying to please somebody who won't be pleased. You will be happier
yourself if you make her happy. Treat her as a daughter. Why should you
not? Good-bye!"

She was gone before Mrs. Cuthbert could recover her breath from the
shock her audacity had given her.


"Free! Free! Free!"

Tomina almost sang the words to herself as the trap drove her home. It
was a dull, grey afternoon in December, but she cared little for her
outlook. Tom was coming to her that evening and she must be home in
time to welcome him. Susan was waiting at the gate when she arrived,
and though it was already dusk, several doors opened in the village
street, and women's heads nodded cheerfully at one another as the trap
drove past.

"She's back. Our young lady is back!"

Tomina almost danced into the house.

"Oh, what a cosy fire; how delicious to be home! Oh, Susan, what a
lot I have to tell you! And what a useful member of society I have
become! Do you know I can make Benger's Food, and trim caps, and wash
handkerchiefs, and rub rheumatic shoulders, and light fires when they
go out, and darn stockings! Oh, there is no end to my accomplishments.
And now have you got my brother's room ready? Let me see it. He will be
here directly, and, Susan, you will lose your heart to him! There never
was his equal in the world and there never will be!"

So Tomina chatted and laughed. And then she sat down suddenly and
turned so white that Susan thought that she was going to faint.

"I'm only rather tired. I was up at five o'clock this morning trying
to get my packing done. I was given no time to do it, you see, and you
know how stupid I am over packing."

"You look a perfect shadow, ma'am. I knew you'd be just wore out. Sit
down and let me take off your boots."

"Just to-day, then, but I'm going to turn over a new leaf and do
everything for myself. Oh, Susan, I could hug you, for your dear, kind
face! Do you know I haven't had one kind word from anybody for a whole
month? Yes, I have—the little housemaid befriended me."

Susan waited on her hand and foot, and then, when Tomina was
comfortably established in her cheerful sitting-room, she brought her
in tea and insisted upon her having a cup at once.

Tomina felt better after that, and began to tell Susan of some of her
experiences, which, now that they were over, seemed very amusing.

Then the sound of a trap was heard. Tomina rushed to the door, and the
next moment was in the arms of her beloved twin brother. He was not
much like her, except in his eyes, which twinkled as irrepressibly and
mischievously as Tomina's.

"Oh!" she said. "How good you smell! It's your serge! It holds the salt
sea in it! It does seem delicious to have a man in the house again. Now
sit down. Do you still condescend to tea?"

"Tea! Yes. They never know how to make good tea at sea. Well, Tomina,
how goes it? You're rather a fraud. What are you doing in this
ungetatable place? I expected to find you in some kind of rest cure.
The 'mater' said you refused to see her or any one, but she did hear
from somebody that you weren't so bad as you made out. So to-morrow
prepare to see a party descend upon you. They're coming in their car."

"Oh, Tom, how horrible! I want you to myself. I've such oceans to tell
you! Who is it that will be coming?"

"Some one I want you to like. I've got news for you, but it will keep.
I've got a heavy thirst on, so be prepared to give me five cups at
least!"

Tomina poured out his tea with a perturbed look. Then her brother
laughed at her.

"My news will keep. Tell me how your van is going! That was very
sporting of you! I hope I shall be introduced to it."

"Oh, Tom, I have such shoals to tell you! But I must hear your news
first. I must! You have shaken my very foundations. Don't tell me
you're going to marry!"

Tom nodded at her, and his eyes glowed as he said—

"To the most charming girl in the world, and she's coming down
to-morrow to make your acquaintance."

The blow had fallen. Tomina shivered; then she braced herself to bear
it, and smiled one of her sweetest smiles.

"And who is she, Tom? I must know that first before I congratulate you."

"You have never met her. She has been out in Australia for the last
five years. Her brother has a Government billet in Queensland. They
entertained a lot, and were awfully good to me. She's much too good for
me, but that's her look-out, I tell her. And she came home a month ago,
and she's getting a married sister to motor her down here to-morrow."

"They'll never be able to get up here, if they come by the London
Road," said Tomina, slowly.

"I say, Tomina, don't be a squelcher! You've often advised me for my
good."

"Yes—yes," said Tomina, with feverish haste. "I'm so glad for you, but
I always think a naval man's married life must be so disappointing; and
I thought you had the makings of a bachelor!"

"You hoped I had," laughed Tom. Then he stood up, his hands in his
pockets. "Wait till you see her. I was clean bowled over, and you'll
not be surprised. Now prepare for shock number two. We're going to be
spliced next week; and I've applied for a week's further leave."

"Oh, Tom, you wretch! And I've lost one week of you, and I suppose
you'll only give me a few days."

Tears were crowding into Tomina's eyes. She felt as if she were losing
the one she loved best on earth; she knew that nothing would ever be
quite the same again.

Tom looked at her uncomfortably; then he turned and gazed out of the
window.

"You've an awfully pretty view here," he said.

Tomina got up from her seat, and put her arms round her brother's neck.

"Give me time, Tom dear, you know I want you to be happy, but you have
sprung a mine upon me! I shan't say a word till after I have seen her.
I'm sure she must be nice, or you wouldn't have chosen her. I used to
laugh at your fastidiousness as far as girls were concerned, didn't I?
Come and sit down and yarn to me as you always do. And then I'll yarn
back. It is good to have you here."

So brother and sister began to talk. There was much to tell on both
sides, and they talked hard till past twelve o'clock that night.

When Tomina at length got to bed, she was too tired to sleep. And Tom's
news was a greater trouble to her than she would allow even to herself.

"It was bound to come. I only pray she may be good enough for him. I
won't be selfish. I would not have him live and die a bachelor."

But though she comforted herself with this view, she spent a restless
night, and came to the breakfast table the next morning with dark blue
lines under her eyes, and cheeks so colourless that Tom exclaimed—

"What a washed-out rag you are! No wonder you were sent down here to
get a bit of colour!"

"I'm all right!"

"You aren't worrying your little self over my news, are you?"

Tomina forced a laugh.

"There seems nothing to worry about. What time do they hope to get
here?"

"Some time early in the afternoon. I heard from her this morning.
She—they want me to go on with them to Dilchester Burrow. They're going
to put up there for the night."

Tomina said nothing.

Tom looked sharply at her.

"I'll come back to you to-morrow if I do, Tommie. They're going back to
town. If you'll have me till the end of the week, I'll stay. And then
won't you come back to town with me?"

"In time for your wedding? Of course I will," and Tommie's tone was gay.

Tom turned rather earnestly to her whilst they were breakfasting.

"I'm building great things on the meeting to-day. I want you to have
Doreen to stay with you when I'm gone. Just at first—she hates town
so—later she'll pay visits to her own people, but if—if you like each
other, I'm wondering whether you wouldn't like to live with her. She
has had a small house left her. It wants a lot of doing up, but in a
few months' time will be fit for occupation. We're going to spend our
honeymoon at the old Hall near it. It's the dower house; and her old
aunt died and left it to her. Her cousin is a bachelor and is abroad,
so the Hall is virtually shut up. What do you say?"

Tomina gasped.

"Oh, Tom, don't go quite so fast! You're turning my quiet life
topsy-turvy. My dear boy, you can't shake two women together in a
moment of time, and make them into a couple of lovebirds in a cage!"

Tom laughed merrily.

"I'll be mum till you've met. Now, are you going to walk me round to a
few of your favourite haunts?"

"You must give me a little time for my housekeeping. I talk every
morning, and Susan acts. But she won't act unless I talk. I shan't keep
you waiting long."

They had a pleasant walk through the village a little later. Thor was
very suspicious of the new-comer at first. But after inspecting his
boots, and walking round him with a critical eye, he decided that he
must be one of the family, and followed close to heel behind him.

And as they walked along, Tomina began very quietly to tell her brother
of the change in her life. She finished by saying—

"It's all so new to me that I'm very, very full of it. And, of course,
you can't tell what a tremendous difference it has made in my outlook.
I hope it will affect my outside life; it ought to, but it has
revolutionised my inner life, I can tell you."

Tom did not scoff. His twin sister had always been very dear to him,
but he was puzzled.

"You're not the kind to be religious," he said, with a shake of the
head, "and I don't know where you get it from. There's no one else of
that breed in our family."

"But there will be soon," said Tomina eagerly. "Oh yes, I can assure
you that you will be the next. I know you will, for I'm praying hard
for you. And when you begin to feel uncomfortable, you'll know what it
is. I'm quite positive that I shall be answered, for the promise says
so. And the promise has been the means of my salvation!"

"I do hope you will leave poor me alone!" said Tom, with his merry
twinkle. "I don't want to be shaken out of one skin into another, as
you seem to have been."

"And then Doreen must be the next!" said Tomina, looking away with a
dreamy smile. "Perhaps she may be the first!"

Tom's eyebrows worked rather nervously.

"I say, Tommie, don't be—a—fanatic. I shall be hearing of you in a
Salvation Army garb next, beating a drum in a poke bonnet!"

"Don't be a foolish boy! There's nothing fanatical about the real
thing, Tom; and it really is so simple and so easy, and so awfully
restful and inspiring, that I'll never rest till you have it."

"Have what?" said Tom, desperately. "I do hate that 'it'! People who
talk religion always say so much about 'it'!"

"Then I won't mention it again," said Tomina. "There's really no 'it'
about it."

There was silence for a few minutes. Then Tomina burst forth.

"I'm like the pedlar, the one who first spoke to me, you know. I can't
conceive why it's the fashion for us all to scoff and despise the
One Who made us, and Who sacrificed Himself for us, and Who loves us
through thick and thin, and through all our scoffing and despising! I
was awfully afraid of good people. I hated them, and as for wanting to
be good myself—well, I was just like you, I thought it belonged to the
Salvation Army kind of person. It's a funny state of things. We're all
just alike!"

Tom was speechless.

And Tomina wisely said no more just then. She suddenly changed the
subject.

CHAPTER XV

A SAILOR'S BRIDE

THE visitors were late in arriving, and the car did not mount the steep
hill. Tom grew restless and went off down to meet them.

Tomina stayed where she was. Her heart was in a tumult. She dreaded,
yet longed, to see Tom's future wife. She heard voices coming up the
village street, but she sat still by her fire and waited. She was
looking very charming this afternoon. She wore a grey cloth skirt, with
rather an ethereal filmy lace and chiffon blouse to tone with it, and
she had tucked some violets into her belt. Her hair, as usual, curled
round her broad, white brow. If her face looked a trifle white, and her
eyes soft and grave, she had only to speak to bring out all the sparkle
and charm that was her chief characteristic.

In a very few minutes, the door opened and Tom ushered his visitors in.
Mrs. Letterby was first introduced, a smart, plump little woman with
plenty to say for herself, the type of woman that Tomina knew well in
town—good-natured, self-indulgent, and with very moderate brains. It
was the tall stately figure behind her that made Tomina instantly alert
and interested. When she threw up her veil, Tomina saw a very proud,
small head with a wealth of soft dusky hair, deep inscrutable unsmiling
eyes, a pale oval face, delicate nose, rather a prominent chin, and a
sensitively curved mouth, as grave and unsmiling as the eyes. Just a
little glimmer of a smile came to the lips when Tomina stretched out
both her hands, and raised her face to hers with her own fascinating
smile.

"For Tom's sake, you must let me love you!" she said impulsively.

"Or try to," said Doreen Forrester.

There was no doubt about it she was very beautiful, but there was
something strangely aloof and distant in her bearing and tone.

Tom did not seem to perceive it. Worshipful adoration was in his eyes.
As he stood by her, Tomina felt an unreasonable vexation rise within
her that Doreen should make Tom appear a short man. He in reality
topped her by a good inch, but he was broadly made, and she was thin
and willowy. She sat down and handed her long motor veil to Tom, then
she looked into his face and smiled, and Tom's honest face grew crimson.

Tomina watched them both as she talked to Mrs. Letterby. She was rather
relieved than otherwise when Tom took Doreen out into the garden. Then
she felt she could speak freely.

"This is all very sudden, isn't it? Why are they in such a hurry to get
married?"

"My dear Miss Gavin, if you ask me, I should say because your brother
has no patience, and Doreen is tired of being a single woman. She
wasn't very happy out in Queensland, got bored with the perpetual
entertaining. I hope your brother will understand her. I never could.
She is older than he, you know."

"Only a year," said Tomina, quickly.

"A year in age, but quite ten years in experience. She was always very
grave and intense even as a child. I was the pickle, always tumbling
into scrapes, and then out of them. You are not much like your brother.
He told us you were his twin."

"No, I am not much like him," said Tomina, "but tell me more about your
sister. She does not look happy."

"Don't you think so? You really mustn't get out of me all her love
affairs. She has had a good many. Men say she is elusive, and their
curiosity is roused. She is more a man's woman than a woman's. Your
brother is very favoured, for she means to stick to him this time."

"You are rather implying that she has been engaged before."

"I am not going to say. Doreen is old enough to know her own mind now;
and she is not a girl to take the advice of others. What a tiny cottage
you have here! Do you like it?"

"Very much."

"You don't fit it."

Tomina laughed.

"That is rather an unkind speech. I didn't fit it when I first came
down, but I flatter myself that I do now. Is—is Doreen living with you?"

"Till she marries. She doesn't want a crush. It will be quite a simple
affair. I am keeping a spare room for you."

"Oh, thank you, but if I am in town, I must go home. My stepmother
would feel hurt if I did not."

"I forgot you lived in town. I believe I have met Lady Gavin; we don't
stay much in London. We have a house there this winter, but we live in
Hampshire. My husband is a Member, and the House is sitting now, so of
course we must be in town."

Conversation rather languished between them. Mrs. Letterby would not
give Tomina much more information about her sister. She learnt that
their parents had died whilst they were still in the schoolroom, and
they had more or less looked after themselves till Mrs. Letterby
married. Then Doreen had made her home with her until her brother asked
her to go out and keep house for him in Australia.

"I want to see a little of Doreen," said Tomina, presently. "Do you
think they would be vexed if we joined them in the garden?"

Mrs. Letterby rose with alacrity. They found them pacing the little
path by the sweet-briar hedge, she still with that grave brooding
expression of countenance; and Tom with his face aglow, and eyes
sparkling with animation.

Tomina cleverly detached Doreen from him, and took her out of the side
gate under pretence of seeing the old church.

"I want to see something of you," she said. "I suppose we are the two
women that Tom loves most in the world. But it is always a trying
experience when a sister has to take the second place."

"Is it?" questioned Doreen, slowly. "I used to wish my brother married
when I lived with him. But he was a different stamp of man from yours."

"Tom is a dear," said Tomina, warmly. "His only fault is that he is too
unselfish as a man. He always was that. I used to be the selfish one as
children; he generally gave way to me. I do hope you and he will have a
happy time together. I wish he had not to leave you so soon."

"We shall have two weeks together," said Doreen, very quickly. "It is
the life that appeals to me. We shall not be likely to get tired of
each other, and the time he is away will soon pass. As one gets older,
time seems nothing. Everything passes, bad times as well as good."

Tomina looked at her a little inquiringly, but Doreen's face was so
cold and impassive that she dared say no more.

"Tell me about the little old house that has been left you. Tom
mentioned it to me. Do you like it?"

Tomina had kindled the spark at last.

Light and warmth leapt into Doreen's grey eyes.

"Oh, it is exquisite," she said. "It is a dream of a place. It came
so unexpectedly that even now I can hardly believe it is true. I am
longing to go down and take possession. It has a thatched roof with
many gables and irregular corners to it. The windows are genuine leaded
casements. All the rooms are odd and uneven. I mean that you step down
into one and up into another, and you never know where the doors are,
as they and the cupboard doors are often in the wall, and are papered
over so that you do not notice them. The drawing-room runs from east to
west and is upstairs with a balcony outside it, and you look out upon a
deer park with a lake in the distance, and hills beyond."

"Oh, go on!" cried Tomina. "I love the sound of it."

"The downstair hall ends in a conservatory and has a wide old-fashioned
hearth. The dining-room and morning room have quaint glass cupboards
in the wall, and deep window-seats. And then the garden, it's just a
garden out of a book—winding paths and old red brick walls covered with
fruit, and little lawns between lavender hedges and herbaceous borders
of old-fashioned flowers, and a sundial surrounded with roses, and a
terrace walk bordered with roses. My aunt spent all her time in her
garden, and all her money. I never dreamt it would come to me. It is
enough to content a woman without any husband at all!"

"I am glad you say that," said Tomina, inwardly wondering at the
transformation in her, "because it shows that you have no other reason
for becoming engaged to Tom, but because you really love him!"

Doreen's long lashes drooped over her eyes, and the colour faded again
from her cheeks.

"You are quick at deductions," she said.

"Am I? I only see that you could quite well have gone off to this house
and taken possession without burdening yourself with a husband!"

"I don't think I could."

The words left her lips very slowly. Her animation had gone, she was
again the quiet inscrutable girl, and Tomina could not win another
smile from her. Presently Doreen said—

"Why do you live in such a very out-of-the-way place? You do not look
like a country person."

"Ah! You wait till you see me in my oilskins on a wet day driving my
van! You like the country, don't you? Will you come and stay with me
for a little when Tom leaves you?"

"Would you like to have me?"

"Very much. I want to know you. Will it take me a long time?"

Doreen's eyes met Tomina's mischievous ones, but she made no reply.
Then she suddenly laid her hand on her arm.

"Are you sweet all through?" she asked. "If so, I would like to be with
you. But if you question in your heart continually whether I am good
enough for your brother, I would rather have the ocean between us."

It was Tomina's turn to look serious now.

"I shan't be critical, Doreen. I honestly would like you to come.
Somehow or other I feel that we shall be friends."

"Then I will. I do love the country. I am tired of people, I am tired
of talk. When I take possession of my house, the sense of silence and
peace there will be the greatest attraction to me."

Their little talk was over, for the others now overtook them, and they
returned to the cottage for tea. Tomina had no chance for a talk with
her brother. Only as she insisted upon helping him into his overcoat in
her little hall, did he turn to her and say—

"Isn't she adorable?"

And Tomina answered promptly—

"She is charming."

Then he went off with them, and Tomina had to possess her soul in
patience till he returned to her the next day. She felt very mystified
and uneasy over Doreen, and longed to talk to Tom about her.


He came back in the best of spirits and quite ready to talk only of the
one who was at present absorbing his life.

Then Tomina asked him this question—"Is she happy, Tom?"

"Happy! Well, I should think so! Why should you doubt it?"

"She has such a sad face."

"Until she smiles. And I keep her smiling when I am with her," said
Tom, stoutly.

"She must be fond of you," said Tomina, slowly and thoughtfully, more
to convince herself than him of that fact, "for she is not obliged to
marry. A girl who is a possessor of a beautiful old home could have a
very nice life in it without a husband. She is quite independent, isn't
she?"

"Trust a sister to take the shine out of a fellow's self-esteem!"
laughed Tom. "Why should you doubt her affection for me? And as to her
house, in honesty, I must say that apart from me, it would be a white
elephant, for her aunt never left her a penny of money."

"How dreadful for her! Has she no money of her own?"

"Nothing to speak of. A paltry twenty-five pounds a year, or something
of that sort. But I've always said that a man is in a queer corner when
he lives on his wife's money! I wouldn't do it!"

Tomina felt a sudden sinking of heart. Was this possibly a reason for
her accepting Tom? She had a strange feeling that Doreen did not care
for him as he cared for her.

"You got on all right with her, didn't you?" pursued Tom. "I knew you
would—once you saw each other. She isn't very keen after people as a
rule, not after her own sex. I think she likes men better. She's so
different from the ordinary run of girls, not all that chitter-chatter
and rot. She likes sensible talk, she's awfully clever, you know, reads
no end. Says she's never lonely if she has books. But she told me she
was coming to stay with you, and then later on you'll fix it up with
her, and when I come home, I shall find the two of you waiting for me!"

"Oh, Tom, not so fast!"

Tomina laughed as she spoke.

"I really cannot promise to live with Doreen, I don't suppose that she
would care about it, neither should I. But I mean to be her friend if
she will let me. You mustn't ask more than that."

"Doreen will like what I like," said Tom, cheerfully. "If she knows
that I want you to live with her while I am away, she'll have you."

"But please leave us free, dear Tom, to settle that between ourselves."

Then Tom burst into a rhapsody of all Doreen's perfections.

And Tomina listened, and wondered if all men were so simple in their
trust and adoration as this sailor brother of hers. The time flew
whilst he was with her, and then rather reluctantly, she accompanied
him to town.


She was received by her stepmother and friends as if she had been away
in a foreign country. They all congratulated her on her improved looks,
and took it for granted that her rest cure was complete, and that she
had left the country for good. Tomina did not at first undeceive them.
Indeed, she hardly had time, for Tom's wedding was upon them, and
Tomina passed through it as if in a dream. There was a great rush, in
spite of Mrs. Letterby's announcement that it would be very quiet, and
Doreen seemed absorbed and overwhelmed by her own friends and relatives.

But Tomina got one moment with her when she was dressed in her bridal
garments. She had shut every one out of her room saying she wanted to
be alone, and then she had drawn Tomina inside for a second.

"I have hardly seen you; tell me—you know your brother as no one else
does—will he be satisfied—is he satisfied with me?"

It was such a strange question for her to ask that for one moment
Tomina was dumb. Then she took both her hands in hers, and she noted
how cold and trembling they were.

"My dear Doreen, Tom's one idea is that you are perfection. You have
got the whole of his affectionate, honest heart. I pray you may never
fail or disappoint him."

Other words were trembling on her tongue, but she did not say them.

She longed to cry.

"Oh, don't marry him even now if you do not love him!" But she dare not
be the cause of agitating her at this last moment.

Doreen leant forward and kissed her.

"I am glad to have you as a sister," she said.

And then Tomina was obliged to leave her.


She was rather a sad spectator in the fashionable church. A weight
seemed to fall upon her spirits. As bride and bridegroom came down
the aisle, she brightened up. Tom, in his uniform, was handsome and
radiant; and Doreen, though very pale, was smiling and looking her
happiest.

Their eyes met Tomina's and both nodded to her in such a bright,
reassuring way that she took herself to task for her silly fears and
fancies.

And the rest of the ceremony passed off without a hitch.

When Doreen wished Tomina good-bye, she said—

"May I come down to you in a fortnight's time?"

"Oh yes—yes! I shall be waiting for you!"

But Tomina's eyes were misty as she spoke.

Doreen was looking very beautiful. She held herself proudly and her
eyes were bright, and there was a pink colour in her lips. She and
her bridegroom were whirled away in their motor amidst a shower of
confetti, and a little of the old-fashioned rice. And though many of
their friends were saying what a successful wedding it had been, Tomina
knew in her heart that somewhere there was a rift within the lute.

She did not stay in town very long, though pressed to do so.

Lady Gavin was a little uneasy, and had a serious talk with her the day
before she left her.

"You know, my dear girl, that I have never interfered with your
pleasures or plans, but I hardly think you can continue to live alone
in this country cottage of yours. It is so—so peculiar. People will
talk. It was very well as long as you were in delicate health, but you
are stronger now, are you not? You musn't slip out of everything and
get unsociable and cranky. You are too young for that kind of thing. I
hope to see you happily married. Then I shall consider I have done my
duty toward you, and begin to think of settling down to a peaceful old
age."

Tomina's gay laughter rang out. Lady Gavin was the last person on earth
to own to increasing years, nor would she ever be likely to settle down
to a quiet old age.

"You must give me my year. I haven't had it yet. And town life does not
agree with me. You know it never did."

"You look very well. I found out quite by accident that you are near
Colonel Norman's place. Did you see anything of him?"

"A little," said Tomina, carelessly.

"It is dreadfully sad about his mother. I suppose you know they are at
Cannes now. A friend of mine is in the same hotel with them. She says
it is pitiful to see the son tied to his mother's couch; she won't let
him out of her sight. He is just like a nurse attendant. I can't fancy
him in that 'rôle.' He used to be such a smart, keen soldier, and was
always so autocratic and masterful. That was the cause of your quarrel
with him, was it not?"

Tomina winced as if a nerve had been touched.

"You musn't mind my going back," she said. "I am developing a new part
of me in the country—the part that has been lying dormant. And I am
not sufficiently strong in that part to come back to the rush of town
gaiety yet."

Lady Gavin looked at her with a perplexed face, but after a little
further effort to dissuade Tomina from returning, she gave it up,
and they parted the best of friends. She added to her stepdaughter's
uneasiness by saying to her in parting—

"I hope this marriage of Tom's will turn out a success. If you are
going to have his wife to stay with you, you may make something of her.
But she's marrying him for some convenience, and her heart isn't in it.
I dare say it will turn out all right, as Tom will be away so much.
Unless another man appears, and then I wouldn't trust her."

Tomina looked horrified, but her stepmother only laughed.

"I don't want to shock you, but you're old enough to be able to read a
woman's face. Something in her past has spoiled her life; that is very
plain if you watch her."

And as Tomina was in the train whirling through the town into the great
country beyond, her thoughts were centred on Doreen.

CHAPTER XVI

A QUIET CHRISTMAS

THE weather turned very cold when Tomina arrived home. The first day
that she drove out in her van again, she had a surprise for her women
passengers. Susan had worked hard with her kettles, and in the van were
some hot water foot-warmers.

"No cold feet in my van," said Tomina, gaily, as she got upon her seat.
"If our feet are warm, the rest of us will be, our hearts included, in
spite of the old saw."

"Eh, dear soul, 'tis comfort sure enough," said an old woman with a
happy sigh. "It do warm the cockles of yer 'art, as you say, mem."

They were a very happy jovial party to-day. They loved to have Tomina
back, and said one to her—

"Me dear, the van wi'out our carrier is a dumpsey place. You mustn't
never leave off a-drivin' us."

"She'll be carried off herself, I reckon, one day," said another, "but
we'll hope your man will live in these parts, miss, and then you shall
still be our carrier."

"Oh, don't wish me such a fate as yours!" said Tomina. "To be tied up
to a man is such bondage—now, isn't it?"

There was a cackle of laughter at this.

"'Tis Bible orders," said one old woman, solemnly—"that the woman serve
the man; an' as I tell the young folks, if the maids want to turn that
topsy-turvey, the Lord will have it agenst 'em in the judgment."

Tomina got sober at once.

"I wish we all lived our lives according to the Bible, Mrs. Sapper.
What a happy world it would be, wouldn't it?"

"It would be a world of Sundays," said another.

"Oh no—I don't believe that the Bible and religion only belong to
Sunday. We are meant to have our busy weekdays, but every bit of work
is gilded with sunshine if we feel we belong to God."

There was sudden silence in the van. Tomina surprised her audience.
They thought her very good company, but rather a careless, giddy sort
of individual.

"Be you proper religious, miss?" asked a chapel-goer, gravely. "Have
you got your soul saved?"

There was some indignation at this, and the speaker was hushed.

"'Tisn't preachin' and such like we has in this van," said a Mrs.
Hutton. "Look at 'ome, Mrs. Coke; we heard a proper wrangling night
afore last, when your Bill came home a half-hour behind his time!"

"Oh, hush!" said Tomina, looking back over her shoulder at the woman.
"We won't say any more now. But Mrs. Coke is quite right—our religion
is not right unless we can say our souls are safe in God's keeping,
and—and I dare to hope that mine is."

The talkers were silenced.

Their carrier was continually surprising them, and for her to "talk
religion" was the most surprising event of all.

It was a busy day in Damerton, for it was the Christmas market. The
shops were gay with bright evergreens and all kinds of Christmas
novelties. Tomina had a good deal of shopping to do for herself to-day.
She was looking into a toy-shop window wondering what she could buy for
Harry, when he himself rushed into her arms.

"Oh, Tommie darling, is it you? I thought I was never going to see you
again. And you are going to have me for Christmas, aren't you? We break
up the day after to-morrow, and Miss Dale has brought us out to see the
Christmas market."

"Of course, I'm expecting you," said Tomina, putting her arm round the
boy. "I'm getting a dull old woman, now I've nobody to play with."

"Oh, you should play with Thor, he's ripping! Tommie, you aren't going
away to London and leave us altogether? Miss Dale heard you were. I
think if you did, I should turn into a little old man with wrinkles!"

He was dancing round her as he spoke, and then Miss Dale came up with
two other small boys. Instantly Harry's manner altered, and Tomina
laughed at his consequential gravity.

"It's a friend of mine," he said, in a lofty aside to his two
schoolfellows. "I'm going to spend Christmas with her."

Miss Dale and Tomina exchanged a few remarks, and then the former
called Harry to follow her.

For a moment he lingered, and put his mouth up close to Tomina's ear.

"Tommie, if you had to give a present to a—a kind of lady—not a very
old one, what would you get for her?"

"I'd go to the penny stall in the market," said Tomina, promptly, "and
buy her a bundle of tape—a nice, useful thing to have—or a pen, Harry.
They have lovely pens there for a penny."

"But she mustn't know how much it costs," said Harry, with a frown.

"You needn't tell her."

Harry was called again; he was obliged to go.

"If I don't get it at the penny stall, she won't know," he said.
"Good-bye, Tommie!"

He dashed away.

And Tomina went on shopping with a catch in her throat.

"Oh, I wish I was a mother of a family!" she said. "One wants to have
children belonging to one at Christmas-time."

She came home that night very tired. And Susan was aghast at all the
parcels brought into the house.

"Something for each of my old women," said Tomina, laughing, "and for
the young ones too, of course. And I saw Harry, Susan. We shall have
him here in two days' time."

"Mrs. Francis called, ma'am, and seemed to want to see you badly. She
said she'd come again to-morrow morning."

"It's about Harry, I suppose."

Tomina relapsed into silence. Mrs. Francis brought thoughts of Colonel
Norman and his mother. She remembered that she had promised to write to
him at Christmas-time. She had only heard from him once, and that was
a terse few lines telling her of their safe arrival abroad. Tired as
she was, after her evening meal was over, she got out her blotter, and
sitting in her low chair by the fire, sat gazing into the hot coals,
pen in hand.

"I must get it over," she said at last, rousing herself from a reverie
into which she had sunk. And then she wrote as follows:—

 "MY DEAR COLONEL NORMAN,

 "My best wishes for you and your mother at this time! You see I have
remembered my promise to write, but letter-writing is always an effort,
and my pen seems to paralyse my brain. We are having hard frost now,
and I spend most of my time dreaming over the fire. Do you remember my
brother Tom? He has just married. I am selfishly sorry, for the bits
of time he spent with me on leave were always very precious to me, and
they are gone for ever now. I went up to town, but I like it less than
ever, so I am back here jogging away on market days with my van, and
learning a good deal in my studies of nature and humanity.

 "How is your mother? My heart sinks down as I think of the enormous
amount of resignation and patience it must take to bear such a lot as
hers, and yours, cheerfully. Do you ever smile, I wonder? Does she?
Does the Riviera sunshine reach your heart?

 "Well. I must close. We do Christmas in the old style in this village,
and if you think of me at all, you must imagine me wreathing church
pillars with ivy and holly berries and moss, or going round the village
with a big basket of goodies under my arm, and pulling aside my
curtains to listen to the waits and carol singers outside.

 "As a matter of fact, I expect I shall do none of these things because
of my unregulated mind, but I mean to enjoy everything if I can.

 "Your old friend,

 "TOMINA."

 "P.S.—Do you remember I told you I was in a metamorphosis stage? I have
got through into a new life altogether—at least, my inside man has—and
my outlook upon the world is quite, quite different.

 "For the better I hope, but I cannot write about it.

 "I suppose some need no change. Yet one of my old dears said in the van
last market day—

 "'If I think as I thoughted when I were a maid, why, life has taughted
me nothing!'

 "Can you divide the human race into these two classes?—'What can I
give?' and 'What can I get?'

 "Isn't this all very egotistical? Adieu."

This letter went its way, and brought a prompt reply—

 "MY DEAR TOMINA,

 "You 'Colonel' me, but I will not 'Miss' you. We are too old friends
for that. Your letter arrived on Christmas morning, and if the sunshine
had not reached my heart before, it has done so since. My only fault
is that you might have been more egotistical and given me some further
and clearer account of your dear little self and surroundings. You may
be quite sure that I shall imagine you in many Christmas pictures of my
own composition. If you sit dreaming over your fire, I sit on the sunny
verandah outside my mother's room and dream my dreams as I look away
at the distant mountain with peeps, between mimosa bushes, of the blue
Mediterranean.

 "My mother is better in many ways. She can talk, but she will always
be a helpless invalid. She now has a longing for home, and when the
warmer weather comes, I think we shall return to England. But the Court
is going to be sold, so I do not know where we shall settle. We may
go down to Devon. Will you write again and cheer our spirits? Even a
letter is an event to my mother now. She loves all news in any shape or
form. It is her only entertainment. Will you give us this pleasure from
time to time? I do not understand your metamorphosis. You did not need
any change in my eyes.

 "But then I am only an old fogey, and a very dull one now at the best
of times. I saw your brother's marriage in the 'Times.' It surprised
me. I knew her years ago. She was a brilliant beauty, and I quite
thought she had married an old chum of mine, but I suppose they altered
their minds. Adieu, Tomina! May Christmas blessing be yours!

 "Always your devoted

 "GERALD."

Tomina heard about Yockley Court being for sale before she received
this letter. Mrs. Francis told her when she came to see her. She wanted
to know if she should have Harry at the Hall, or whether Tomina meant
to keep him with her for the whole of his holidays. When this matter
was settled, she began to talk about the Normans.

"It is so sad that they should have to give up the Court, but I think
Colonel Norman has known for some time that it must go. He could not
pull things together, though he tried hard. And, of course, this stay
in the Riviera is a great expense. I think it just wonderful, don't
you, that a man of Gerald Norman's sort should be such a patient,
devoted son? I have known him many years, and some of his friends used
to call him 'the Despot.' But we are given many lessons to learn, are
we not? And I am sure his character will be mellowed and softened under
this trial."

Tomina would never discuss Colonel Norman with any one, least of all
with Mrs. Francis.

But she no longer disliked the young widow. She felt that now she had a
great bond in common with her.

"You think that we all have to go through discipline?"

"We are told so, are we not?"

And Mrs. Francis looked at Tomina very sympathetically.

"I know some people who have never had a trouble in their life," said
Tomina. "And I used to think that if you were good, you courted trouble
at once."

"Not necessarily. But we are reminded that a father chastens his
sons—not strangers."

Tomina's face grew very grave and sweet.

"I hope I am not going to be chastened yet. I am only two months old—a
mere baby."

Then Mrs. Francis's face lighted up, and her eyes glowed. Putting her
hand on Tomina's arm, she said caressingly—

"How very sweet of you to tell me what I have been longing to know!"

"Did you guess, then? Why haven't you spoken to me?"

"I have learned to be patient. I have prayed for you every day since we
first met."

"But why?" asked Tomina, touched and a little mystified. "What made you
think of me at all?"

"I fancy most people who have met you think about you. You came down
here a perfect stranger to us all, and now I think I may say that you
live in every one's heart."

"Thank you," said Tomina; "I don't believe you pay empty compliments. I
suppose you have thought me very scatter-brained and frivolous?"

"It seemed to me that you were in a transition stage, like an empty
house waiting for an owner."

"Then why didn't you tell me so?"

"Would you have liked it?"

"No," and Tomina laughed as she spoke; "I don't think I should have
ever spoken to you again."

"That was what I felt. I was ready if any opportunity had come, but I
prayed much."

"Well," said Tomina, softly, "I hope the empty house is tenanted. But
I still require a good deal of sweeping and garnishing. Harry was a
little angel in disguise. You taught Harry, and Harry taught me, so I
do owe you something after all."

Quite simply she told Mrs. Francis of how one thing had led on to
another, until the centre of her life was disturbed and changed.

"It was reading the Bible so much to Harry that showed me my need. And
then, when I was in despair about myself and didn't know what to do,
Mrs. Cayley solved my difficulties with the simple words, 'Ask, and ye
shall receive.' It just flashed through me what a fool I was not to ask
for everything at once. So I did it."

They sat on talking like close friends, and when Mrs. Francis at last
rose to go, she kissed Tomina.

"I do hope that you will break through your unsociable rule, and come
and see me some time."

"I will. I should like to do it."

So they parted, and Susan could not keep the glint of satisfaction out
of her eyes when Tomina said to her—

"I like Mrs. Francis at last, Susan, you will be glad to hear."

"'Tis time indeed, ma'am! I do think for two of our nicest ladies not
to be on close visitin' terms is very strange! And 'tis only for want
of knowin', as I have told you often."


When Harry came home, Tomina found she had her hands full. They had
a very happy Christmas. On Christmas Day they dined at the Hall. On
Boxing Day, the Rector gave a village tea and Christmas tree. And that
same week, Tomina gave a tea to all of her regular passengers in her
van. She heard from her brother and his wife on Christmas Day. They
both seemed happy.

Doreen wrote as follows:—

 "MY DEAR TOMINA,

 "Isn't it strange that we have come into each other's lives in such
a short time? I hope you're having a good Christmas. We are. Tom and
I spend most of our days in choosing papers and chintzes and bits of
antique furniture for my house. We do it from catalogues, and it is
great fun. Our present quarters are very luxurious, but hardly snug.
And I am so glad that my home is not a great rambling place like this.
Tom goes next Friday. Will you have me on Saturday? I am quite looking
forward to it. And then we shall renew and cement our friendship, I
hope. May I send you my love?

 "Your affectionate sister-in-law,

 "DOREEN GAVIN."

Tomina gave a little sigh.

"I am glad she likes me, but she has taken Tom too quietly. Her letter
is more full of that house of hers than of him."

It was arranged that Harry should go to the Hall the day before Doreen
arrived.

"You are sure, Tommie," he said, "that you don't send me there because
you're tired of me?"

"Quite sure, sweetheart! But we haven't much room, you know, and Susan
couldn't wait upon all of us."

"Susan says," announced Harry, solemnly, "that the way you wait on
yourself now is a marvel! She says those that ain't born to it rarely
take to it late in life."

"Oh, Harry, you and Susan will be the death of me!"

And Tomina laughed so infectiously that Harry joined her, though he did
not know at what she was laughing.


When Saturday came, Tomina awaited Doreen's arrival with a strange
mixture of feelings. She was very low at Tom's departure, and felt
that she had been defrauded of his society. Doreen would always in the
future stand between her and her twin brother. She had had a great
struggle in her heart to think kindly of Doreen, and yet she had a
curious and intense interest in her, and was glad that she would have
the opportunity of getting to know her.

Doreen arrived late on Saturday afternoon after dark. Tomina sent
a close fly for her. And when she came into her little room, her
shoulders were powdered with snow.

They kissed each other warmly. Doreen's cheeks were pink, and she
declared she was warm with the exertion of walking up the steep hill.

"It's an awful place to get to in winter!" she said. "You could never
ask an elderly person here or a delicate one, for the cabman insists
upon turning you out at the bottom of the hill. He said that it would
do me good to walk. And he was right. I was freezing slowly inside his
carriage."

"I sent a foot-warmer and a rug."

"Yes," and Doreen smiled as she spoke. "He said to me, 'Our young
lady be wonderful thoughtful for the feet. Her van be fitted up with
these here hot tins.' By which he meant that you gave your passengers
foot-warmers, I suppose?"

"Of course," said Tomina; "it's the least I can do for them."

"How snug and cosy you are here! It is like a bird's nest."

"I have never suffered from the cold in my life," said Tomina, "and as
this cottage is not so warm as the London houses, I have piled comforts
in every room, and keep fires going in them all. Susan was quite aghast
at first, but I found a small girl in the village who comes in every
morning to help her, so we're all right. Now tell me how Tom is."

"Quite well. He went off at six this morning, before I was up."

"Didn't you go to see him off?"

"To Southampton? Dear no! Why should I? How could I have come down to
you to-day if I had?"

"I pictured you both going down to Southampton yesterday, and dining
and sleeping there," said Tomina.

There was a little reproach in her tone.

"Men don't like to be fussed over by their belongings," said Doreen,
indifferently.

Tomina made no reply. There was a little restraint in their
conversation after that.

Later on in the evening, after they had had a dainty little dinner, and
were sitting in two big easy-chairs before a blazing fire, Doreen began
to talk about her house, and then she grew enthusiastic at once.

Tomina listened and was interested. But at last she said—

"I believe you're one of those women whose home is the dearest thing in
the world—dearer than a husband."

"Perhaps you are right."

Doreen's face grew grave at once, and a sad look came into her eyes.

"I suppose I may be romantic," went on Tomina, "but I feel that I
shouldn't care a bit about my circumstances as long as I were with the
one I liked."

"You are younger than I. As you get on in life, you learn to be content
with the second best."

Tomina's bright brown eyes dwelt reflectively upon Doreen's proud,
clear-cut face.

"I feel as if it is an insult to Tom to call him second best."

Tomina's tone was fiery.

"I did not necessarily allude to your brother. A home has its
attractions to me, and from what I hear of you, I should have thought
you sympathised with me. You became tired of your stepmother's gay
house in town; I became tired of the same thing out in Queensland.
When I went out there, I thought I should get away from society, but I
discovered my mistake."

"Why did you want to get away from society?" Tomina could not refrain
from asking.

"Why did you?" Doreen asked, looking at her steadily.

Tomina laughed in an embarrassed fashion.

"I was sent away by doctor's orders."

"Yes, but why did you wilfully let your health go? Why did you rack
your body and nerves to pieces? There is no need to do it. We both know
that."

Then Tomina smiled her sweet smile.

"My dear Doreen, our lives are our own; we need not pry inquisitively
into each other's motives and way of spending our existence."

Doreen looked sombrely into the fire in front of her. They fought shy
of personal topics after that. Doreen described the place in which she
was going to live, and talk flowed smoothly till bed-time.

When they said good night, Tomina looked up into Doreen's face with a
little of her old gay audacity.

"Sisters-in-law rarely like each other. Do you think we shall be
exceptions to the rule?"

"I don't see why we should not be," answered Doreen, promptly, "if you
will be reasonable, and not expect me to be for ever extolling your
brother's virtues and bemoaning his absence."

"My dear Doreen, I would rather you were sincere than anything else in
the world."

"And," pursued Doreen, gravely, "just as you can be happy when your
brother is at sea in spite of your great love for him, so can I, and
I mean to be. It takes a good deal to make me content and happy, but
when I have once reached that stage, it will take a good deal more to
disenchant me. So do not try to do it."

"I wouldn't dream of doing such a thing. You ought to be a blissfully
happy woman."

They did not kiss each other as they separated for the night.

"We shall go slowly," said Tomina to herself. "I wonder if she will be
dull company?"

But she was not. She was full of information and interest. Gardening
was her hobby, but she was also a great reader, and could talk well.
She had had many strange experiences in Australia, and Tomina listened
to her stories with a fascination which surprised herself. Doreen was
quite content to saunter about the village and take an occasional walk
with Tomina; the rest of the time she spent over the fire with a book.


On one of the carrier's days, she accompanied Tomina in the van, but
she was not a success in it. The passengers were afraid of her, and the
usual jokes and stories were not forthcoming.

Tomina taxed her with stiffness.

"You don't like my dear country people. You are too sensible; we are
all silly together when we go to market. Everything amuses us, unless a
tragedy has happened, and then you should see our round eyes and open
mouths. Sometimes the tragedies are more amusing than painful. I wish I
could tell you some of them. They are inimitable in their slow country
dialect."

"I don't care for the lower classes," said Doreen, a little
indifferently.

"But if you're going to live in the country, you must. You will be
surrounded by them."

"I shall live my life to myself. I am looking forward to it. I have
been entertaining people that have never appealed to me for years. Now
I shall have a rest."

"That was how I first felt when I came down here. And then I had a
surfeit of myself; and the villagers don't want entertaining—they
entertain me. I wonder—I wonder if you will be led along the same road
as I was?"

Doreen looked questioningly at her and Tomina's eyes glowed as she said—

"When we know each other a little better, I'll tell you what has
happened to me down here."

She said no more, and Doreen asked no questions.

CHAPTER XVII

AN ENGAGEMENT

TOMINA and Doreen lunched at the Hall and dined at the Rectory. But
neither of them cared for visiting. One afternoon, young Tracy St.
Vincent came over to call. In the course of conversation, he asked
Tomina if she could ride.

"I have a spare mount. I wondered if you would like to exercise her.
She is a black mare and a very easy goer."

"I do ride occasionally, but I really have no time at present to
indulge in it."

"Would Mrs. Gavin like to try her? Oh, I say, you might be friendly
with me! I'm awfully down in my luck! The country isn't my style at
all. I haven't a single friend."

"You are a very foolish young man," said Tomina, sitting up very
straight and speaking in a grandmotherly fashion. "Take the people as
they are round here, and don't keep abusing the country. That is what
they dislike in you."

"Do they now? Who told you that? That stiff old Johnny at the Hall
here? Norman is still away, isn't he? Why is he selling his place? I
met the fellow who has bought it yesterday. He came over to my place to
ask me about one of my keepers who is leaving me."

"Who is buying it?" asked Tomina, with interest. She was always glad
when Tracy got off himself.

"He's an old chum of Norman's—Maurice Holme, he calls himself. A major
in the same regiment as Norman used to be in, he told me. He and his
wife are staying at the 'Red Deer' in Damerton. He's rather a decent
chap—not so stiff and starchy as most of our neighbours. He took me for
an old inhabitant; it was quite a change from the way most treat me."

Just by chance, Tomina happened to glance at Doreen. She was struck by
the ashen pallor of her face.

"Aren't you well?" she asked.

"I think your room is very warm," Doreen said, rising as she spoke and
walking towards the door. "I'm all right; don't trouble to follow me."

She closed the door after her. Tracy St. Vincent went on talking, but
Tomina only half heard him.

At last, she asked—

"How long is this Major Holme staying in Damerton? Is he going to take
possession of Yockley Court at once?"

"I believe so. The arrangement is that he should walk straight in and
have the use of the contents of it till Norman can come back to see to
his personal effects."

"Oh, I am sorry—I can't bear old places to lose their owners! Has he a
family?"

"Didn't ask him. His wife is with him."

Tomina was very distrait for the rest of Tracy's visit. She was
relieved when he went. He did not stay to tea, as he was going on to
the Hall.

Doreen sent word by Susan that she had such a bad headache that she was
going to bed, and would rather not be disturbed.

Tomina sent her up a cup of tea, but did not attempt to go near her.
She sat over her fire thinking deeply. Before she went to bed, she
tapped at Doreen's door. It was locked. So she called out good night
and went to bed.

The next morning, Doreen appeared at breakfast with much her usual
manner, and apologised for her abrupt departure the previous afternoon.

"I do get very sudden headaches, and it's no good inflicting other
people with one's misery. Silence and bed generally cure me."

"That is fortunate," said Tomina, quietly.

Doreen looked quickly at her, opened her lips as if about to say
something, and then closed them.

They walked out together after breakfast, and Doreen said suddenly—

"I think I shall have to leave you at the end of this week, Tomina. I
must be up in town to see the decorators about my house."

"But you have only been with me a week."

"Ten days, have I not?"

"And yesterday morning you said you need not be back in town for
another fortnight."

Tomina's tone was quiet dignity itself.

Doreen was dumb.

Then Tomina turned to her, and she linked her arm in hers as she spoke
as if to take away the sting of her words.

"Yockley Court is a long way from here, Doreen, and you need not go
near Damerton."

Not a tremor nor an extra pulse beat from Doreen, but she looked
steadfastly in front of her as she said—

"I did not intend to conceal it from you. Of course, it was palpable
from my want of self-control. I do not wish to run the risk of meeting
Major Holme. It is extraordinary that in a place like this I should
hear of him."

"Not so extraordinary as my meeting Colonel Norman down here, for they
are evidently friends, and it is quite natural that one should take the
property from the other. How strangely we are connected, Doreen, you
and I! Can't we talk freely with each other?"

Doreen drew a deep breath.

"You never told me that you knew Colonel Norman."

"You never asked me."

"I don't know why you should expect me to confide in you when you are
so reticent about yourself. You reminded me the first night that I came
here that our lives were our own, that we need not be inquisitive about
each other's way of living."

"Perhaps I was wrong," said Tomina, in a low voice. "I haven't much to
tell, Doreen. I had a bitter quarrel with Colonel Norman years ago; we
parted, and the savour of my life seemed gone. That made me reckless.
And then I met him down here, and now we're friends—nothing more, and
he is away with his invalid mother, nursing her and keeping her happy."

"But he is not married?"

"No," said Tomina, a startled look coming into her eyes. "Did you ever
meet him?"

"Yes, more than once."

There was a little silence between them.

Then Tomina spoke, and there was almost a touch of tenderness in her
tone.

"You need not tell me anything, Doreen. I guess. Major Holme has been
in your past life, and though you are married to another man who holds
your heart, you don't feel ready yet to meet your old admirer."

"Ah," said Doreen, with a quick-caught breath, "you have only
yourself to thank for your separation, but I did nothing but love too
wholeheartedly. We were within a day of our marriage when—I can't tell
you the sordid story—another woman turned up and threatened him, and I
happily heard of it just in time. He married her two months afterwards,
and to use his own words to a friend of his, he has been in 'hell ever
since.'"

"Do you write to him?"

"Don't insult me."

"Forgive me, and thank you for your confidence. How crooked life is
sometimes!"

"It's not very exhilarating as one lives on."

"I don't know. It depends upon the part of ourselves which we
cultivate. That is what I have been taught since I have been here. And
I have got a new lease of life altogether. I feel as if I could be
ready for all the blows that fate could deal me. I dare say I should
crumple up when they came, but I don't know. All I do know is that I
have a breadth and height of vision that I was in absolute ignorance of
before."

"How do you mean?"

"I have got into touch with the Unseen."

Doreen looked at her with interest.

"Are you a spiritualist?"

"Not as you mean it. But we all belong to the Unseen, you know, and
numbers of us are going into it every day."

"How strangely you talk!"

"Perhaps I'm beating round the bush," said Tomina; "it is a way we
English have when we don't want to open our hearts to each other.
But in simple language I have been brought into touch with God, and
am beginning life again with my hand in His. You've no idea of the
difference it makes in everything. I walked alone for twenty-six years
of my life, and it wasn't a success, though I tried my utmost to make
it so."

Doreen gazed at her gravely and thoughtfully.

"I have walked alone all my life," she said, "and shall continue to do
so till the end."

"That will be a pity," said Tomina, "because I see now what a lot of
happiness and sunshine we miss if we do so. And we do make a mess of
things, don't we?"

"Or rather other people do it for us," said Doreen, bitterly.

They said no more. Doreen abruptly changed the conversation, and Tomina
felt a great pity for her, as she saw that poor Tom had not yet taken
the place of her former lover in her heart, and that the past was
casting a blight over her life.


In two days' time, she left Little Honeycombe, and Tomina did not seek
to detain her.

They drove together to the station, and fate did indeed seem against
them when, pacing up and down the quiet little country platform, they
saw Major Holme with his wife. When he saw Doreen, he lifted his hat
apparently with the greatest composure. But Tomina's quick eyes noted
that his hand trembled like a woman's as he held it out.

"How are you?" he said. "Who would have thought of seeing you in this
part? May I introduce my wife to you?"

Doreen held herself proudly; she shook hands with him, and bowed to
his wife, who was a very smart woman of fashion, but who did not bear
the stamp of a gentlewoman. Then Tomina was introduced, and a few
commonplace remarks were made till the train came in.

Mrs. Holme had evidently no idea who Doreen was; she suggested that
they should travel together. But Doreen promptly said she was going
third class, and Major Holme did not endorse his wife's request.

As he wished Doreen good-bye, a miserable, pleading look shot into his
eyes. Her face was as cold and hard as steel. And when Tomina kissed
her in parting, it did not relax or soften.

Tomina came home in low spirits. Doreen had told her she must come
to her for a visit after she had settled in her new house, and she
had promised to do so. But she still felt vexed with her for not
appreciating her husband more, and she did not look forward to the
visit.


A week or two later, she met Major Holme at the Hall. His wife was
not with him, and he seemed a different man without her. He turned to
Tomina at once.

"You and I must be friends," he said in his cheerful, hearty voice;
"for I believe you know Norman, and he and I have always been pals!"

"You are coming to live at Yockley Court," said Tomina.

"Yes, we are—at least, I'm going to settle my wife in. But I am still
in the Service, and have just been ordered up to Edinburgh."

"Won't your wife be lonely?"

"She says she won't. She has always been wild to possess a country
house—the only thing Norman can't give us with his is a ghost. And
that's a sad lack."

Tomina laughed.

"You can soon conjure up one. Have you heard from Colonel Norman
lately? Do you know how his mother is?"

"About the same, he says. I heard yesterday. Funny chap, isn't he? He
can alter his mode of life so extraordinarily quickly! He was just the
man I should have said who would always stay in the Service and never
be happy anywhere else. But he chucked it, and then came off down here
and settled into a country gentleman. I came down for his shooting
about a year ago, and you'd never have guessed he'd been a soldier.
Never mentioned it. Now he's had to chuck the gentleman 'rôle,' and
he's a sick-nurse and attendant to his mother, and I hear can't be
dragged into anything out there—plays a game of golf occasionally and
drops in at the club, but otherwise leads a woman's life. He and I
write to each other on and off."

"I think he has been having some buffets of fate," said Tomina. "I
expect his heart is still in the Service."

"Do you think so? I say, do tell me—has Miss Forrester married a
relation of yours?"

"My twin brother."

"Really! Do they live in this part?"

"My brother is in the Navy. She was only staying with me; she is
settling into a house somewhere in the Midlands."

Major Holme stroked his moustache thoughtfully. Tomina wondered what
were his thoughts. But Sir Alfred Digby came up at that minute, and
they had no more opportunity for private conversation.

She did not meet him again for some time.


The winter slowly passed. Tomina came out of her shell, joined the
Rectory working party, and undertook to help Mrs. Herbert with the
village clothing club every Saturday.

"I have a lot of lost time to redeem," she said to Mrs. Francis, "and
if there is anything that I am capable of doing, I mean to have a try
at it."

One afternoon, Mrs. Francis came to see Tomina in the dusk after tea.
They were very friendly by this time, but Tomina saw from her manner
that she had called for some special purpose, and she wondered what it
was.

Mrs. Francis seemed a little nervous and restless. Presently she asked
rather abruptly—

"Have you heard from Colonel Norman lately?"

"No," said Tomina, slightly surprised at the question. "We do not
correspond."

"You are old friends, are you not? He told me so."

Tomina did not reply.

Then Mrs. Francis laid her hand softly on her arm.

"I want to tell you something. I wonder if you will give me your
congratulations? We have cared for each other for some little time now,
and we are going to make our engagement known."

Tomina turned to her quickly; a pink flush rose into her cheeks.

"Of course I congratulate you! I hope you will be very happy. I guessed
it a short time ago."

She spoke quickly and feverishly.

Mrs. Francis gazed into the fire with dreamy eyes and a happy smile
about her lips.

"You have always liked him, have you not? I was sure of your sympathy."

Wild rebellion and misery were seething in Tomina's heart. She hardly
knew what she said.

Mrs. Francis's voice seemed to come to her from a great distance off,
and after a few commonplace remarks, the young widow rose to go.

When Tomina had wished her good-bye, she came into her sitting-room and
threw herself impulsively on her couch. Thor, seeing her face buried
in the cushions, crept up to her and licked her hand. When Tomina was
alone, he generally kept her company. Tomina took no notice of him at
first, until he gave a low whine of perplexity and uneasiness. Then she
sat up and clasped her arms round his shaggy head.

"Oh, Thor, dear Thor, you're a safe confidant! My punishment has come
to me at last for my pride and indifference! I might have known my own
heart before it was too late, but, oh, how fickle men are! And Major
Holme was quite right; he knows him well. He has, after all his earnest
protestations to me, suddenly transferred his affections. I suppose he
thought, and quite rightly too, that I ought to know my own mind, and
as I seemed so decided, he took me at my word. And, Thor, there hasn't
been a day when I have not been thinking of him. The future seemed
to hold only him in it! Aren't you sorry for me? Oh, I am sorry for
myself! It is a bitter and sudden blow."

Tears dropped on the dog's head. Thor looked up with tender, speaking
eyes. He knew his mistress was in trouble, and he wagged his tail to
and fro and watched her intently to see if there was anything he could
do to comfort or please her.

And then Susan came into the room, and Tomina sprang up with a gay
laugh on her lips.

"I have heard such interesting news, Susan, but perhaps I had better
not tell you yet."

"Indeed, I can guess, ma'am. 'Tis all over the village, and we're just
delighted. Mrs. Francis is too good to remain a widow all her life.
