Now, if only you and Sir Alfred could come together, it would fit in so
well, wouldn't it? And then we shouldn't lose you from amongst us."

"My dear Susan, Sir Alfred is a confirmed bachelor. You mustn't take to
matchmaking. And I am cut out for a spinster's life; you won't lose me
just yet, I can assure you!"

Then with a smile still on her lips and an agonising weight on her
heart, Tomina went out of the room.

She found it beyond her powers to discuss Mrs. Francis's engagement
with Susan.

She went early to bed that evening, but not to sleep.

Tomina had been half conscious for a long time that her feelings were
undergoing a change as regards Gerald Norman. She would never have
suffered so much all those years had her love for him entirely died.

As she had said to Doreen, life had lost its savour since their
quarrel. And yet she had resolutely and determinedly put him, or
sought to put him, out of her life. When he had insisted upon renewing
their acquaintance, she had at first passionately resisted it. When
later she gradually drifted into friendship, she still crushed the
inclination in her heart to forgive him. Now she knew she had nothing
to forgive; the pride and the wilfulness had all been on her side, and
no self-respecting man could have acted otherwise than he had done. Her
heart was still in his keeping. She was counting the days, too, when
she might see him again. She had already pictured him in imagination
once again pressing his claim upon her. This time she would yield, she
assured herself; she must do so—she could not hold out any longer.

And now this blow had fallen. She was no longer enshrined in his heart.
Another had taken her place. Her opportunity for yielding to his
importunity would never come. He did not want her.

The shock of it stunned, bewildered her. She tossed restlessly from
side to side. Sleep would not come. And then suddenly her soul found
repose in prayer.

"God is my Father," she thought, and a little thrill of hope and light
sprang up in her heart. "He loves me. He knows all about it. If I have
lost Gerald in this life, it must be for the best reason in the world.
I am not guiding my own life any longer. God is. I will trust Him. I
will wait and see what comes."

In a very few moments, the tumult in her heart was stilled and she fell
asleep.

CHAPTER XVIII

DOREEN'S HOME

"GOOD morning! I am working my round in this part of the world, and I
have a message for you from old Martha Tubbs. 'Tell her to come along
and hearten me up a bit, for I be terrible lonesome in bed'!"

It was Dr. Merton who stood outside Tomina's window. There was a breath
of spring in the air. Tomina had opened the window and was leaning out
of it, with somewhat wistful eyes, when the doctor had marched in at
her gate, and now was speaking to her.

He glanced at her with his keen professional eye.

"You are not looking well. What have you been doing to yourself?"

"I am not your patient any longer," said Tomina, with a little of her
old gay humour, "so don't ask inquisitive questions. And may I in
return ask you what you have been doing to yourself? You look as if you
had had a fortune left you!"

Dr. Merton certainly was in the best of spirits; there was an alert
spring in his bearing, a joyousness in his eyes that was not usually
there.

"Well, don't you think a fortune has befallen me?" he asked. "I hear
that you have been told, so I await your congratulations."

"I have been told nothing," said Tomina, looking at him in a puzzled
way.

"Surely Mrs. Francis came to see you yesterday?"

"Yes, she did."

But still Tomina remained mystified.

Dr. Merton looked at her, and his eyes began to twinkle.

"You think her wasted upon a country doctor?"

"You don't mean to say that you are the man? That she is engaged to
you?"

"Didn't she tell you so? We have known each other for a long time."

Tomina had been well trained from childhood in self-control. Her gay
life in London had been good discipline in that way. She looked up at
the doctor and smiled.

"I have been very stupid. You need not mention it to her, but I
quite misunderstood her when she was trying to tell me about it. I
am delighted, Dr. Merton. I don't know which of you I feel I ought
to congratulate more. You will make an ideal couple. And, oh, what
an amount of goodness there will be in one house! It will be almost
heaven! Or the courts of it at least."

Dr. Merton laughed.

"I assure you, we are both very human. But I felt you would be glad."

He remained talking some little time.

But when he had left her, Tomina felt almost as stupefied now by the
revulsion of feeling that passed through her, as she had before, when
she had first received the communication from Mrs. Francis which
she had so absurdly misunderstood. She went over in her mind the
conversation of the day before, and could only conclude that Mrs.
Francis's remark about Colonel Norman was quite irrelevant to the
announcement of her engagement.

"She must have thought I knew all about it, and I never had any idea!
How extraordinary that Susan should not have undeceived me! I suppose
she knows."

In she called Susan, who promptly said—

"I knew the doctor would come and tell you himself, ma'am. I saw him
coming up the path. He does deserve her, doesn't he? We've said a long
time ago they ought to make a match of it!"

"It is delightful," said Tomina.

She did not let Susan know of her mistake, but after her anguish of the
evening before, she felt she could hardly bear the relief and happiness
that came to her now.

She put on her hat and coat and, calling Thor, took a long walk. It did
her good, both mentally and physically. She came home with a soft fresh
colour in her cheeks and such light and sparkle in her eyes, that Susan
told a neighbour that Miss Gavin was getting more beautiful every day.


On the following Friday a lively discussion took place in the van upon
the merits of masterfulness in married life. Of course, the doctor's
engagement was the beginning of the discussion.

"I do say," said Mrs. Capper, "that they be a proper suited couple.
Mrs. Francis is just as gentle as the doctor be firm. There'll be no
fallin' out atween 'em!"

"Well, now," said Tomina, looking back over her shoulder at her
passengers, "do you think that the husband or the wife should rule in
the house?"

There was a good deal of laughter.

"Why, miss, the man ought to be master, Bible says."

"But if he drinks like a beast," said one young woman, sharply; "'tis
according to the natures of 'em!"

"There be Tom Lorrimer," said old Mrs. Capper, "who be under his wife's
thumb and afraid to wink his eye unless she gives 'im leave. The only
time he do be able to stand up to her is when he's had a drop too much,
and he told me he took it on purpose so as to master her!"

"I'd master him!" said another woman, vindictively.

"I'd like to see my husband a masterin' of me. The woman should be
missus of her house. She be in it an' keeps it; the man be only there
nights and the evenin's!"

"It's like this, miss," said the oldest woman there; "there be some men
born to be masters, but there be others that be best managed for. If
a man be born and bred to rule, his wife had best give in for sake of
peace, but if he be a poor weak sort o' chap, 'tis for the good of all
that his wife rules for 'im!"

"I believe you are right," said Tomina, thoughtfully. "Circumstances
alter cases. I used to think a man was a tyrant if he insisted upon
having his own way, but if it is a good way, the woman ought to yield."

Then Mrs. Judd, of the general shop spoke. "There be grand talk now of
women rulin' in Parlyment and havin' their votes. Miss Merryweather
believes in that; she gave us a long talkin' to one day, but I says to
'er—

"'Miss Merryweather,' I says, 'you may be very clever-headed, but
when it comes to makin' yourself heard in the Public Halls in that
there Parlyment House, why, my John would roar you down easy! If the
Almighty,' I says, 'wanted you to up and speak in Parlyment, he'd have
given you a voice to do it,' I says.

"Then she says to me, 'Do you know some women's voices carry farther
than men's; what about actresses?'

"'I don't know nothin' about actresses,' I says; 'they be not for
respectable ladies to copy.'

"And, of course, 'tis not only the voice, if men have always made laws
for hundreds of years, doesn't it follow that they be more clever at
it than the women? 'Tis just because 'tis a new thing that these here
women be wantin' to try their hand at it. They've nothin' else to do.
Let 'em get married, and have a dozen children to wash and feed and
clothe and a house to keep clean, an' a husband to cook for, they
wouldn't have the time or thought to be wantin' to be standin' amongst
the men in Parlyment!"

"But all women can't marry, Mrs. Judd," said Tomina, "and some think
that they could make better laws for women than the men can do."

There was a chorus of dissent at this.

"We'd rather the gentlemen ruled us than the ladies," said one. "A
gentleman—well, be he not so sharp-eyed and inquisitive as a lady?"

Here the van stopped to take up Mrs. Cayley.

Tomina greeted her warmly, and made her comfortable in the corner
behind her.

"We've been talking about husbands and wives, Mrs. Cayley, and which of
them ought to be the ruler. What do you think? Some of us don't like
to be ruled and managed by men, and some of us do. It depends upon the
man, don't you think?"

Mrs. Cayley looked round her with her sweet, gentle smile.

"I don't think there'll be any talk of ruling, where there be love for
each other. When love rules, you only see the love, and not the rule."

The women were silent.

"Yes," said Tomina, "of course, you are right. It is love and trust
that is wanted in married life, and then there will be no talk of rule."

Her thoughts went to Colonel Norman.

"My love was not deep enough or we should never have separated," she
said to herself. "I feel now that, if he were to want me, I would never
wish to have any other will but his."

The talk went on in the van, but Tomina did not join in. She gazed
wistfully and dreamily in front of her, and it was only when she
arrived at Damerton that she roused herself to her usual business.


A few days after this, she received a letter from Tom, the first he had
sent her since he sailed.

 "DEAR OLD TOMMIE,"

 "I am not a good fist at yarning with my pen, but I feel I treated you
a bit shabbily. My great happiness made me a selfish brute. Isn't she
wonderful? Are you two real friends? I want you to be. Have you gone to
stay with her yet? Take the first train and go! I want you to tell me
about her. She, like myself, is a bad correspondent; she never tells
what I want to know, and how can she? I charge you to do it, and you'll
earn my everlasting gratitude. Write me twenty pages. Don't stint paper
or ink. Tell me all about her, how she looks, where she is when you're
writing, what she is doing, what gown she is wearing, whether that rare
smile of hers creeps over her face. It comes so slowly and it goes so
quickly, but I learnt to watch for it, and whilst it lasted it warmed
my heart and made me feel I could go through fire and water to get it
again. Oh, Tommie, I can tell you I feel in a desert, stranded and
forlorn. My only comfort is living once again that blessed fortnight
in imagination. I shall hope for continual letters from you when you
get to her. Do go at once. She like's you, and she'll be lonely; she'll
miss me, though she is never one to proclaim it. This postbag is being
closed.

 "Adieu.

 "Your devoted Twin,

 "Tom."

"Oh dear, oh dear!" sighed Tomina. "How foolish young husbands are! He
little knows, poor boy, how indifferent Doreen is to both of us. And
how can I force myself upon her before she asks me? Is that all the use
I can be to him now, a kind of mirror reflecting for his benefit all
she says and does?"

But she was not surprised to get a letter from Doreen in a week's time
to ask her for a "short visit."

She once again placed Joe in charge of her van, and went to the Dower
House at Walburton.


Doreen met her at the station in a pretty little pony-trap. She told
Tomina it was her cousin's wedding gift to her. She was looking
brighter and happier, and talked away about her house and garden the
whole time. It was a drive of two and a half miles, and they arrived
at the house just before dusk. The last rays of the setting sun were
gilding all the windows as they entered.

"It's a good omen for your visit," said Doreen, pleasantly. "Now you
must not expect perfection, though I have aimed at it, and some of the
rooms are still in rather an unfinished state. But don't you think I
have a sweet house?"

Tomina could quite satisfy her on that point. Indeed, the quaint,
old-fashioned cottage, with its low, long irregular rooms, and
beautiful oak beams and panelling would have satisfied any artist's
heart. Doreen had good taste; and every room was a picture of dainty
freshness and comfort. They had a cosy tea in the drawing-room, and as
it was still early spring, a bright wood fire was very acceptable.

Tomina tried to feel that this was Tom's home, but she could not
picture it.

When Doreen took her into the third sitting-room, she looked at the
rose-covered chintzes, and the fragile Chippendale chairs and tables,
and asked—

"Is this Tom's smoking-room?"

Doreen raised her eyebrows.

"This is the morning room. I write and work here, of course. I dare say
Tom will be able to bring his pipe in when he likes."

"But have you no man's room?" questioned Tomina, persistently. "When he
is home, where is he to sit?"

"With me, I should think," said Doreen, and a cold inflection was in
her tone. "Considering how much he will be away, I have not, I confess,
furnished a room for his sole and absolute use."

Tomina was silent.

"There is a room—the housekeeper's," went on Doreen, in milder tones,
"that I dare say Tom may like to use. My maids will not want it. I only
keep two, and they have such a beautiful kitchen that they do not need
a servants' hall. But there will be plenty of time to furnish that
later."

"Oh yes," said Tomina, quickly, "you will be able to do that, of
course. I know Tom's ways when he is at home, and he would be bound to
have a den of his own."

After dinner they sat over the fire and talked. But Tomina wisely kept
off the subject of her brother as long as she could.

"What will you do with yourself when your house and garden is in
perfect order, and you have no more planning and contriving?" she asked
Doreen.

Doreen sat back in her chair with clasped hands in her lap. She was
always composed and still, her face was a striking contrast to Tomina's
sparkling vivacious one. Her eyes were clear and steady, as she
answered slowly—

"I shall live in my books when I am not gardening."

"What kind of books do you live in?"

"It depends on my mood," said Doreen. "Poetry when I am feeling flat
and dull. Philosophy when I want to think."

"You read to take yourself away from your surroundings?"

"Yes, always."

"I don't. I like to get knowledge for practical purposes. But then
it is only lately that I have tried to do useful things, and I am so
ignorant! You would laugh to see some of my purchases in the Damerton
book shop. Handbooks on cookery, of course, and medicine in the home,
and the first course in nursing and ambulance, and dog diseases, and
scientific dressmaking, and bee keeping, and gardening."

"But why have you so many hobbies?"

"Oh, they're not hobbies exactly, only I don't feel so lamentably
ignorant when I drive my van. My passengers appeal to me on every kind
of topic. Two of them had a dispute on the efficacy of onions for
poultices the other day. I had never heard of the onion cure. They
expect me to be a regular encyclopædia of knowledge. And I don't like
disappointing them."

"I wonder how soon you will tire of that van?"

"Not just yet."

"But you will not want to spend the rest of your life in that part of
the country?"

"I shall never go back to London."

"You will stagnate there."

"That is what Alice Merryweather always says. But I find I am always
making new discoveries and learning new things."

"You ought to marry," Doreen said, looking at her with sombre eyes.
"You have taken up a 'rôle' that does not suit you. A woman carrier
to my mind ought to be a gaunt, big-limbed, hard-featured person. You
don't look as if you could weather a puff of wind!"

"There I am a fraud, for I've weathered many storms, and have never
been in better health all my life. And marriage is not necessarily the
sole aim and object of a woman's life. I could not undertake it without
real love!"

The words slipped out of her lips before she thought. But Doreen looked
at her with a calm smile.

"Love is tempestuous, and uncertain, it never lasts. I do not think I
know a single couple amongst my married friends who keep their love
after ten years of married life. They get accustomed to each other,
indifferent I should say. Love is 'not' necessary, Tomina. It is a
waste of one's heart's blood often."

Tomina shook her head.

"I can't argue with you. A married life without love must be like—well,
like bread without any butter."

"Bread is very wholesome," said Doreen, with the shadow of a smile.
Then she rose from her seat and stood with her back to the fire, and
her hands clasped behind her. Looking at Tomina almost tenderly, she
said: "I feel so many years older than you, Tomina. It is difficult to
kindle a fire when it has burnt out and there are only ashes left. It
will never be kindled again in my heart. It is of no use us uttering
all these platitudes; you are not pleased with my attitude towards your
brother, but you need not be afraid. I mean to be serene, and helpful;
he will never find that I shall fail him."

"But he does love you so intensely," said Tomina; "it is such waste on
his part, is it not?"

"No, I think I like it. It soothes and comforts me."

"But why should he give you so much and you give him so little?"

Tomina's tone vibrated with feeling.

"I am giving him a great deal, and he knows it. As you said just now,
marriage is not the sole aim and object of a woman's life. It was not
mine, and I told him so. I warned him beforehand what I could give him
and what I could not. And if you leave us alone, you will find we shall
be as united a couple as the majority are."

"I shall never mention the subject again," said Tomina, quietly.

And she did not.


The following morning she was shown the quaint old garden, and Tomina
fell in love with it at once. Crocuses and early daffodils were
springing under the old red brick walls, the brown buds on trees and
shrubs were swelling visibly, and birds were singing ecstatically as
they flew here and there collecting for their nests. Doreen pointed out
the wooden seats in sheltered corners, and said she meant to bring her
writing and books out of doors as soon as the weather became warm.

"Do you know where I first got my love for a garden?" she said. "It was
about six years ago before I went out to Australia. I visited a convent
in France, and they took me into the garden, where some of them were
gardening, and some of them walking about and reading their books. It
was summer-time, and the peace and stillness under the shady trees,
and the sweet scent of the lilies and heliotrope and lemon verbena
followed me for years. It made me wish to be a nun. And when I came
into this legacy, my cup of happiness seemed full. I shall wander round
and imbibe all the scents and sounds and sweetness that will be here.
You see it in spring now. You have no idea of the wealth of bloom and
beauty that will come by-and-by!"

"You are making one envy you," said Tomina, with her gay laugh. "I can
see you on a summer's morning. It seems to suit you. You must always
wear a white gown rather long in the skirt, so that you can trail
properly over the green lawns, with a basket of roses hanging on your
arm, and a book in your hand. Then you can pose by a sundial, and
follow with your eyes the flight of rooks or doves from a dovecote. Oh,
it will be charming! But there must be an observer somewhere, or your
dream-garden will never be described in the orthodox fashion."

"You are laughing at me! I shall welcome no intruder. It is the peace
and privacy that I shall value. Can't you understand a little? I have
been at every one's beck and call since I left school, always making
myself agreeable when I did not feel so, always entertaining, always
talking small talk, paying empty compliments, making friends with
people I thoroughly despised, accepting their hospitality, joining in
empty chatter, in gay trivialities, never having a moment to myself,
smiling when my heart was aching, pretending and posing and acting day
after day, till my soul sickened and withered under it all!"

At last Tomina saw something of the fire beneath Doreen's still serene
exterior. She slipped her arm into hers and gave it a little squeeze.

"Of course, I understand, dear," she said. "Haven't I gone through it
myself? Aren't hundreds of women doing it still? And yet half of them
don't realise the pitiful slavery of it, and how they could break away
and call their souls their own if they choose. I broke away, and I
am never going back to it again. You and I are fortunate. We have no
obligations to make us do so. I know that some are nobly doing their
duty towards husbands and parents and children, and could not make a
fresh life for themselves. They must stay in it, and endure. But there
are others who could do as we have done."

Tomina's eyes were misty as she spoke. She went on—

"I seem to feel so much more for people than I used to do. I think it
is my van. I hear and see so much of human life as we go trundling
along. Sometimes I wonder, if I went back to my London life, whether
I could help any girls to escape from it, and see what a much bigger
purpose might be in their lives; and how we were sent into the world
for that purpose. But, of course, I might not succeed in making them
see it, and might only do more harm than good."

"I don't think the modern girl would stand much preaching," said Doreen.

"Perhaps not. And we are all led differently, are we not? I remember
telling my doctor that I felt like a traveller leaving the busy
high-road and turning down a green bye-lane. That was when I first
arrived in the country. Now, as I look back, what a beautiful country
my green lane has led me into, and what vistas and views I have got!"

"Views of what?" Doreen asked, looking down upon her curiously.

"Of the Golden Unseen Land," said Tomina, softly.

And Doreen said no more.


Tomina enjoyed this visit of hers. She and Doreen gardened
industriously every morning.

They drove out in the afternoon, and occasionally had to receive
callers. Long letters went out to Tom, but Tomina was the one who wrote
most, and she described her life in every detail.

Now and then in talking together over the fire in the evening, they
would touch the fringe of deep subjects. Tomina was quite ready to talk
of what she had lately experienced, but she was also ready to wait, and
draw back, if Doreen showed impatience or distaste of such talk.

Time slipped away, and then Tomina said she must return home. As London
was on her way, she determined to stay with her stepmother for a few
days before returning to Little Honeycombe.

Doreen parted from her with sincere regret. She drove her to the
station in her pony-trap.

They had been rather silent for some time, when she suddenly startled
Tomina by saying abruptly—"You spoke the other day of every one having
a high purpose in their lives. What is yours?"

"To be a carrier," said Tomina, slowly and sweetly. "Wasn't it strange
that I tumbled upon my vocation in real carelessness of soul? I mean
with God's help to be a carrier to the end of my life. The legend of
St. Christoforos has helped me so much."

Doreen looked interested.

"I used to be so fond of that story when I was a child," she said. "I
think you would always help people with their burdens, Tomina. You
are so sympathetic, and yet so gay. I am a very grave person, as you
know, and as such appreciate brightness so very much. Sometimes people
crudely imagine that the sober-looking cannot enjoy fun. Why, it is
those that feel cold that enjoy the sunshine most!"

"It's a lovely thought, isn't it?" said Tomina, with glowing eyes.
"Life does seem so hard to so many, it is like struggling through
rushing water; and if one can help each other through, it is glorious.
I have been so awfully selfish. I have sat with folded hands, refusing
to put my little finger out to help anybody. Our Rector's wife is quite
overwhelmed sometimes. I refused to go near her because she was 'good'
and tried to do me good. Oh, how I did dislike her at first, and how
much I like her now!"

"Do you? I should never like any one who 'tried to do me good.' But I
should like some one who shouldered my burden with me and stepped along
a bit of my way. And you have done that, Tomina. Life has been better
and sweeter since you came."

Tomina could not speak. When she kissed her in parting, she said—

"I shall often think of you in your retreat. We are real friends, are
we not? The recluse and the carrier!"

And at Tomina's happy laugh, Doreen gave one of her rare sweet smiles.
Tomina carried that away with her, and thought that, after all, Tom was
a lucky man.

CHAPTER XIX

TOMINA'S DESPERATION

LADY GAVIN welcomed Tomina very affectionately. Her niece, Madge
Dudley, a girl about Tomina's own age, was staying with her. She was
genuinely glad to see Tomina again.

"We have heard such absurd stories about you, that I am glad to see
you much the same as usual. I pictured you in a smock, or is that male
attire in your part of the world? You hardly look like our country
cousins. Tell me what you have been doing with yourself."

They were dining alone, a rather unusual event, and so talk became
personal.

Tomina tried to tell her.

Then Lady Gavin said—

"As you have quite got over that affair with Gerald Norman, I'll tell
you that I have been spending the afternoon with his mother. They came
from the Riviera a week ago and are in some nice rooms overlooking
Kensington Gardens. It's quite painful to see her, and as for him, I
wouldn't know him. He is almost ridiculously devoted to her, and she,
poor thing, cries herself silly whenever he leaves her."

"It's rather touching," said Madge. "I saw him the other day, Tomina;
he was buying flowers for her, but he looks quite careworn and aged.
I couldn't get a laugh out of him. Do you remember the day he marched
into this room in a perfect fury because you had not met him at the
arranged time? Billy Cassel turned up, and he was so amusing that
you forgot the appointment altogether. I can see Gerald now in his
immaculate frock-coat and light spats and hear him say to you:

"'I have been waiting three-quarters of an hour! We are punctual in the
Service!'

"And then you smiled at him perfectly calm. 'But I am not
in—service—yet!' you said.

"And he turned quite white, bowed, and returned you the tickets—for a
'matinée,' wasn't it?—and took his leave promptly. I can remember how
frightened and indignant you looked."

"I am sure I never showed that I was frightened of him."

"I always knew you were!"

"How is Mrs. Norman?" said Lady Gavin.

"She will never be better. She can talk, and she can be lifted into
a carriage or bath-chair and taken out, but she has to be fed like a
child; her limbs are powerless."

"It is like a living death," said Madge. "I don't think people should
be seen when they are like that; it makes one feel so miserable."

"Oh, Madge, surely one's own feelings don't matter!" said Tomina.

"Mine do very much. That doesn't sound like you, Tomina; you used to
say that you never intended to take other people's troubles on your
shoulders, for your own were far too heavy!"

"Did I? I am a selfish creature at the best of times."

Through all the light chatter that ensued, Tomina's thoughts were
far-away. It had been a distinct shock to her to hear that Colonel
Norman was once again in her near neighbourhood. She felt she longed
to see him and yet shrank from doing so. Her last waking thought that
night was—

"I must not leave town without seeing him. I may never meet him again."

She heard the next day that they were going down to Torquay very soon.
She was making up her mind to call upon Mrs. Norman when Lady Gavin
informed her that she had every minute of the day mapped out for her.

"We do not get you up here very often. I am opening a bazaar at
Knightsbridge this morning at twelve o'clock—I want you to come with
me; and we have an 'at home' this afternoon and a few people to dine.
Your friends will want to see you, Tomina."

Tomina sighed, but was very submissive.

Madge laughed at her resigned expression.

"You have been getting lazy in the country."

"Yes, my days there are not so full as yours."

"You have forgotten how we work. I mean to cut the 'at home' to-day.
You can take my place, and I shan't be missed."

"What are you going to do?"

"I'm going to my dressmaker at once. Will you come with me and give
me your advice? It won't take you long. And then I'm going down to
Richmond to lunch with the Fitzpatricks."

Tomina agreed to accompany her to her dressmaker's, promising to meet
her stepmother at the bazaar later on. When she and Madge eventually
separated, Tomina found that she had nearly an hour on her hands, so
she sauntered up Regent Street, and looked in at the shop windows.

Suddenly she met Colonel Norman face to face. As she shook hands with
him, her heart beat violently. He had been so much in her thoughts that
the unexpected sight of him almost unnerved her.

But outwardly Tomina was calm and smiling.

"I have been hearing from my stepmother that you are in town. I hope
your mother is better?"

"I think she is. Lady Gavin very kindly came to see her. They were old
friends, you know. It is good to see you again."

The light was in his tired eyes as he spoke. Tomina noticed for the
first time that he was getting grey.

"I am only in town for a day or two."

Then Colonel Norman spoke in a hesitating tone—

"I wonder if you would befriend me? I'm somewhat in a dilemma, for I'm
very ignorant—but I'm doing a little shopping for my mother. She has
begged me to do it myself. Her maid doesn't please her. It's a—a hat
or a bonnet for her, and she gave me the name of a milliner somewhere
about here."

"I shall be delighted," said Tomina. "What is the name of the milliner?
I expect it is 'Le Brun.'"

"That's it. A French place, she said."

They were close to the shop. Tomina accompanied him in, half amused,
half touched, by his natural bewilderment in his task.

"It must be something bright and cheerful, but which will be
comfortable lying down," said the Colonel, gravely.

"We'll look at some motor bonnets," said Tomina, gaily; "they have
perfect dreams here, and I'm sure we can pick out one which she will
like."

In a few minutes they were deep in the important choice. Grey, blue,
mauve, and rose bonnets of all sorts and shades were shown them. Tomina
insisted upon trying some on herself.

"I declare! I shall get one for my van. It's just what I want on a cold
and windy day, for spring is very stormy at Little Honeycombe. How do
you like this, now? It would do for either your mother or myself?"

She turned to Colonel Norman, looking perfectly bewitching in a black
satin bonnet fitting close to her head with a tiny bunch of cherries on
either side over her ears, and tied with cherry-coloured ribbon under
the chin.

"I think it would suit your lady perfectly, sir," said the milliner.

Tomina blushed and laughed as she turned away.

Colonel Norman's look remained with her, but there seemed to be sadness
as well as tender admiration in it, which rather puzzled her at the
time.

After a little further consultation, a pretty mauve bonnet trimmed with
a tiny wreath of heliotrope, was ordered to be sent on approval for
Mrs. Norman, and Tomina ordered the cherry bonnet for herself.

As she left the shop, she said lightly—

"We have done our shopping very satisfactorily. I don't know what my
old women will say to me! But they ought to like my choice, for they
are exactly like our grandmother's coal-scuttles, are they not? Now I
must leave you! I ought to be in Knightsbridge at twelve. Will you call
a taxi for me?"

"Can you come to see my mother once before you leave?" Colonel Norman
said after he had hailed a taxi. "Could you come to-morrow afternoon—to
see if our choice is approved?"

He tried to smile. But Tomina's quick eyes saw that he was weary in
body and soul; and her heart ached for him.

"Yes, I will," she said promptly; "I will come between three and four."

He wrung her hand, and they parted. Tomina was singularly grave and
sober for the rest of that day. Her friends agreed that her time in the
country had improved her health but not her spirits. And she, hearing
the flow of chatter and laughter around her, wondered that she could
ever have been in it and of it.


The next day, she went round to the house in the quiet terrace
overlooking the gardens. She found Mrs. Norman alone, but she was
supremely delighted to see her. She was lying back on her couch looking
haggard and aged, though her maid had tried to hide the ravages of
sickness and years by a liberal coating of enamel and rouge. The
fretful discontent in her voice was very noticeable.

"Tomina Gavin, is it not? Well, I am glad to see you. My illness seems
to have cut me off entirely from all my friends. You know of the
terrible time that I have had? I can't speak of it. And doctors are
a fraud. They take your money and do you no good. Look at me, still
stretched flat on my back and unable to move a single limb, and I have
been like this for over six months. Don't you think they ought to have
cured me or found me some remedy by this time? Sit down and talk to me.
Gerald is out; he had to go to our lawyer about business, he said. It
is most inconsiderate, I told him. There is no reason why the lawyer
should not come here, and I should have enjoyed listening to their
conversation. But men are so secretive about business matters, and
Gerald is very obstinate. I spoiled him as a boy, and I suppose I am
reaping the result."

She paused for breath.

"How did you like your bonnet?" Tomina asked.

"Oh, you helped to choose it, did you not? I liked it fairly well. Of
course, I have never worn such things, and I do hate novelty in any
shape or form. I said to Gerald, if he liked to walk by the side of an
old crock—made old before her time—she had better be as respectably
clothed as she could be! It's an awful existence; I wonder if you have
any conception how awful it is to me! It seems superfine torture to fix
on me, above all others, as the one to drag on this living death!"

Her voice broke—she began to cry weakly.

Tomina tried to comfort her.

"No one knows how long my days are," the invalid sobbed, "or how weary
the nights. If I had a daughter, it might be easier for me. I suppose
Gerald does his best, but men are no nurses, they are so dense, so
clumsy, so unsympathetic. Sometimes he goes out for half an hour, he
says. I wait and watch the clock till he comes back. It is all I can
do. The half-hour stretches into an hour, and then he tells me he met
somebody, or my errands took him longer than he thought. And last night
I was wakeful, and I wanted to change my position. I won't have the
nurse sleep with me—she worries me to death—and so Gerald sleeps in the
dressing-room. Well, I called and called—so like a man, he was sound
and fast asleep! And when he was roused at last, I was crying from
weakness and weariness. I can't tell you how I suffer!"

Tomina listened and sympathised, and Mrs. Norman talked on, about
herself and her pitiful lot.

At last, Tomina said—

"I feel a little shy about suffering. I have never had it, but there is
special reward and blessing for those who bear it patiently. Doesn't it
comfort you to think of the time when there will be no more of it?"

"You mean when I am dead?" Mrs. Norman cried shrilly. "Oh, how cruel of
you to remind me of such a thing! Do you mean to say that you think I
shall never get back the use of my limbs in this life? I will, I must.
I'll try every specialist in the country. What is the good of spending
money on anything but health? I say to Gerald, when he speaks of taking
a house,—

"'What good does a house and grounds do me? Why six feet square is
enough for my wants at present. My couch is my dwelling-place.'

"I told him I would never, never go back near the Court. I am glad he
has sold it, for we wanted the money. I mean to try every cure in the
world before I rest satisfied that I can't be cured. We are going down
to Torquay. I have heard that there is a very clever doctor there. And
I am strong enough to have massage now, they say. I have had a little
of it, but it worries me, and I generally send away my masseuse after
the third day. Gerald is vexed when I do it, but he is often vexed
now with his poor old mother. Must you be going? Gerald ought to be
in—he knows that I cannot bear to be alone. Men are so thoughtless and
self-absorbed. He never seems to realise the long hours that I have to
lie here doing nothing."

Tomina took her leave in deep depression of spirit. She was
disappointed that she had not seen Colonel Norman again; she had
counted upon it, and it looked as if he had purposely avoided her. Then
her heart was overwhelmed with pity as she thought of his devotion and
selfless life so unappreciated by his mother. She felt, with a shock,
that suffering had not refined or beautified Mrs. Norman's character.
On the contrary, she seemed more selfish, and more peevish than when
she had seen her in the early days of her illness.

"No wonder he looks weary and aged!" she mused. "Why does he not
provide her with a lady companion and go right away from her for the
time? It is spoiling his life."

The following day she received a letter from him.

 "MY DEAR TOMINA,

 "Forgive me for writing instead of seeing you, and a thousand thanks
for your visit of mercy yesterday! My heart sorely wanted to be with
you; my head took me right away from your zone. I feel in justice
to you, Tomina—because I would not have you regard me as fickle or
changeable—I must explain, that after long and earnest thought, I have
come to the conclusion that I must remain an unmarried man. My poor
little mother is my first consideration now. I shall devote the rest
of my life to her. She may live many years, the doctors say, and I
would neither remove myself from her reach when she so pathetically
depends upon me, nor yet bring a young wife into such a depressing and
unwholesome atmosphere.

 "Perhaps you will say I am wasting paper and ink in explaining my
position to you, as you have repeated to me again and again that you
could not be to me what you once were—and what in my heart I try to
imagine that you still are. This may be so. But if I do not throw
myself in your way again, it is not because I have ceased to care for
you. There is an inner shrine in my soul which you will always occupy.
I can never, never change; but I need not and will not ever bring
myself before you again. This is in fact a letter of farewell. I want
you—God knows how much. I want—you—to be happy. I believe you will be.
I like to think of you amongst your devoted villagers in the peaceful
country. And if by-and-by, the man of your choice turns up—ah! well—I
am not such a stoic as I thought myself—we will not mention him. Only
Gerald Norman will always be

 "YOUR TRUE FRIEND.

 "If you send me no reply, I shall understand that you have accepted
and understood my explanation."

Tomina read this letter in the privacy of her bedroom. She looked dazed
at first, and then she burst into a passion of tears. She wept till she
could weep no more, and brought on a racking headache. Then promptly
she put herself to bed, and stayed there, refusing to see any one at
all that day. The next morning, she appeared at breakfast very white,
with dark circles round her eyes, but with very resolute lips and
shining face.

"What is the matter with you, Tomina?" her stepmother asked. "I have
not seen that look upon your face since that time when, as a child, you
slipped off by yourself to the dentist to have two teeth out!"

Tomina tried to laugh.

"I remember! I desperately wanted a five-shilling kodak—and you always
gave me half a crown for each tooth out. I shall never forget my fury
with the dentist who refused to extract sound teeth out of my head!"

"Are you meditating any such idea of self-sacrifice now?"

"Oh no, indeed! I am struggling with a mental problem, that is all."

She wrote a reply to the letter received.

 "MY DEAR GERALD,

 "My pride, my waywardness, my obstinacy, have all melted into one
intense desire to make up to you for all I have made you suffer in
these past years! I am going, metaphorically, to throw myself into
your arms! Of course, I had a miserable sleepless night after that
letter received from you. It was not sheer contradictoriness, Gerald,
that made me desire what I had previously spurned, when you told me
you could offer it no longer. I felt as though, if I accepted your
resolution, and passed out of your life, I would be the most miserable
and disconsolate creature in the universe. And you gave me no option in
the matter. I lay hour after hour trying to see a way out of it, and
then a delicious ray of early morning sunshine came stealing into my
room and alighted on a small picture which I always carry about with
me. It is a picture that reminds me that I am 'Tomina the Carrier.'

 "And the light flashed through me, and I saw that I should not be
unmaidenly or forward if I persisted in your reconsidering your hasty
decision.

 "You are trying to do what no man ought to do, or is able to do. Your
mother told me she ought to have a daughter to relieve you. I want to
be that daughter. It will be no depressing or unwholesome atmosphere
for me. The two of us together will carry the burden so lightly that
it will prove to be no burden at all. I am in training for carrying,
remember. It is my vocation, and I have hugged it and am delighting
in it, and if I can smooth even a tiny wrinkle out of your careworn
brow, or bring the glimmer of a smile to your face and heart it will be
sufficient reward. For, perhaps, I ought to tell you that the love I
once had for you, and which I thought was crushed out of existence, has
been there all the time. And if we love each other, Gerald, surely it
can be no hardship to help your poor little mother through some of her
weary days? I shall wait in town for your answer.

 "Your repentant

 "TOMINA."

And when her letter was sent, Tomina possessed her soul in patience,
and was sweetness itself to her stepmother, accompanying her
everywhere, and making herself pleasant to all her old acquaintances
and friends. Colonel Norman's reply was brief.

 "MY DEAR TOMINA,

 "Your sweet pity for me and my circumstances came like balm to the
wounded. No, I cannot let you sacrifice yourself. You do not realise
the life I lead. It is not fit for any woman save the very strongest
and most professional of nurses. Do not let us torture each other any
more. I could not and would not allow you to carry the least corner of
my burden. No true man would. And I care for you too much to bring you
into a life that would sap every bit of brightness and spirit out of
you.

 "May God bless you! We leave to-morrow afternoon for Torquay.

 "Your affectionate friend,

 "GERALD."

Tomina read this in her bedroom and stood looking out of her window
with despair in her eyes and heart. She came downstairs and told her
stepmother that she must return to little Honeycombe the next afternoon.

"I hate London," she said, with quiet emphasis. "I am always tossed
up and down when I am in it, and I am longing for the peace of the
country."

"Dr. Carisbrooke would like to see you before you go," said Lady Gavin.
"I met him to-day, and he asked after you."

"Oh, I am not in the doctor's hands any more!"

"He is more a friend than a doctor. He is coming to lunch to-morrow."

"I can catch the three-o'clock train then from Paddington."

"Tomina is as restless and excitable as ever," Lady Gavin said to her
niece later that day. "She is full of moods. Yesterday she was almost
brilliant in her talk and laughter; to-day she is like stone, utterly
unresponsive and dull. And she is so thin! I shall tell Dr. Carisbrooke
that I think she has been in the country long enough. It is actually
making her religious!"

Madge laughed.

"Is that the climax? Why shouldn't she be religious if she likes? We
are supposed to be a Christian country and nation. I very often wish I
was; it makes people really content, they say. And it has altered her.
She used to be one of the most selfish of persons, and she is certainly
not that now."


Tomina tried to fill up every moment of her time those last days in
town, so that she would not be obliged to think. The next morning she
went out shopping, for she wished to take back some presents to some of
her old women. She was in Oxford Street, when once again she saw Gerald
Norman approaching her.

A panic of fright seized her. She could not and would not meet him
again. Hastily, she turned and crossed the street to avoid him. Then,
in a moment, she missed her footing and fell immediately in front of
a motor. For an instant she thought all was over, then somebody had
caught her and swung her clear of the traffic.

The next moment she was standing safe and sound on the pavement with
somebody's arm round her. She heard as if in a dream a voice saying:
"That was a near shave!"

And then "Tomina!" was breathed into her ear.

She opened her eyes.

"Of course, I might have known it would be you," she said with a
quick-caught sobbing breath; "you always come to my rescue! We can't
escape each other!"

Colonel Norman made no reply. He only held her tight. Then, calling a
taxi, he helped her in.

"Are you hurt, sweetheart? Tell me."

Tomina shook her head. The fall and the fright had unnerved her. She
leant her head against the broad shoulder so close to her and began to
cry.

And Colonel Norman was shaken out of his cold grave demeanour. For one
awful second, he had thought the motor was crushing the life out of his
only love. He gave an involuntary shudder even now.

"You ought not to go about alone," he said. "If I had not seen you! You
were dashing so recklessly across!"

"I was running away from you," murmured Tomina.

Then she shifted her position and sat up.

"I am better now. It gave me such a fright, for I never expected to
be alive. I suppose, if I had been killed, I shouldn't have been
frightened at all."

But Colonel Norman did not move his arm. He was still holding her.

"I wanted to see you," he said, "but I did not like to call. I am
afraid you must have thought my last letter very hard. It really is for
your own sake, dearest."

"You mustn't call me 'dearest.' I am nothing to you now. Yes, I did
think it hard. If you thought more of me, you would know that you are
breaking my heart."

"You could not live the life that I am doing, Tomina."

"I could, and I should enjoy it. I should try to brighten up your
mother and would remove some of your furrows of care. I should manage
you both. I am quite capable of it. Where are we going, Gerald? I must
get home. I am going down to Little Honeycombe this afternoon." Tomina
was rapidly recovering her composure.

"I ordered him to drive on till I told him to stop. I am sure your
stepmother and all who care for you would say it was criminal to tie
you to my mother's bedside. She is very exacting, poor little thing,
and would demand the uttermost from us both."

Then Tomina had an inspiration.

"I believe I have just time to go and see your mother and wish her
good-bye. Shall I? Would you tell the driver where to go?"

"That's most kind of you. She will be so pleased."

Directions were given, and then Tomina said—

"Now we'll be very nice to each other for the rest of our time. I do
feel that I owe my life to you. How quick you were! Even now, I don't
know how you did it. But you always do turn up in the nick of time. Do
you remember when you rescued me from the tree?"

Colonel Norman smiled.

"You were very angry, were you not?"

"Ah! I think I have altered since then."

Then silence fell upon them, but Tomina did not feel embarrassed.
She was only quite content to sit by his side and think over her
inspiration.


A little later, she was greeting Mrs. Norman affectionately.

"It's good to see you again, my dear. Come and sit down and tell me
what you have been doing with yourself."

"I have been falling down in the middle of the street and your son has
been picking me up."

"How horrible!"

"Yes, wasn't it?"

Tomina went on to describe her accident. Colonel Norman stood at the
window listening and occasionally putting in a word.

"We are going to Torquay this afternoon," said Mrs. Norman. "I am
really quite sorry to go. I have no friends there, and I do like to see
people sometimes. Do you ever come down to Torquay?"

"No, I don't think I have ever been there, but perhaps I may come some
day."

"I wish you would."

Then Tomina got up from her seat and knelt down by the side of Mrs.
Norman's couch. Putting her hand softly in hers, she said with great
ease and quietness—

"Mrs. Norman, you said the other day that you would like to have a
daughter. Now, I have come to ask you if you will accept me as one."

For a moment the silence was so intense that you could have heard a pin
drop.

Then great tears rose in Mrs. Norman's eyes. She looked across at her
son.

"I should like it very much. Gerald, why did you not tell me?"

Before he could speak, Tomina said hurriedly—

"He is not quite sure whether we should all be happy together, but we
would, would we not? I could stay at home and take care of you when he
went out. We should be glad to get rid of him sometimes. And I'm sure
I could shop for you better than he does. I would choose you lovely
bonnets!"

"My dear child, I am very, very glad. I sometimes think I shall not be
a burden for very long. If I don't get better, I must get worse. There
is no doubt of that."

Then Colonel Norman came over to the couch, and Tomina rose to her
feet. She had a pink flush on her cheeks, and she dared not look at
him. For one moment she wondered if he were too angry to speak.

Then he took her hand in his, and, in a deeply moved voice, he said—

"You will give us your blessing, mother? We mean to make you happy,
if we can. I feel as if Tomina has been snatched from death to-day,
because you and I need her so badly. She says she will look after us
both."

"So that is all settled very comfortably," said Tomina. There was a
little tremor in her voice as she bent over Mrs. Norman and kissed her.
"And now you will see me down at Torquay before very long, I expect. I
must go now. My doctor is coming to lunch with us, and I promised that
I should be back."

She smiled and nodded to her and then left the room. Colonel Norman
followed her into the hall, and then for an instant they stood and
looked into each other's eyes.

"Don't be angry, Gerald. There was no other way. Tell me you are not
sorry I did it."

"My darling!"

He could get no further than those two words, but he took her into his
arms and kept her there.

CHAPTER XX

HER INSPIRATION

"AND so, you see, Dr. Carisbrooke, I am perfectly cured."

"I really think you are. I am afraid you are not placid enough to grow
much flesh."

Lunch was over. Tomina was her old self again, impetuous and gay, and
very dignified by turn.

As her stepmother said, "One could rarely guess what she was going to
do or say next."

But the old doctor, remembering the nervous, strained expression of
her face when he last saw her, was very pleased at the result of the
country's quiet.

"And now I suppose your year of exile is over, and you will come
back amongst us, and live the same old life until you have another
breakdown?"

"Oh dear, no! You are responsible for having changed my whole life.
You buried me, and now I have come to life again, but with quite a
different outlook, and—if I may say it—with a different soul."

She and the doctor were talking quietly together in the drawing-room.
Lady Gavin had gone to her room to rest, and Madge had gone out.

Dr. Carisbrooke nodded gravely.

"I congratulate you—"

"So you will not see me pursuing pleasure, for all I am worth, any
more. And I have learnt to love the country and hate the town."

"What else?"

"Much that I cannot talk about. You see and study human beings a good
deal, don't you? Do you ever tell them the secret of real peace and
content?"

Then Dr. Carisbrooke's eyes glistened.

"Ah!" he said. "You have indeed been learning things in your retreat."

"Well, now, I'll tell you what I've been doing."

Tomina gave a short account of her van.

"I can't tell you what it has done for me! It has made me determine
to be a carrier for the rest of my life. Not always in a van, but
anywhere, everywhere. For, Dr. Carisbrooke, life is a burden to most
people. Not when we are very young, but as we live on, it gets pretty
heavy for some shoulders. And I'm going to try and help carry for
those who are sinking under the weight of theirs. I can't tell you
what I have learnt merely by listening to the everyday sorrows and
difficulties of village people. It has made me see that those who only
shoulder a light burden like myself are bound to share the heavier ones
of others."

"Is this Tomina Gavin?" said Dr. Carisbrooke, smiling. "You have indeed
changed. And so you're going back to your country life again? God bless
you, child!"

The old doctor was visibly moved.

Then Tomina smiled suddenly and brilliantly upon him.

"I think I must confide in you what I have not told my stepmother yet.
I am going to marry Gerald Norman. You know him?"

"Should think I did. Have been attending his mother since she came to
town. They're leaving for Torquay this afternoon at my recommendation.
I want her to see a friend of mine there. Well, I always felt there was
something wrong in the break between you and him, but I'm bound to say
he has come out in a marvellous light. His life for some months has
been a severe test, physically and mentally."

"I know it, and I mean to go shares."

"Is that why you are marrying him?" asked the old doctor, sharply.

Tomina laughed.

"That's why I am 'insisting' upon marrying him soon. Don't ask any more
questions, Dr. Carisbrooke. I'm the happiest woman alive, and Gerald
and I understand each other."

When the doctor had gone, Tomina ran lightly up to her stepmother's
room. She found her lying on her couch, reading a novel.

"Is your cab here?" she asked, turning to look at her stepdaughter, and
wondering at the brightness of her face. "What does Dr. Carisbrooke say
to you? Doesn't he think it absolutely unnecessary for you to banish
yourself from town any more?"

"He thinks it the best thing I can do. I haven't much time, but I would
rather tell you than write it. I am going to meet Gerald Norman and
his mother at Paddington, and go down for part of the way with them.
I branch off at Bristol. They go on to Torquay. I am really going to
marry him, after all."

Lady Gavin put down her book in amazement.

"Well, you astonish me! I did not know you had had anything to do with
him for years. And what is going to become of his mother? I suppose he
will put her in some nursing home, or get a companion for her."

"Indeed, no—I am going to help Gerald nurse her!"

"It's preposterous, Tomina! You couldn't and wouldn't do it! Why, your
life would be slavery! He has no right to ask you to do such a thing."

"He didn't. I asked myself. Good-bye mother—I must really go. I have
been in one wild rush ever since I got up this morning. Tell Madge my
news. I am so happy that I feel treading on air!"

Then Tomina kissed her stepmother and sped away. The taxi was already
at the door.

A little later, she was greeting Colonel Norman on the platform at
Paddington station. He had engaged a special carriage for his mother.
Tomina looked up at him with a radiant face.

"Isn't this delicious? Yesterday I quite thought I was going to make my
journey alone, travelling away from you for ever. To-day—why, Gerald, I
do believe you have already one wrinkle less across your forehead!"

He smiled upon her—he could not help it.

"I am sadly wanting a long, sensible talk with you, but how are we to
get it? My mother's one desire is to have us both with her."

"We can take one turn along the platform," said Tomina, "before the
train starts. And I suppose we shall see each other again soon, shall
we not?"

"How is it to be managed? I cannot come to you."

"Then I must come to Torquay. I know a Mrs. Darrell there; I will ask
her to invite me for a short visit."

"I hope you will come to us. We shall be in rooms."

"Well, we will see."

"It is all wrong," Colonel Norman said hurriedly. "You must let me
speak out. I have no right to drag you into the shadows. I would
not have you lose your freshness and gaiety for worlds. It would be
impossible for you to keep it, if you were to live with us. I—"

Tomina interrupted him.

"I don't think I want to listen to this long, sensible talk of yours,"
she said, laughing, "if this is to be a sample of it. Just tell me you
are happier than you were yesterday. Don't let me feel I have added
to your cares! Why, you foolish man, nursing an invalid is a woman's
duty—not a man's! A woman makes nothing of it. Look at hospital nurses;
what a cheerful race they are! I am not a young girl. I have something
independent of circumstances to make me happy, and I am just longing
to show you what good times we will have—your mother, you, and I. Let
us thoroughly enjoy our journey to Bristol together. Do remember that
my butterfly days are over; I'm going to be a busy brown bee, which is
much more comfortable and snug than a fragile thing made of gossamer
wings."

Colonel Norman drew in a breath.

"I will save my protestations," he said. "We will enjoy ourselves
to-day, dearest."

And enjoy themselves they did. Mrs. Norman lay on her comfortably
improvised couch and smiled as she listened to Tomina's bright
talk. She was told about the van and all the quaint humours of the
passengers, and the amusing incidents that occurred upon the road.

Colonel Norman's eyes never left Tomina. Never in all the years that
he had known her did she appear more charming than now. There was a
softness and sweetness in her ripple of talk, a sudden touch of pathos
and tenderness in her tone that he never remembered in her before. And
her delicate beauty seemed to have ripened and strengthened into more
of a woman's nature and less of the girl's. She had always bewitched
him with her rapid variety of moods; he had known that she was not all
frivol and froth, but now he was allowed to see something of the deep,
true feeling within her.

And when she turned to him with wistful, gentle appeal in her laughing
eyes, he longed with a hungry longing to have her to himself alone.

Yet nothing made him oblivious of his mother's needs. He and Tomina
vied with one another in arranging her cushions for her comfort and
in beguiling the time away. Tomina gave her an inimitable description
of the morning when Gerald had met her running breathlessly after the
van, and how he had dealt with the mischievous old man who had driven
off without her; then of the day when he had so resolutely climbed up
and taken the seat beside her; and lastly, of the terrible thunderstorm
when she and Harry were fixed up in their tree, unable to move.

"You see, Mrs. Norman, nothing would keep him out of my life, do what I
would. And this morning—surely it was not this morning, it seems years
ago—this morning he really saved my life—"

As she spoke, Tomina looked across at Gerald with soft radiant eyes.

"Of course," Mrs. Norman said with interest, "you belong to each other.
I am not so selfish as to wish to keep my son entirely to myself. I
like to see people and to have them about me, and I am delighted to
have you, dear, as a daughter. What a lot we shall have to arrange!
There will be the wedding, and then—where shall we settle, Gerald? Is
it quite impossible to go back to the Court? With Tomina with us, I do
not think I should mind so much."

"I fear we could not do that, mother, it is sold, you know."

"I never could understand why you sold it so hurriedly. I am sure,
if you had had patience, things would have come right. I can't help
feeling it was your lack of business qualities and want of knowledge
that landed us in such money difficulties. If your dear father had
lived, how different it would have been! You see, you were trained to
be a soldier, and soldiers know nothing of business. It almost seems a
waste for you to have left the Service; you did no good. I dare say you
did your best, but you must have muddled things terribly to necessitate
the place being sold."

"You told me when we were abroad that nothing would induce you to go
back there, mother dear; you wanted it taken off our hands."

"But people may change their minds sometimes." Mrs. Norman's tone was
irritable. "Now I feel quite differently about it."

"Never mind! We won't talk over money matters now."

Tomina was conscious that Gerald was deeply hurt by his mother's words,
though his composure was wonderful. She did not know how much of this
kind of reproach he was continually called upon to endure. Knowing how
masterful and intolerant he had been in the old days, how intensely he
had resented any criticism or blame, she marvelled at his patience and
forbearance now with his poor mother. She dashed into the conversation.

"Let us picture our home," she said. "We have the whole world at our
disposal, Mrs. Norman. I do love seeing new places, don't you?"

"I am tired of moving about," was the rather peevish reply. "I want to
settle down. I want to have my own things about me."

"Gerald and I must go house-hunting, and come back and tell you all
about them. It will be delightful."

"I cannot be left alone."

Mrs. Norman's good temper was forsaking her. She was not a good
traveller, and her son instinctively felt that she was suffering. He
bent over her and shifted her position a little.

"Would you like me to read to you? Perhaps you could get a little
sleep. I have a paper here. I will read you the Court news; I know that
always interests you."

Tomina sat still, gazing out of the window. A sudden fear seized her
that life lived out by Mrs. Norman's bedside might prove too trying for
her patience. And if she failed in good humour and perfect serenity of
temper, she would add to Gerald's burden instead of lessening it. She
remembered her month with Mrs. Cuthbert, and how desperately she had to
struggle against impatience at that lady's selfish garrulous talk.

And then Gerald began to read. He had a very pleasant voice, and in a
few minutes a soothed, rested look came over his mother's fretful face.
In a short time, she slept peacefully, and then very gently Gerald came
across to Tomina. He sat down by her side, and then, putting his arm
round her, drew her to him.

"My darling, you see how it is with my poor mother. I warn you that on
some days nothing pleases her. I am grieved that you should have seen
her like this to-day, but you must make allowance for her. Travelling
always upsets her."

Tomina nestled against his broad shoulder.

"It only makes me think about you," she said. "I don't know this saint.
How did you become one? All at once?"

"She is my mother," he said softly, "and I love her. But trouble has
taught me lessons, and it has drawn me to the One Who chastens as well
as blesses."

"Ah!" said Tomina, with a happy sigh. "Then you and I will love and
serve together. I felt there was something different about you."

They sat silent, quite content that they were with each other. Then he
said—

"Tomina dearest, if you marry me, I shall not be able to give you the
comforts which are natural to you. My little mother cannot understand
it, but for a long time her income has only been half what it was when
my father was living. We shall have to be content with a small house
and simple living. This is one of the reasons that made me feel I had
no right to speak to you now."

"Why, Gerald, what nonsense! Have you seen the size of the cottage I
now live in, and in which I am so supremely happy? And I have my own
money, you know, which is more than ample for my wants. I never have
valued big houses and property as some people do. I would be perfectly
happy in a cottage. Don't you see that my greatest pleasure will be
to make you happy? I believe, I hope I can. Can't we find some sweet
little country house or farm where we could enjoy all the country
people, and things which I have learnt to love?"

Gerald hesitated.

"My mother," he said, "wants a great deal of amusement. She likes to
lie on her couch, watching a busy thoroughfare. It seems to distract
her thoughts."

"Then we must live in town," said Tomina, bravely and cheerfully. "What
does it matter where we live? You and I will be together, and we shall
never, never be anything but supremely happy."

"I'm afraid I shall never be able to have my sensible talk with you,"
said Gerald, as he looked down upon her with fond, smiling eyes. "You
have bewitched me, Tomina. I shall not be able to live without you."

They talked in soft whispers then, as only lovers can. Mrs. Norman
slept on and never woke till they came to Bristol, and Tomina had to
leave them.

The rest of her journey seemed like a dream. She arrived at her
cottage tired in body, but in very good spirits. Susan welcomed her
rapturously, and had plenty of village news to give her.

And then she went early to bed, for she had had a very strenuous and
exhausting day.


It was astonishing how easily she slipped into her simple life again.
The spring weather was delightful to her. When she took the van out,
she wore her motor bonnet with the cherries, and the admiration which
it evoked was tremendous.

"'Tis what Miss Kirk in Damerton allays says," announced Mrs. Tibbs,
"if you can't afford to foller the fashions up to time, put your
old-fashioned gown in a box with camphor and wait a few years, then
bring it out, and you'll be as stylish as you like, and no cost
to nobody! It stands to reason, fashion comes back agen and agen.
Now that there bunnet be just a pattern sure enough made after my
great-grandmother's, for I have her picture taken in it."

"Ah, dear Miss Tomina! You're lookin' bonny! We all do say as we shan't
have you single much longer?"

"I don't believe you will," said Tomina, looking at them seriously.
"You all say a woman ought to marry, so I think I shall take your
advice."

"Hark to her now! Have you seen a gentleman to your likin', miss?"

"Yes. Now guess. He did my van a good turn one day!"

"Why, 'tis the Colonel what used to live over to Yockley. He ketched
hold of Will Carpenter and shook him upside down!"

"Is it, miss? Surely you wouldn't favour such a grave gentleman?"

"Hush! Don't be so sharp, Mrs. Crabbe. A young lady doesn't always like
the free-and-easy sort."

"It is Colonel Norman. Now I'm waiting for your congratulations!"

There was a great buzz and interest in the van. Some groaned, but all,
as they expressed it, "wished her well."

"It do seem very soon to lose you. 'Tis what we all feared, that it
couldn't last long. Young ladies don't turn into carriers for life.
'Twas bound to come to end some time. Eh, dearie me, 'tis a knock-down
blow for us!"

"I'm not going to give up the carrier's business," said Tomina, looking
at them with her bright smile. "I shall be a carrier, or try to be,
wherever I am. But if I have to leave this part of the world, I shall
undertake to see that this van goes on. You have got on very well
without me all these weeks."

Old Mrs. Crabbe shook her head sadly.

"No, me dear, we hain't! There be too much wrangle, and we like a
carrier with plenty o' fun and sperrit. Now, Joe be very well for a
lad, but he have no sperrit!"

Joe happened at this moment to be walking up the hill by the side of
the cart, so he did not hear the discussion.

"But," said Tomina, "I think spirit is not necessary in a carrier.
Faithfulness, honesty, and a good memory are what is needed, and Joe
has all that."

"There be weddings in the air," said Mrs. Tubbs, gloomily. "Mrs.
Francis up at the Hall is marrying our doctor, and that be comin' off
next week."

"So soon!" exclaimed Tomina. "I did not know it."


The very next day Mrs. Francis came to see her and told her the news.

"I do so want to see you about Harry, for you have done so much for
him," she said. "Dr. Merton and I want to adopt him, and have him to
live with us altogether. Do you object?"

"Indeed I don't. I think it will be splendid for the child. Dear little
Harry! He has helped me so much. I feel I owe him a lot!"

"You will still be able to see him."

"Yes," said Tomina, slowly, "as long as I am here, I shall."

"Are you thinking of leaving us?"

Tomina looked up at Mrs. Francis.

"Only of following your example."

"Oh, my dear, I am so glad. You are not fitted to live alone. I always
said so."

For a moment, Tomina was silent; then she said—

"You don't ask me his name."

"Somebody that I know?"

"Yes, and somebody whom you like."

"Not Gerald Norman? Oh, delightful! I always wondered if he—if you
cared!"

"I knew him long before we came here," said Tomina. "We had quarrelled
very bitterly, or we might have married long ago. And he was ready to
forgive before I was. We did not fraternise much, did we, when he was
here? I don't think I should have come here at all, had I known that
Yockley Court was in the neighbourhood."

"I am so interested," said Mrs. Francis, earnestly, "and so very, very
glad for Gerald to be going to have brightness and happiness in his
life at last. How is his mother?"

"Just the same—a permanent invalid. Do you know, I find Gerald so
altered that my heart aches for him. I long to be able to give him some
of his freedom back again. He has been absolutely tied to his mother's
sick-room ever since she was first taken ill. Not one son in a thousand
would have done what he has done!"

"But, my dear, you cannot take his place. Are you going to nurse her
between you? I cannot think that right. You are young, and trained
nurses could spare you. Of course, I know that, as long as Mrs. Norman
lives, Gerald will have her in his home, but I think, for his sake
and yours, you ought to come to some different arrangement about the
nursing."

"Oh, we shall see," said Tomina, brightly. "I don't feel that
it will be a burden to me. I love Gerald so much that I long to
give—give—give—do you understand me? I long to bring the light to his
eyes again, to hear his tread get brisker, and his voice have the old
masterful ring in it. Both he and his mother want a third person badly
who will shake them out of their groove. He is too resigned, it makes
him dull! She thinks it quite impossible in her circumstances to laugh
or even smile."

"I wish you had a happier commencement to your married life."

"It will be heavenly!" said Tomina, with shining eyes. "Nothing you can
say will damp my hopes."

And Mrs. Francis wisely said no more.

Dr. Merton and Mrs. Francis were married very quietly the following
week; only Tomina and some near relations were at the wedding. They
went to the Lakes for their honeymoon.

CHAPTER XXI

CONGRATULATIONS

TOMINA did not stay very long at home, for Gerald Norman wrote to her
on behalf of his mother, begging her to come and pay them a visit.

She went, and brought her own bright atmosphere with her. Gerald and
she went out together every afternoon whilst Mrs. Norman rested. That
was the only time they had together, besides an odd hour or so in the
evening. Mrs. Norman demanded as much time and attention from Tomina as
from her son. But Tomina was able to send Gerald out when she stayed
in. And though very difficult to persuade at first, he eventually gave
way. She was too delighted to see his brisker and brighter ways to be
sorry for herself.

She would not allow anybody to pity her.

"It is very good for us that our time together should be so curtailed,"
she told him when he and she were walking along the sea front together,
"because it makes it doubly sweet and precious."

She had been listening to Gerald's "long and sensible" talk, and had
successfully contested each objection he brought forward against their
speedy marriage.

"I am going to share and share alike with you. Of course I do not wish
to force myself upon you, but I know that you are bent on sacrificing
yourself for the rest of your life, and that I am going to prevent."

And at last, Gerald put his misgivings and objections in the background
and began wholeheartedly to plan out their future.

"One thing I am determined," said he; "that if you marry me, we shall
go right away together for our honeymoon. For a fortnight at least, I
must leave my mother. But how to do it is a puzzle."

"We must get some nice friend to come and stay with her. Have you no
relation who would do it, Gerald?"

"No; we are singularly bereft in that way. I have some girl cousins,
but they are all in Canada."

And then Tomina brightened up.

"I believe I know a girl who might come. I happened to hear from her
only yesterday. She was companion to an old lady living near Damerton,
a Mrs. Cuthbert. I took her place whilst she went home for a holiday.
She has just left Mrs. Cuthbert; the old lady never keeps anybody very
long. She is a nice girl and a thorough lady. Shall I write and ask
her?"

"After we have broken it to my mother. And now let us settle the day.
Why should we wait, Tomina? We have known each other for so long; and
it is weary waiting."

"You need not wait any longer, Gerald. I am ready."

Tomina spoke very softly.

Gerald and she had stopped and were leaning against a bit of old wall
overlooking the blue sea. Nobody was about. He put his hand upon hers
caressingly.

"Sweetheart, I am taking your all and giving nothing. But, God helping
me, I will make you happy. My love is deep enough and strong enough to
do it, I believe, in spite of our sad circumstances."

"I haven't the shadow of a fear for our future," said Tomina, throwing
back her head and gazing with bright glowing eyes over the white surly
billows that were rolling in on the sandy beach. "If we had married
each other a few years ago, Gerald, it would have been different. I
think I should have been intolerant of any sickness or difficulties or
strain. Then I had nothing but gaiety to live for. But now, if waves do
roll over us like the sea is breaking over those rocks, we are part of
the Rock itself, we shan't be moved!"

Then she broke into a laugh.

"I expect I sound an awful prig sometimes, don't I? But I almost
glory in burden carrying now. It makes me feel like my dear Saint
Christoforos, only he was so strong that it would take a good deal to
make him feel. I expect—" here her eyes grew dreamy and wistful—"that
was the reason the Christ Child appeared—to make him feel his weakness,
nobody else in the world could—I want to test my powers of endurance,
Gerald!"

Then she looked up at him in dismay.

"What am I saying? I am making out that your dear mother is going to be
my heavy burden, and she won't be—she won't be! She and I are very gay
and happy together. I let my tongue run away with me."

"I love to hear it," said Gerald, smiling down upon her. "We will have
no pretence between us, Tomina dear. An invalid is bound to test our
patience. I have proved it, though it is and always will be to me a
service of love. And I know there will be many difficult days to get
through. But I will not let the burden press heavily on you; that I can
prevent."

They talked on, and, before they returned to the house, they had
settled their wedding-day. It was to be very quiet, and Tomina said she
would ask her stepmother to refrain from telling any one when it would
be.

"I think I should like to ask Doreen to it. I am getting fond of her,
and I should like you to know her, Gerald."

"But I do know her," said Gerald, rather sternly. "She has spoiled the
life of my best chum. Have you not met Maurice Holme?"

"Yes. Tell me his side of the story, will you? I have heard Doreen's."

"It's a case of a woman's impulse getting the better of her. Holme's
present wife is a shady sort, as you must know. She made a dead set at
him at Gosport, where our regiment was quartered. She and a married
sister made up a plot together, and they brought about a situation,
perfectly innocent in itself, but compromising in the eyes of
convention. The girl thought she would frighten him into marrying her,
but he simply applied for leave and cut her entirely. Then he became
engaged to your sister-in-law, and this girl turned up the day before
the wedding, and wrote a private note to Miss Forrester. She had been
quietly biding her time. What she wrote, I'm sure I don't know, but
she was believed, and Holme was allowed no chance of explanation. The
marriage was broken off."

"But he married his present wife," cried Tomina; "that showed he must
have been guilty."

"Not by any means. He married her because he was reckless and
desperate. He acted on impulse, as Miss Forrester did; I dare say his
present wife threatened him with breach of promise. He had been ass
enough to write her letters. If ever a man's life has been ruined,
his has, and it need not have been. A woman can be desperately cruel
sometimes. There could have been no mercy in her composition."

Tomina looked very grave.

"She is a desperately unhappy woman herself. I pray she may not bring
unhappiness to Tom. If only they had never met!"

Gerald shrugged his shoulders.

"I have no pity for her. Poor Holme is another matter. He has
established his wife at the Court, as you know, but he is seldom there.
She won't follow him in the Service. She loves her comfort too well,
and I believe is filling the house with a lot of her London friends."

"Then you don't want Doreen at our wedding?"

"My darling, have her by all means, if you like."

"You must remember she was told the very worst, and she felt as if he
had been deceiving her all through!"

"A woman has no right to lose all faith in a man at the first
trumped-up story she hears! Would you have done so?"

"If I had heard something unpleasant about you? No, I should not have
believed it. But then we have known each other for years. I am sure of
you. I know your character. You see, Doreen had no time to investigate.
She was going to marry at once. She could not put it off. It either
had to be one thing or the other; and she felt it would be too great a
risk."

Gerald said no more.

But Tomina received the following letter from Doreen in answer to her
news and invitation:—

 "MY DEAREST LITTLE SISTER,

 "I do offer you my heartfelt congratulations. I always felt that you
would not remain single for long. I know Colonel Norman slightly,
and should say he was worthy of a woman's trust. Dear Tomina, don't
be vexed if I do not appear at your wedding. I do hate all marriage
ceremonies. They are so irrevocable, links forged for all time, and
forged so often in such a light-hearted, irresponsible fashion! I have
nothing to say against yours. I do not think you would marry unless
your heart and soul were in it, and I am sure, from what I know of him,
that he would not! You will bring any one happiness, dear, for you
carry about with you such a fund of sunshine. We less fortunate mortals
