have to depend much on summer days to keep us content. The summer days
are mine at present. Gardening engrosses me from early dawn to late at
night; but, oh, the exquisiteness of bud and flower, and all the sweet
scents that nature gives us so abundantly!

 "Your affectionate sister,

 "DOREEN.

 "I heard from Tom yesterday. He is very well."


And so a week or two later, Tomina was saying good-bye to all her
friends at Honeycombe. She kept up her van to the very last day.
She had formally given it over to Joe, who was enchanted to be sole
proprietor of it. He gave general satisfaction to the passengers,
though the lamentations were loud at Tomina's coming departure. Their
great distress when she told them her wedding was to be in town, made
Tomina alter all her plans at the last moment.

She wired to her stepmother and to Gerald that she would like to be
married in Little Honeycombe church. Could they manage it for her?

Lady Gavin groaned and wrote a letter of five pages, explaining why it
must be in town. Then, in a P.S., she added,—

 "But, of course, you always get your way, so I will come."

And Gerald wired—

 "As in either place I must have special licence, certainly have it at
Honeycombe."


And now on a lovely June morning Tomina, starting with a heavy van-load
for the last time, was able to tell her passengers the good news. There
was great jubilation when they heard it.

"We hardly never has weddin's nowadays! 'Twill cheer us up wonderful."

"Yes, 'twould be heart-breakin' to see you go off just a single maid by
yoursel' to marry such a way off from us all."

"'Tisn't as if there be folks in town who would give you so hearty a
set-off as we should, as have known you all this time."

"No, if good wishes bring luck, you'll have more luck from Little
Honeycombe than from Lunnon, that be quite positive certain."

"Oh, my dears," said Tomina, laughing merrily, though there were tears
in her eyes, "how good you all are to your carrier! I meant to have
crept quietly into some dim empty church in a quiet old square in
London, where nobody would have seen me, but now, I suppose, I shall
have all your eyes upon me, and one will say that my veil was crooked,
and one that the tip of my nose was red, and one that my voice was
shaky, and you'll all be disappointed with my looks and behaviour! But
you won't be disappointed in the looks of my bridegroom. I dare you to
criticise so much as the heel of his boot!"

There was a regular cackle of laughter at this.

"Hark to her! Isn't that the way with us women? Our men be more than
perfec' when they courts us! My dear, you'll live to sing a different
song after a year or two. Men do disappoint, like a blue cotton gown,
first time in wash!"

"Yes, sure enough; they don't come through wash-tubs satisfactory. Much
rubbin' always does for 'em!"

"Best not rub 'em."

This last remark was from a Mrs. Trent, a very silent woman.

Tomina hastened to turn the subject to other safer topics than that of
husbands. As old Mrs. Crabbe said: "Some of us married women don't know
how to vally our privileges!"


When the return from market came, there was a good deal of mysterious
bustle on the part of Mrs. Judd.

Tomina heard a whispered inquiry—

"Have 'ee got it, Mrs. Judd? Plenty o' it? We will all be wantin' to
buy a bit. Is that bit of a bag all 'ee can get?"

"Hush, now, there be four bags. Mrs. Tubbs, I'll trouble you not to put
your feet on that one. There now!" There was a stifled exclamation:
"Oh! You orkward woman, you!"

Tomina looked back into the corner.

"What is happening?"

"'Tis one of Mrs. Judd's parcels. Nothin' partikler; it be burst!"

"A bag of rice, isn't it? What a pity!"

The van had not started, so Tomina helped to gather up some of the
scattered grain. She could not understand the whispered nods and
mysterious smiles amongst the women.

"We shall go short in rice puddings if we don't save it," said Tomina,
gaily.

And then she caught the murmur and understood—

"Bless her 'art, 'tis she what will be goin' short o' luck!"

But Tomina knew when it was tactful to be silent, and she was now.

She was quite sorry when that last market day came to a close. One of
the passengers suggested that she should drive to church in the van,
but Tomina said that, as it was only across the road, it would be
hardly worth while.

There were great discussions as to who would give her away, and for the
next few days the topic of Tomina's coming wedding was more than meat
and drink to the villagers.

She herself was very busy. She went up to town for the inside of a week
to do her necessary shopping. But a big trousseau was out of place for
her, she said. She would only need the simplest clothes, and not many
of them, as she preferred getting them as she wanted them. Lady Gavin
was scandalised at the whole affair, but was as wax in Tomina's hands.

And at last, the eve of the wedding came. Gerald was put up at the
Hall; his best man, a Mr. Russell, was put up at the Rectory, and
Tomina and her stepmother shared the cottage.

In the afternoon of that day, Tracy St. Vincent rode over. He brought
an exquisite old silver wrought jug, as a wedding present, with
him. "It's one of the antique things that were in the house when I
took it over," he explained. "I've been awfully bowled over by this
engagement of yours, but I won't be such a cad as to refuse you my
congratulations. And I suppose Norman is more your sort than I am. I
don't think I shall ever marry. Shall do the heavy squire to the end
of my life. With you and Mrs. Francis both married women, there'll be
nobody that I care to talk to. Are you going to settle down here?"

"I am afraid not," Tomina said, after thanking him warmly for his
present. "We have decided just to move about as Mrs. Norman likes. She
is a great invalid, and rather restless. For the present we shall live
at Torquay, and then later we think of going up to Edinburgh. Mrs.
Norman has some distant relatives there. Perhaps we may go abroad in
the autumn."

"Then you'll be lost to us. It's just like my luck."

He would not be comforted. But Mrs. Merton told Tomina that he was
gradually taking his place in the country, and his very honesty and
sincerity of purpose would win favour amongst his neighbours.

Harry came to tea that same afternoon. He was enchanted at being chosen
as sole attendant to the bride. He was not too big to fling himself
into Tomina's lap when Lady Gavin had gone out of the room.

"Tommie, I do love you!" he exclaimed. "You won't go away for ever,
will you?"

"I hope not, Harry. But you have a happy home now, haven't you?"

"Yes."

His face assumed a dubious expression.

"I can't never say to the Doctor or Mrs. Merton, 'Let's have some fun!'
I could to you, couldn't I?"

"Yes, indeed you could."

Then Tomina bent her head and kissed his rosy face.

"Oh, my Harry, will you always remember through life what you have
taught me!"

"To play cricket?" questioned Harry, eagerly.

"No, to love God, and to believe in His close presence."

Harry was instantly sobered. He twisted his restless fingers in and out
of a long chain hanging from Tomina's neck.

"Do you know, Tommie, I've been worrying lately—just to myself, you
know, when I'm in bed, because I have always loved God, and loved Jesus
Christ, but I can't truthfully say I love the Holy Ghost. Do you think
it's awfully wicked of me? We've been hearing about the Trinity at
school; our master gives us a talk at prayer-time. I don't seem to know
Him, and when I was small, I used to think a ghost a frightful thing.
It's another name for spirit, our master says. I asked him."

"Dear Harry, don't worry yourself. Ask God in your prayers to show you
how to love Him, and if you love God, you must love Him. They are One."

"Yes, I'm so glad. You don't think it's wicked of me. And, Tommie,
you're quite sure you like Colonel Norman well enough to be your
husband?"

"Quite sure," Tomina responded.

"I only remember when he got us down from the tree you weren't very
pleased with him!"

Tomina laughed.

"I was in fault then, Harry. Colonel Norman is much too good for me!"

"Nobody couldn't be that—not the King himself," asserted Harry, with
all the eagerness and loyalty of his boy's heart. "I shall feel like
a king myself when I carry your nosegay up the church, or is it your
dress, or your gloves, or your Prayer-book, or is it all of them?"

Tomina informed him on this point.

When he had left her, she wandered out herself into the back garden.
She felt she must be alone.

It was a sweet still evening; her spring flowers and sweet-briar hedge
comforted her with their fresh scent. A cloud, slight at first, but
rather depressing, seemed to hover over her. And then suddenly a step
which she had learned to love sounded behind her, and Gerald took her
hands in his, and, stooping, kissed her.

"I felt I must have a moment with you alone to-night. Am I selfish?"

"No. You come when I want you, Gerald. Suppose I am a failure? Suppose
I have overrated my good qualities and underrated my bad ones? I am
wondering if I may not prove an additional burden to you. I am not
perfect. I never shall be. I may get cross and irritable and impatient.
Then your mother will suffer and so will you. I am such a groper—such a
beginner in some things!"

"My darling, I want you to be your impulsive wayward enchanting little
self, not a cold stone statue of virtue and of perfection. I quite
expect you to be often cross, and I shall be even more so, but love
isn't bowled over by human infirmities."

"No," said Tomina, and she put her arms softly round his neck, "love
understands; and we all of us sometimes drop the things we are
carrying. I shall not be a perfect carrier, I know."

Then they sat together on the rustic seat.

The peace of that evening remained for ever stamped upon their souls.
When they parted, Tomina's cloud had disappeared. She awaited her
wedding without a shadow of misgiving for the future.

CHAPTER XXII

WEDDED

"WHY, Mrs. Dodd, why ever didn't 'ee turn up on Wednesday to our young
leddy's weddin'?"

"Ah, me dear soul! I were comin' right enough, and fit to cry I was
when my 'usband's aunt from Damerton, 'er what has a bit of money, took
me by surprise, and come over for the day. And nothin' would do but I
must cook 'er a dinner of pork chops which she brought in her bag! I be
fair longin' to hear all of it!"

"Well!" said Mrs. Judd, settling herself back in the van, and preparing
to be chief spokeswoman. "We've never 'ad such a thorough downright,
enjoyable ceremony. To begin with, the peal o' bells began just after
ten o'clock. My nevvy Frank was chief ringer, and he made the chaps
put their 'arts in it, I can tell you! They was clashin' and boomin'
on an 'off for over an hour. The church were as well filled as 'tis
at 'Arvest Thanksgivin'. An' the children were let out o' school ten
minutes to eleven. I didn't go inside church too soon, for I wanted to
ketch sight of the bride a-walkin' from the 'ouse. But the bridegroom,
he arrived at church soon after the Rector."

"What did he look like?" demanded Mrs. Dodd, with breathless interest.
"I always have feelin' for the man; he do cut such a poor show by the
bride."

"Oh, as grave as a judge, and his lips clapped close and his eye like a
hawk's, dartin' roun' the corner at everybody!"

"Ah, well," put in another woman, "he was a fine set-up gentleman, an'
when he saw 'er comin', he fair ketched his breath like us all did!"

"But I seed her comin' out of her own door," went on Mrs. Judd, in
lofty voice. "She were in the most bee-oo-tiful gown of white satin and
a veil were hangin' over her, but it 'coulnn't' hide her pretty face,
bless her 'art! And the grand leddy from London were walkin' beside
her."

"You don't say so! But surely she had a gentleman to walk with her!"

"Not one, this 'ere leddy—a leddyship I heard tell—is her stepmother
and she giv' her away. She were dressed in plum satin with a feather
boa, and she 'ad a pair o' spectacles fixed on a 'andle which she 'eld
up to her eyes, and looked—well, she looked like a duchess! Miss Tomina
she stepped along very softly and quietly, and there was that boy she
be so taken with—Harry—he comes after her close at 'er 'eels! Well,
just as they comes to church door, she lifts her eyes, catches sight of
me as were prepared to follow 'em in as fast as I could. And she just
give me the sweetest nod, seemin' to say: 'There you are, glad to see
you!'"

"But do tell who were in the church of the gentry."

"'Twas all kept very quiet; the Rector he married 'em, an' Mrs.
Herbert, an' Dr. and Mrs. Merton, an' Sir Alfred, and Miss Merryweather
turned up, she 'as come to see Mrs. Merryweather through her first
baby. It come yesterday; did you hear of it?"

"Yes, that I did. The milkman from farm told me so—"

"Well, she were there, and then there were a strange gent, a friend of
the Colonel's—he looked as if he were lookin' on at a show. His lips
were curled up nasty, and he gave a heave of his shoulders now and agen
in an impatient sort o' way. But there, when I was tucked in tight
along with some others, I 'ad no thought but for our young lady."

"An'," interrupted Mrs. Tibbs, "there were one that thought a mighty
lot of 'erself, and that were Susan! She 'ad her best black silk and
'ad a red rose in her buzzom, and her hat! Well, it must have bin one
of Miss Tomina's cast-offs! We all said that, for we 'ad a partikler
look at it. It were black, but it 'ad some wonderful cream roses, made
almost to smell, they was so perfec'! And a lace veil twined in and out
like a regular creeper! She 'eld her head as high as the leddyship, an'
I were just in the pew behind, so I whispers over:

"'You do be lookin' up!'

"An' she never so much as turns her 'ead, the hussy!"

"Tell on!" said Mrs. Dodd, who was drinking in every detail with great
avidity.

"When they stood together, he were a whole head taller than 'er. Her
voice were clear as a bell when she made responses. I were near cryin'
the whole time. But when they went in the vestry an' the Weddin' March
begun, my 'art began to palpitate. It fair burst the 'ooks and eyes
of my bodice! And then they come down the aisle, and Miss Tomina were
noddin' and smilin' to everybody all round. An' he 'ad lost his stern
face and were quite affable like. Then the chillen awaitin' for them
outside dropped roses in their path, and then every one of the gentry
went back to the Cottage. The Rectory maids 'ad helped, and we heard
tell there was quite a choice meal laid out.

"And 'twasn't long before the fly drove up. Peter Hamblin drove, and
then was our time. We closed in and gave 'em a reg'lar good peltin' o'
rice, and then Miss Tomina afore she gets into the carriage, she looks
at us and laughs. She's always different to other ladies—she calls out
in her pretty way, wavin' her hand to us:

"'Good-bye,' she says, 'but I promise when I get tired of my husband,
I'll run back to Little Honeycombe and drive the van again!'

"And didn't us laugh? And give such cheerin'. Well, it must have been
'eard a mile off!"

"Oh, I do wish I had been there!"

"An' then we all had a big tea in the schoolroom. 'Twas her wish; but,
eh, dearie me, we shall never have a carrier agen like Miss Tomina!"

The sighs that echoed this speech were great. Tomina had gone out of
their life for the time. But she had left behind her many legacies of
goodwill and friendship amongst the simple village folk.


She and her bridegroom went to a quiet little Cornish fishing-village.
They would not be out of reach of Mrs. Norman. Christabel Avenel was
with her, and she proved quite a success. And for a fortnight, Tomina
and Gerald were rapturously happy. They determined to live only in
the present, and enjoy life as they had never enjoyed it before. Then
at the end of their time, they returned to Torquay, and gladdened the
heart of the poor invalid.

Tomina did indeed prove herself to be the sunshine in the house. She
was always gay and sweet-tempered; Mrs. Norman's fretful complaints
only brought out the deeper tenderness in her nature. Gerald would look
at her and marvel.

Their stolen time together was very sweet. And though sometimes they
were met with reproachful tears upon their return to the sick-room,
Mrs. Norman did make an effort now and then to be unselfish.

They went to Edinburgh when Torquay became too warm. And when the end
of summer came, they began to discuss a winter abroad.

But Mrs. Norman managed to contract Influenza, and was so weakened
and pulled down by it, that her doctor advised them not to leave
England. They finally went down to Penzance, and took rooms in the most
comfortable hotel there.


As time went on, Tomina began to find that what she first treated so
lightly was gradually becoming an effort and a struggle. There were
days when the fractiousness and unreasonableness of the sick woman told
upon her nerves, and she felt she longed to get away from it all. One
morning, when by a violent fit of weeping Mrs. Norman had stopped a
little expedition planned by husband and wife, Tomina sat in her room
work in hand, but with a frown upon her brow. She had been silent for
some time, and Mrs. Norman hated silence.

"Where is Gerald?" she asked presently.

"Gone down to the Club, I think, to have a look at the papers."

Tomina's tone was curt. She was still harbouring resentment towards her
mother-in-law for having stopped their expedition.

Mrs. Norman heaved a heavy sigh.

"I hardly ever see him! He used to sit with me in the morning and
read the papers—I couldn't have believed that he would have become so
neglectful."

"But you have me, and he will come in to lunch."

"A daughter-in-law is never the same as a son, and you are irritable
to-day, or sulky, I don't know which. I might have known how things
would turn out. Since you have married Gerald, you try to keep him away
from me. I'm a lonely, unhappy woman. You are both longing for me to
die. I wish I could. My life is one long misery; you have stolen my son
from me. He no longer cares to be with his poor mother, and you mount
guard in my room, and try to keep him away from it."

Mrs. Norman had often spoken in this strain before, but Tomina had
had sufficient self-control and patience to answer her gently. Now it
seemed the last straw.

"You are most unjust and selfish," she said, rising from her chair; "we
are both spoiling our lives for your sake, and this is how you treat
us. If you think I am keeping Gerald out of your room, I had better go.
I will send your maid to you—"

She walked out of the room, with head erect. But when she reached the
sitting-room, she sat down at the table in the window and, resting her
head in her hands, burst into tears.

Gerald came in, and found her sobbing her heart out. She tried to tell
him what had happened.

"I have lost my temper with your mother, and been very rude to her.
I can't go on any longer, Gerald. It's beyond my strength. Nothing
pleases her now. The more we give up to her, the more she demands. She
is wearing us both out, and she is getting to dislike me. She thinks I
turn you against her."

Gerald sat down, with a worried look upon his face.

"Tomina, sweetheart, be brave a little longer. I will not harbour the
thought that we were mistaken in marrying. We have had, and will still
have, happy times together. If your nerves and health are giving way,
would you like to pay a few visits? Then you would come back fresher,
and more able to bear with my poor mother."

"Without you? Oh no. I couldn't. Don't ask me! There! I have done—I
think tears do one good sometimes. I am not going to give up my 'rôle'
as carrier so easily, but I have had a tumble and dropped my load. Now
I am up again. Don't look so grave, Gerald. I wish I was as strong and
serene as you are. Nothing ever moves you!"

"My wife's distress does, especially when it is I and mine who bring it
upon her."

His voice broke for a moment. He drew her to him.

"Sweetheart, if you can bear it a little longer, I think one day you
will look back and be glad. I had a long talk with our doctor this
morning. I met him out. He says my mother is weaker in every way, and
that he does not think she will last through this next winter."

"Oh, Gerald!"

Quick tears sprang into Tomina's eyes.

"I am so ashamed of myself. I told you I would fail you, didn't I? We
must, we 'must' let her have all the sunshine we can in the Valley! I
won't break down again. Will you go to her? I will come in later. I
hope she will have forgotten my rudeness."

She put up her face to his and kissed him, then pushed him from her.

"Go quickly, and I will run out and buy a few flowers as a peace
offering."

The little storm blew over for the time. Tomina was daily learning
that, though weak in herself, there was a Power that could keep her,
and with the sad knowledge in her heart that Mrs. Norman's days were
numbered, she kept back hasty words, and only showed a bright smiling
face.

The time came when Mrs. Norman grew more placid. As weakness increased,
gentleness came with it, and she now listened with interest when Tomina
took up her Bible and read to her from it.

When Christmas Day dawned, it found husband and wife sorrowing over
their loss. Though for her they could rejoice, they missed intensely
the object of their loving ministrations.

Mrs. Norman had not found her Valley shadowed by fears. Like a little
child, she had been led to trust in the One Who could carry her through
it in perfect peace. Her mind was, fortunately for herself, not strong
enough to dwell much in the past. But her last conscious words were to
Tomina—

"You have been so good, so patient with me! I wish I had been more
patient myself."

And Tomina felt thankful that she had been allowed to ease those last
suffering months.


When the spring came, husband and wife began to plan out their future,
and then a very warm invitation came from Dr. and Mrs. Merton to go and
stay with them.

"Let us go, Gerald. I long to see Little Honeycombe again; will you be
sad being so near your old home?"

"I don't think I shall ever be sad, as long as I have my wife with me."

So they went.

The following two letters speak for themselves:—

 "MY DEAREST TOMINA,

 "This is to let you know that I have a baby boy, the image of his
father! When I married, I hardly thought of motherhood. This tiny atom
has brought quite a fresh range of ideas and thought to me. I thought
my life would be spent in nursing and training and watching over plants
and flowers. Now they only form a background to my son. I am now
nursing and training and watching over a human soul. And I am trembling
at the responsibility. I want you to talk to me. I want—I think I want
your bright unwavering faith in God. Will you be his godmother and will
you come to his christening? Do, if you have a little bit of love for
me.

 "Your loving sister,

 "DOREEN.

 "P.S.—Do tell me your news, and where you are going to settle."


 "MY DEAREST DOREEN,

 "Your news enchants me! Of course I will come, if only for a few days.
I cannot leave Gerald for long. You don't know how he has felt the
strain of the last year or so. He still looks worn and thin, but his
energy is coming back, and our plans are made. We are going to take
over an untenanted farm on the Yockley Court estate. Major Holme has
gone out on a shooting expedition to the Himalayas, and he asked Gerald
if he could look after his property for him. He has only a very young
and inexperienced agent. I think with some alterations, we shall make
this farmhouse into a very pretty and cosy home, and I shall get the
desire of my heart—to live in the country! I know you will sympathise
with me.

 "How selfish I am writing about our affairs and leaving your sweet baby
out in the cold! My dear Doreen, I shall love to be his godmother!
Thank you a thousand times for asking me. How pleased and proud Tom
will be! I can't imagine him a father. How well I can understand the
difference he will make in your life, and I am glad and thankful
you will not be limited to your garden children, but will have the
enjoyment of seeing his dear little life unfold and develop. I feel I
know something of small boys now after my experience with Harry. They
are so delicious in their eagerness and freshness and strong faith!
What talks we will have when I come! Meanwhile talk to God about him,
dear. He sent him to you, and He loves him and wants him to grow up in
His family.

 "Doreen, I do feel so thankful I came down to Little Honeycombe. It
will always be the dearest place in the world to me. The place of my
resurrection, when all things became new!

 "I am enjoying every moment of every day. Yesterday I went over to
see Susan, and from her I wandered round the village. They are all
clamouring to have their carrier back, but, do you know, this is just
one thing that Gerald will not allow me to do? And by his rather
peremptory request that I never drive that van again, I think he must
be regaining his health and spirits. We had quite a battle of words
this morning about it. But, of course, I gave way and with as good a
grace as I could. I feel that the old desire to have my will uppermost
has left me. And Gerald is such an adoring husband that when he puts
his foot down, I know it is for my sake he does it. I tell him that he
fears the van would be a rival, and thinks that if I once began driving
it again, it would take possession of me. And so you see, Doreen, here
ends 'Tomina the Carrier'!

 "Much love, dear. How I long to see you with a baby in your arms!

 "Your loving sister,

 "TOMINA."


It was a lovely summer evening.

Tomina was in a blue sunbonnet watering her roses.

The pretty thatched farmhouse behind would have delighted an artist's
eye. Inside and out there was nothing to spoil the quaint old-fashioned
air about it. The broad oak door stood wide open showing a low wide
hall, with the stairs winding up from the centre of it. Two dogs, one
a red setter, the other a lively terrier, were lying in the porch.
Tomina's faithful Thor was standing by her side, looking on with grave
interest as his mistress handled her watering-can with much energy.

Then the garden gate slammed, a quick man's tread made all the dogs
spring up with welcoming barks, and Gerald greeted his wife—

"Well, here I am, and my work done for the day."

"You look warm and tired, Gerald dear. Come and sit down. Shall I fetch
you a drink?"

"No," said Gerald, smiling as he sank into a garden lounge chair under
a shady acacia tree on the lawn. "You are sufficient refreshment for
me."

Tomina rested her hand lightly on his shoulder, then pressed one of her
swift soft kisses on the top of his head.

"Aren't we happy?" she said, her eyes glowing with radiance. "Do you
think we always shall be? I wonder if you will get bored with your
wife, and only come home to her when you are obliged?"

"Do I deserve such a speech? Isn't it more likely that my bright little
wife will get weary of her prosy old husband?"

"Now, Gerald, you are fishing for compliments. You know you are neither
prosy nor old. Do you know that I met Mrs. Judd when I was out this
morning?

"'Well,' she said, '"they say, ma'am," that marriage makes a woman old
afore her time, but you and your Colonel are both years more juvenile
than you was afore matrimony!'"

"I think I feel so!" said Gerald.

"Of course," went on Tomina, perching herself on the arm of his chair,
"we continued to discuss husbands, for Mrs. Judd has had two, you know,
and now is a widow. She says,—

"''Tis a wonderful freedom to be single again, but 'tisn't exactly
comfortable.'"

Gerald's arm went round his wife's slim waist.

"Poor little wife!" he said. "Do you miss your freedom? Am I an ogre?"

"You can be. My carrier days are over, and I did love them so."

"But you told me you meant to be a much more efficient and noble one
than a mere van driver, and a carrier of burdens will last till the
world ends."

"Do you think so? When I was dressing this morning, I was thinking that
I have no scope for such carrying at present. There are no burdens to
carry in our life now, and I am getting lazy and self-indulgent! And
then I came across such a lovely verse in my morning reading,—

 "'How beautiful upon the mountains are the feet of him that bringeth
good tidings, that publisheth peace!'

"If I can't always carry burdens for people, I can carry them good
tidings, cannot I? And there are a good many even in this quiet part of
the country who would be glad to get peace. A carrier of good tidings!
Doesn't it sound delightful? So cheery, and comforting and sunshiny!"

Gerald Norman looked at his wife with thoughtful tender eyes.

She was still the same sparkling girl, but the undercurrent of
earnestness in her nature would occasionally come to the surface.

"Thank God," he murmured softly, "for Tomina the Carrier!"

 THE END
